	\begin{enumerate}
		\item $f_{ii}= \id_{A_i}$ for all $i\in I$.
		\item $f_{kj}\circ f_{ik}=f_{ij}$ for all $i\leq k\leq j$.
	\end{enumerate}
	The pair $\big((A_i)_{i\in I},(f_{ij})_{i\leq j}\big)$ is called a \textbf{direct system}. 
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}[Direct Limit]
	Let $\big((A_i)_{i\in I},(f_{ij})_{i\leq j}\big)$ be a direct system in a category $\mathcal{C}$.  A pair $\big(X,(\phi_i)_{i\in I}\big)$ where $X$ is an object of $\mathcal{C}$ and $\phi_i:A_i \to X$ are morphisms such that $\phi_j\circ f_{ij}=\phi_i$ for all $i\leq j$ is called a direct limit, if the following universal property holds: 
	For any family of morphisms $g_i:A_i\to Y$ that satisfies $g_i\circ f_{ji}=g_j$ for all $i\leq j$ there exists a unique morphism $g:X\to Y$ such that $g_i=g\circ \phi_i$. We denote the space $X$ by $\colim_{i}A_i$ (or just $\colim A_i$), where the maps $f_{ij}$ are omitted from the notation.
\end{definition}
\begin{corollary}
	As always, the direct limit is (if it exists) unique up to unique isomorphism.
\end{corollary}
\begin{remark}[Construction of Direct Limits]
	We will now see three constructions of direct limits. Let $\big((A_i)_{i\in I},(f_{ij})_{i\leq j}\big)$ denote a directed system in the noted category. 

\textit{ In the category of sets} we define the direct limit by
\begin{equation*}
	\colim_i A_i\coloneq \bigsqcup A_i \Big/ \sim  \,,
\end{equation*} where $x\sim y$ if there exists $i,j,k\in I$ such that $f_{ik}(x)=f_{jk}(y)$. This gives an equivalence relation. The maps $\phi_i: A_i\to \colim_{i}A_i$ are the composition of the inclusion into the disjoint union with the projection onto the quotient space. The universal property is easy to verify. For a family of suitable maps $g_i:A_i\to B$ we define the map $g:\colim_{i}A_i\to B$ as $g(x)=g_m(x)$ for $x\in A_m$. This is well defined since the $g_i$ are compatible with the $f_{ij}$ and unique by definition.

\textit{ In the category of topological spaces} we define the direct limit to be the set-theoretic direct limit equipped with the final topology of the inclusions, i.e. a subset $U\subseteq \colim_i A_i$ is open if and only if $\phi_i^{-1}(U)$ is open for all $m$. Again the universal property is easy to prove by using the construction of the universal map from the set-theoretic limit. To show the continuity we show that $g^{-1}(U)$ is open for any open $U$ which is checked by verifying openness of $\phi_i^{-1}\big(g^{-1}(U)\big)$ for all $i$, which in turn equals $g_i^{-1}(U)$ and is open as the $g_i$ are continuous. Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of $g$ established already in set. 

\textit{ In an algebraic category like semigroups, groups, rings } we define the direct limit again as the set-theoretic direct limit, where the algebraic operations are defined such that the $\phi_i$ are homomorphisms meaning for example (if $+$ is an operation in the $A_i$) $a\in A_i$ and $b\in A_j$ we define $a+b$ in $\colim{i}A_i$ by including the sum $f_{ik}(a)+f_{jk}(b)$ which is defined in $A_k$. Again the universal morphism $g$ is the one from the set-theoretic limit which now is a homomorphism by construction.	
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:kTheo-staircaseMapsClosedEmbedding}
If we build the direct limit of sets and all the maps of the direct system are injective, then the inclusions into the direct limit are injective. If all the maps of the direct system are closed embeddings, then the inclusions into the direct limit are also closed embeddings.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	Recall that the inclusions $\phi_i:A_i\to \colim A_i$ are defined to be the composition of the inclusion into the disjoint union with the projection onto the quotient. Now assume that all maps $f_{ij}$ in the direct system are injective and $\phi_i(x)=\phi_i(y)$. Then $x\sim y$ in the quotient and hence, there exists $i,j,k\in I$ such that $f_{ik}(x)=f_{jk}(y)$ which implies that $x=y$.

	Now assume that all $f_{ij}$ are closed embeddings. Then, the inclusions are injective by the set-theoretic argument. Furthermore, the images of $\phi_i:A_i\to \colim A_i$ are closed. That follows from subsets in the final topology being closed, if and only if all their preimages are closed. Hence we check if $\phi_j^{-1}(\phi(A_i))$ is closed for all $j\in I$:
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:kTheo-staircaseMapsClosedEmbedding}
		\begin{array}{llll}
			\text{ If }j\leq i& \text{then:} & \phi_j^{-1}(\phi_i(A_i))=(\phi_i\circ f_{ji})^{-1}(\phi_i(A_i))=f_{ji}^{-1}(A_i) &\text{which is closed.}\\
			\text{ If }i<j	 & \text{then:}	&\phi_j^{-1}(\phi_i(A_i)) =\underbrace{\phi_j^{-1}(\phi_j}_{\phi \text{ is injective}} f_{ij}(A_i))=f_{ij}(A_i) &\text{which is closed.}
		\end{array}
	\end{equation} To show that $\phi_i^{-1}: \im(\phi_i)\to A_i$ is continuous we assume that $B\subset A_i$ is open. Then $\phi_i(B)$ is open if and only if for any $j$ $\phi_j^{-1}(\phi_i(B))$ is open. This follows from the same calculations as in equation (\ref{eq:kTheo-staircaseMapsClosedEmbedding}) using that the $f_{ij}$ are homeomorphisms onto their image. 
\end{proof}
\begin{comment}

 \begin{definition}[Direct Limit of Sets]\label{def:kTheo-directLimitOfSets}	Let $X_1\to X_2\to \cdots$ be a sequence of sets and injective maps. Then the \textbf{direct limit} $\varinjlim_{m\to \infty} X_m$ is the union $\bigcup_{m=1}^\infty X_m$ modulo the equivalence relation identifying $x$ with $y$ if there exists an $m$ such that $i_m(x)=i_m(y)$. We call $\varinjlim_{m\to \infty} X_m$ the \textbf{direct limit} of the sequence $X_1\to X_2\to \cdots$.
 \end{definition}
 \begin{definition}[Direct Limit of Topological Spaces]\label{def:kTheo-directLimitOfTopologicalSpaces}
	Let $X_1\to X_2\to \cdots$ be a sequence of topological spaces and continuous injective maps. Then the \textbf{direct limit} $\lim_{m\to \infty} X_m$ is the set theoretic direct limit  with the topology given by the final topology with respect to the maps $X_m\to \lim_{m\to \infty} X_m$. I.e. a subset $U\subseteq \lim_{m\to \infty} X_m$ is open if and only if $U\cap X_m$ is open for all $m$.
 \end{definition}
 \begin{lemma}[Universal Property of the Direct Limit]\label{lem:kTheo-universalPropertyOfDirectLimit}
	Let 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			X_1 \arrow[r,"i_1"] & X_2 \arrow[r,"i_2"] & \cdots
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} be a sequence of sets (or topological spaces). Then any family of (continuous) maps $f_m:X_m\to Y$ that are compatible with the $i_m$ (i.e. $f_{m+1}\circ i_m=f_m$) factors uniquely through a (continuous) map $f:\lim_{m\to \infty} X_m \to Y$.	
 \end{lemma}
 \begin{proof}
	We start by showing the existence of $f$ by defining $f(x)=f_m(x)$ for $x\in X_m$. This is well defined as the $f_m$ are compatible with the $i_m$. Now we need to show that $f$ is continuous. For this we need to show that $f^{-1}(U)$ is open for any open subset $U\subseteq Y$. But by definition of the topology on the direct limit, it suffices to show that $f^{-1}(U)\cap X_m$ is open for all $m$. 
	This is clear as $f^{-1}(U)\cap X_m=f_m^{-1}(U)$, which is open as $f_m$ is continuous. Finally, the uniqueness of $f$ follows from the fact that the maps $X_m\to \lim_{m\to \infty} X_m$ are injective and their images cover $\lim_{m\to \infty} X_m$.
 \end{proof}
\end{comment}
 \begin{corollary} \label{cor:kTheo-directGrassmanSystem}
	The so-called inverse system given by the projections 
	\begin{equation*}
		\pi: \C^m \to \C^{m-1},\quad (x_1,\dots,x_m)\mapsto (x_1,\dots,x_{m-1})\,,
	\end{equation*}induces a direct system of Grassmann manifolds with the induced continuous maps
	\begin{equation*}
		i_{m-1}: \Gr_n(\C^{m-1}) \to \Gr_n(\C^{m})\,,\quad U\mapsto \pi^{-1}(U)\,.
	\end{equation*} If we denote the classifying bundle over $\Gr_n(\C^m)$ by $E_m$, then the pullback of $E_m$ along $i_{m-1}$ is isomorphic to $E_{m-1}$.
 \end{corollary}
 \begin{theorem}
	\label{thm:kTheory-classifyingSpace}
	The map 
	\begin{equation*}
		\colim_{m} [X,\Gr_n(\C^m)] \to \Vect_n(X)\,,
	\end{equation*} induced by $f\mapsto f^*E_m$ is a bijection for all compact spaces $X$.
 \end{theorem}
 \begin{proof}
	We shall construct an inverse map. If $E$ is a bundle over $X$, then by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-epiFromTrivial} there exists an epimorphism $\phi:X\times \C^m \to E$ for some integer $m$. Let $f:X\to \Gr_n(\C^m)$ be the map induced by $\phi$. If we manage to show that the homotopy class of $f$ as a map to $\Gr_n(\C^{m'})$ for some large enough $m'$ is independent of the choice of $\phi$ and $m$, then we get a well defined map from $\Vect_n(X)$ to $\colim_m [X,\Gr_n(\C^m)]$ that is inverse to the classifying map. 

	Suppose that $\phi_i :X\times \C^{m_i}\to E$ for $i=1,2$ are two epimorphisms. Let $g_i:X\to \Gr_n(C^{m_i})$ be the maps induced by $\phi_i$. We want to show that $g_1$ and $g_2$ are homotopic as maps to $\Gr_n(\C^{m_1+m_2})$. For this we define the map
	\begin{equation*}
		\psi_t: X\times \C^{m_1}\times C^{m_2} \to E\,,\quad \psi_t(x,v_1,v_2)=t\phi_1(x,v_1)+(1-t)\phi_2(x,v_2)\,.
	\end{equation*} Since the $\phi_i$ are epimorphisms, this map is an epimorphism for any $t$. Let 
	\begin{equation*}
		f_t:X\to \Gr_n(\C^{m_1+m_2})
	\end{equation*} be the maps induced by $\psi_t$. (where we identified $\C^{m_1}\times \C^{m_2}$ with $\C^{m_1+m_2}$ by mapping $(v_1,v_2)$ to $(v_1,v_2)$). Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		f_0=j_0\circ g_1\quad \text{and}\quad f_1=T \circ j_1\circ g_2\,,
	\end{equation*} where $j_i:\Gr_n(\C^{m_i})\to \Gr_n(\C^{m_1+m_2})$ is the map induced by the inclusion $\C^{m_i}\to \C^{m_1+m_2}$ and $T$ is the map induced by the isomorphism $\C^{m_1+m_2}\to \C^{m_1+m_2}$ that sends $(v_1,v_2)$ to $(v_2,v_1)$. Since $T$ is homotopic to the identity, we can conclude that $j_0\circ g_1$ and $j_1\circ g_2$ are homotopic as maps to $\Gr_n(\C^{m_1+m_2})$. Hence, the homotopy class of the map induced by an epimorphism is independent of the choice of the epimorphism and the integer $m$. This concludes the proof.
 \end{proof}
 \begin{lemma}\label{lem:kTheo-colimUndHomVertauschen}
	For any compact $X$ and direct system $\big((Y_m)_{m\in \N},(i_m)_{m\in \N}\big)$ where all $Y_m$ are Hausdorff and all $i_m$ are closed embeddings we have a canonical bijection between the set theoretic direct limit of homotopy classes of maps and the homotopy classes of maps to the topological direct limit:
\begin{equation*} 
	\colim_m [X,X_m] \cong [X,\colim_mX_m]\,.
\end{equation*}
 \end{lemma}
 \begin{proof}
	We denote $\colim_mY_m=Y$ and the corresponding inclusion $\phi_m:Y_m\to Y$ . Since $X$ is compact, any map $f:X\to Y$ has image that lies in some $\im(\phi_m)$ for $m$ big enough. Assume that this was not the case, then there would exist an increasing sequence $(j_i)_{i\in \N}$ such that for all $i$ there is an $x_i$ with $f(x_i)\in \im \phi_{j_i}\setminus \im \phi_{j_i-1}$. With this we define the set 
	\begin{equation*}
		A\coloneq \{f(x_i)\,|\, i\in \N \}\subseteq \im f \subseteq Y\,.
	\end{equation*}Then $A$ is closed, since for all $m$ the intersection $\im\phi_m\cap A$ is finite and since $\im(\phi_m)$ is closed, said intersection is closed. By the final topology we can conclude that the whole $A$ is closed and since it is contained in the image of $f$ it is compact (Here we use that the $Y_i$ are Hausdorff!). Furthermore, the same argument shows that every subset $B\subset A$ is closed, because again $B\cap \im \phi_m$ is finite. This tells us that $A$ as a subset of $Y$ carries the discrete topology: Let $C\subseteq A$ be an arbitrary subset. Then $A\setminus C\subseteq A$ is closed in $Y$ and hence its complement in $Y$ is open and intersected with $A$ yields $C$. Hence, $C$ is open in the subspace topology. This however is a contradiction, as $A$ cannot be infinite, compact and carry the discrete topology.
	Hence any map $f:X\to Y$ has image that lies in some $\im(\phi_m)$ for $m$ big enough. But then there exists a $f_m$ such that $f=\phi_m\circ f_m$.  This assignment is well defined on homotopy classes because if $f,s:X\to Y$ are homotopic, then there exists a homotopy $H:X\times I\to Y$ between them: By the same argument as before, the image of $H$ is compact and hence contained in some $\im \phi_m$. Hence, $f_m$ and $s_m$ are homotopic via $H_m$ as maps to $\im \phi_m$. Hence, we get a well defined canonical map 
\begin{equation*}
	\Phi: [X,\colim_m Y_m] \to \colim_m [X,Y_m]\,.
\end{equation*}This map is injective, as if $f_m$ and $s_m$ are homotopic as maps to $\im\phi_m$, then they are also homotopic as maps to the direct limit. Finally $\Phi$ is surjective, as any element of the direct limit is represented by some $f_m:X\to Y_m$ and hence $\Phi([\phi_m\circ f_m])=[f_m]$. 
 \end{proof}
 
 \begin{definition}
	We denote $\colim_m \Gr_n(\C^m)$ by $\BU(n)$ and call it the \textbf{classifying space} for complex vector bundles of rank $n$. The name originates from the functor $\mathrm{B}$ that sends a topological group $G$ to the classifying space of vector bundles with structure group $G$. 
 \end{definition}
 \begin{corollary} \label{cor:kTheo-classifyingSpaceVectorBundles}
	For any compact space $X$, there is a canonical bijection between the homotopy classes of maps from $X$ to $\BU(n)$ and the isomorphism classes of vector bundles of rank $n$ over $X$:
	\begin{equation*}
		[X,\BU(n)] \cong \Vect_n(X)\,.
	\end{equation*}
 \end{corollary}
 \begin{proof}
	This is a direct application of Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-colimUndHomVertauschen}.
 \end{proof}
 \subsection{K-Theory as a Cohomology Theory}
 \begin{definition}
	We need the following categories of topological spaces:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $\TopC$ is the category of compact spaces.
		\item $\TopCPoint$ is the category of well-pointed compact spaces.
		\item $\TopCPair$ is the category of pairs of compact spaces, where the inclusions are cofibrations.
	\end{itemize}
	Here, we have the natural functors:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $\TopCPair \to \TopCPoint$ by sending a pair $(X,Y)\mapsto (X/Y,[Y])$ with obvious morphisms. If $Y=\emptyset$ this is the disjoint union of $X$ with a point.
		\item $\TopC \to \TopCPair$ by sending $X\mapsto (X,\emptyset)$ again with obvious morphisms. The inclusion of $\emptyset$ into $X$ is a cofibration, as the empty set itself is an open neighbourhood and hence the pair is a NDR pair.
		\item $\TopC \to \TopCPoint$ as the composition. Again, the inclusion of $[\emptyset]$ into the disjoint union $X \sqcup [\emptyset]$ is a cofibration, as $\{[\emptyset]\}$ is an open neighbourhood of itself.
	\end{itemize}
	If we descend to the category where the morphisms are homotopy classes of maps, we get the categories $\hTopC$, $\hTopCPoint$ and $\hTopCPair$. Furthermore, the construction gives a functor from the category of abelian semigroups to the category of abelian groups. 
\end{definition}

  \begin{definition}[Grothendieck Group and Ring]\label{def:kTheo-grothendiekGroupAndRing}
	Let $A$ be an abelian semigroup. Then the \textbf{Grothendieck group} of $A$ is the abelian group $K(A)$ together with a homomorphism $\phi:A\to K(A)$ such that for any other group $G$ together with a homomorphism $\psi:A\to G$, there exists a unique homomorphism $\psi:K(A)\to G$ such that the following diagram commutes:	
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			A \arrow[r,"\phi"] \arrow[rd,swap,"\psi"] & K(A) \arrow[d,dashed,"\tilde{\psi}"]\\
			 & G
		\end{tikzcd}	
	\end{center} If $G$ is a semiring, then $K(G)$ inherits a ring structure and we call $K(G)$ the \textbf{Grothendieck ring} of $G$.
  \end{definition}
  \begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-ConstructionOfGrothendiekGroup} 
	The Grothendieck group exists for any abelian semigroup and is unique up to canonical isomorphism. If $G$ is a semiring, then the Grothendieck ring exists and is unique up to canonical isomorphism.
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{proof}The construction follows the following idea: We define tuples $(a,b)$ that are to be interpreted as differences \enquote{$a-b$}. For this construction to yield something useful we want to enforce the rule that \enquote{$a-b=a+c-(b+c)$} or said differently $(a+c,b+c)=(a,b)$. Finally, to account for the universal property, we want to use the smallest relation that obeys said rule. This gives us the following construction: Let $A$ be an abelian semigroup and $\Delta:A\to A\times A$ be the diagonal homomorphism that sends $a$ to $(a,a)$. Let $K(A)\coloneq A\times A\big/ \Delta(A)$. By this notation we mean: $\Delta(A)$ acts on $A\times A$ via
	\begin{equation*}
		\Delta(A) \times (A\times A ) \to A\times A\,\quad ((a,a),(b,c))\mapsto (a+b,a+c)\,.
	\end{equation*} With this we have the set of orbits which gives rise to a relation:
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{R}\coloneq \{((a,b),(a+c,b+c))| c\in A\}
	\end{equation*}Now we take the smallest equivalence relation on $A\times A$ that contains $\mathcal{R}$ (This relation is made explicit in Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-grothendieckEquivalence}). We define $K(A)$ to be $A\times A$ modulo said relation. Then $K(A)$ is a priori an abelian semigroup, but for $a,b\in A$ we have
\begin{equation*}
	(a,b)+(b,a)=(a+b,b+a)\in \Delta(A)\,,
\end{equation*} and hence $K(A)$ is an abelian group. Also we can see that 
\begin{equation*}
\overline{(a,b)}=\overline{(a,0)}-\overline{(b,0)}=\alpha_A(a)-\alpha_A(b)
\end{equation*} Together with the homomorphism $\alpha_A:A\to K(A)$ that sends $a$ to the class of $(a,0)$, we get the universal property of the Grothendieck group:

Let $G$ be another abelian group and $\psi: A\to G$ a semigroup homomorphism. From this define 
\begin{equation*}
	\tilde{\psi}:K(A)\to G\,,\quad  \overline{(a,b)} \to \psi(a)-\psi(b)
\end{equation*} The well definition of this map can be verified by a calculation. By calculating it also follows that $\tilde{\psi}$ is a group homomorphism and that $\tilde{\psi}\circ \alpha_A=\psi$. Hence, for the universal property we only need to verify uniqueness. So assume that $\theta:K(A)\to G$ is another group homomorphism. Then:
\begin{equation*}
	\theta\left(\overline{(a,b)}\right)=\underbrace{\theta(\alpha_A(a))}_{\psi(a)}-\underbrace{\theta(\alpha_A(b))}_{\psi(b)}=\tilde{\psi}\left(\overline{(a,b)}\right)
\end{equation*}
This concludes the construction of the Grothendieck group. To see the functoriality we first need to define what $K(\beta)$ means, when $\beta: A\to B$ defines a semigroup homomorphism between two abelian semigroups. For this we use the universal property and define $K(\beta)$ to be the unique group homomorphism coming from $\alpha_B\circ \beta : A \to K(B)$. Now the uniqueness of said definition shows the equalities $K(\id)=\id$ and $K(f\circ g)=K(f)\circ K(g)$.
\end{proof}



\begin{remark}\label{rem:kTheo-grothendieckEquivalence}
In $K(A)$ we get the following equivalence for any two pairs $\overline{(a,b)}$ and $\overline{(a',b')}$:
\begin{equation*}
	\overline{(a,b)}=\overline{(a',b')}\,\Leftrightarrow \text{ There exists a } c\in A \text{ such that } (a+b'+c)=(a'+b+c)\,.
\end{equation*}	
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	We start by proving \enquote{$\Leftarrow$}. This direction follows from the properties of an equivalence relation, as then, every element of an equivalence class is also a representative:
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:kTheo-theGrothendiekRelation}
		\overline{(a,b)}=\overline{(a+(a'+b+c),b+(a+b'+c))}=\overline{(a'+(a+b+c),b'+(a+b+c))}=\overline{(a',b')}\,.
	\end{equation} The other direction follows from the minimality of the equivalence relation: To see this notice how the argument above works in every equivalence relation containing the orbits of $\Delta(A)$ and hence works in the smallest one. Furthermore, \begin{equation*}
		(a,b)\sim(a',b')\Leftrightarrow\text{ There exists a } c\in A \text{ such that } (a+b'+c)=(a'+b+c)
	\end{equation*}defines an equivalence relation that contains the orbits and hence this is the smallest one.
\end{proof}


\begin{remark}[A simpler Construction of the Grothendieck Group?] One might be wondering, why we defined the relation on the pair $A\times A$ as the smallest equivalence relation starting by identifying the orbits of the operation of the diagonal, when Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-grothendieckEquivalence} might look and feel different. The reason for this detour is that I wanted to understand what Atiyah meant when he defined in Chapter II.1 \cite{atiyah1989k} word for word: 

	\textit{ A slightly different construction of $K(A)$ which is sometimes convenient is the following: Let $\Delta: A\to A$ be the diagonal homomorphism of semi-groups, and let $K(A)$ denote the set of cosets of $\Delta(A)$ in $A\times A$. It is a quotient semi-group, but the interchange of factors in $A\times A$ introduces an inverse in $K(A)$ so that $K(A)$ is a group.}
	
	Whilst this definition feels easy to the reader, it carries a lot of baggage. First of all, if one is not familiar with commutative monoid or abelian semigroup theory, one might want to generalise the notion of coset from group theory that states that for a subgroup $A\subseteq G$ of an abelian group $G$ the cosets are of the form $g+A$. Whilst this notion can be generalised to semigroups, it would yield something useless. To see this just take $\N$. In the semigroup $\N\times \N \big/ \Delta(\N)$ the cosets $(2,0)+\N$ and $(3,1)+\N$ are different, as $(2,0)$ is not contained in the latter. To give Atiyah the benefit of the doubt we could generalise the definition of cosets as orbits of the right translation, which is what we did in our construction. However, the literature on semigroups from that time defines the notion of quotients in the setting of semigroups with the help of \textit{congruence} instead of cosets. The latter refers to an equivalence relation that behaves well with the operation of said semigroup. Compare Chapter 1.5 \cite{CliffordPreston1961}. It was even shown that the notion of congruence and coset are equivalent for groups (compare for example: Chapter 6.1 \cite{Green1965}). Regardless of what Atiyah meant by his notion of coset, the construction became known and cited. (\cite{Brodzki1996}, \cite{Landweber2005}, \cite{Courtney2004}) None of those references define coset in semigroups and hence this little detour into the history should function as a reminder, that we need to be careful when generalising things from the known categories. We are so used to cosets giving equivalence relations, that it's easy to overlook the needed care. 
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
	We will omit the inclusion maps $\alpha$ from now on and interpret (if not said different) the equivalence class $[E]$ of a vector bundle as an element in $K(X)$.
\end{remark}
\begin{comment}
Let $\beta:A \to A'$ be another semigroup homomorphism. Then we have the following commutative diagram:
~\vspace{0.3cm}

\noindent
\begin{minipage}{0.4\textwidth}
\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzcd}
			A \arrow[r,"\id\times 0"] \arrow[d,"\beta"]& A\times A \arrow[r,"\pi"] \arrow[d,"\beta\times \beta"] \arrow[dr,"\pi'\circ (\beta \times \beta)"]& K(A) \arrow[d,"K(\beta)",dashed]\\
			A'\arrow[r,"\id\times 0"]				   & A'\times A' \arrow[r,"\pi"] & K(A')
		\end{tikzcd}
 \end{center}
\end{minipage}\quad%
\begin{minipage}{0.5\textwidth}
	Where $K(\beta)$  is the map that comes from the universal property of the quotient applied to $\pi'\circ \beta \times \beta $ and itself is well behaved with respect to composition. 
\end{minipage}

\vspace{0.3cm}
Hence, the assignment $A\mapsto K(A)$ and $\beta\mapsto K(\beta)$ defines a functor from the category of abelian semigroups to the category of abelian groups. To see the universal property of $K(A)$ we first notice that for a group $G$ the group $K(G)$ is naturally isomorphic to $G$ itself, via the map $\alpha_G$, since $(a,b)\mapsto a-b$ is a well defined natural inverse of $\alpha_G$ and of the vertical bottom row  of the diagram $\pi \circ (\id \times 0)$. Hence said inverse together with the unique $K(\beta)$ shows the universal property of $K(A)$ as the Grothendieck group. Uniqueness of $K(A)$ is the classical diagram chase argument. Also it is obvious, that if $G$ is a semiring, then $K(G)$ inherits a ring structure and the same argument as before shows the universal property of $K(G)$ as the Grothendieck ring of $G$.
\end{comment}
\begin{corollary}
	In the proof above we have shown that the assignment $K$ is a functor from the category of abelian semigroups to the category of abelian groups. Alternatively, we can view $K$ as a functor from the category of semirings to the category of rings.
\end{corollary}
\begin{definition}
	Let $X$ be a compact Hausdorff space. We define the \textbf{K-group} of $X$ to be the Grothendieck ring of the semiring $\Vect(X)$ of isomorphism classes of vector bundles over $X$ with the direct sum as addition and the tensor product as multiplication. We denote the K-group of $X$ by $K(X)$. As a whole we get a contravariant functor from the category of compact Hausdorff spaces to the category of rings
	\begin{eqnarray}
		\TopC \to \Ring\,.
	\end{eqnarray}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	For a continuous map $f:X\to Y$ we denote the induced map $K(f^*):K(Y)\to K(X)$ by just $f^*$.
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}
	The functor $K:\TopC \to \Ring$ factors through the homotopy category $\hTopC$ of compact Hausdorff spaces together with homotopy classes of maps. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof} This directly follows from Theorem \ref{thm:kTheo-homotopyInvariance}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Reduced K-Theory]\label{def:kTheo-reducedKTheory}
	Let $X$ be a compact Hausdorff space and $x_0\in X$ be a base point. We define the \textbf{reduced K-group} $\tilde{K}(X)$ of $X$ to be the kernel of the map $K(X)\to K(\{x_0\})$ induced by the inclusion $\{x_0\}\to X$.	
\end{definition}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-splittingReducedGroupNatural}
	If $c:X\to x_0$ is the collapsing map sending each point to the base-point, then we get a natural splitting 
	\begin{align*}
		K(X)\cong \Tilde{K}(X) \oplus K(x_0) \, .
	\end{align*}  Naturality here means, that for any homomorphism of pointed spaces $f:(X,x_0)\to (Y,y_0)$ we have a commuting diagram:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			K(X)\arrow[r]\arrow[d,"f^*"]& \Tilde{K}(X) \oplus K(x_0)\arrow[d,"\tilde{K}(f)\oplus (\{x_0\}\to \{y_0\})^*"]\\
			K(Y)\arrow[r]& \Tilde{K}(Y) \oplus K(y_0)
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	We have the exact sequence of rings:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}(X) \arrow[r, "inclusion"] & K(X) \arrow[r, "i_X^*"] & K(x_0) \arrow[r] & 0
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} where $c_X^*:K(x_0)\to K(X)$ is a right inverse of $i_X^*$, and hence the sequence splits and $K(X)\cong \tilde{K}(X)\oplus K(x_0)$. The isomorphism is explicitly given by
	\begin{equation*}
		K(X)\to \tilde{K}(X)\oplus K(x_0)\,,\quad \xi \mapsto \left(\xi- c_X^*\circ i_X^*(\xi),i_X^*(\xi)\right)
	\end{equation*} and its inverse maps $(a,b)\mapsto a+c_X^*(b)$. The splitting is natural with respect to maps in $\TopCPoint$, because for a map of pointed spaces $f:X\to Y$ we get commutativity:
	\begin{center}\label{diag:kTheo-NaturalSplitting}
		% https://tikzcd.yichuanshen.de/#N4Igdg9gJgpgziAXAbVABwnAlgFyxMJZARgBoAGAXVJADcBDAGwFcYkQAdDvR2YAaQC+ACgAaAShCDS6TLnyEUAJgrU6TVu35jJ02djwEi5VTQYs2iEOSkyQGAwqIBmU+otbhADwD65XXYO8kYoACxu5ppWNnr2coaKyCbEapGW1rb6wYlkKWYa6Vw8fELCAJoBWQlEKnnuUSDaFZlxjiHIrnVpngCefpWt2UThXQXsMWowUADm8ESgAGYAThAAtkgqIDgQSOSxy2u7NNtIZFv0WIzsABYQEADWLQfriGcniK716VgAegBUIBojHoACMYIwAArxJxWJZYabXHBPFYvT7vUL7FFIACsxx2iAAbJjDoS8UgAOzHC5XKy3B7IkmUrb4gAcxJeLLJiAAnOykJzmRSaGCwFAkKFufkPFYAMY+Mr-BkcrmfbpWYRcJbwHBLYALQTAXx7cSKoGg8FQtqKEBwhFIvmIJnvM5qkALRUOtH4s4isWIAC0EqlDTlolNIGBYMh0JCNvhiKklEEQA
		\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}(X) \arrow[r, hook] & K(X) \arrow[r, "i_X^*"']          & K(x_0) \arrow[r] \arrow[l, "c_X^*"', bend right=49]                                & 0 \\
			0 \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}(Y) \arrow[r, hook] \arrow[u,"\restr{f^*}{\tilde{K}(Y)}"]& K(Y) \arrow[r,"i_Y^*"] \arrow[u, "f^*"] & K(y_0) \arrow[r] \arrow[l, "c_Y^*", bend left=49] \arrow[u, "(\restr{f}{x_0})^*"'] & 0
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	The well definition of $ \restr{f^*}{\tilde{K}(Y)}$ follows immediately from the commutativity. The commutativity itself follows from $K$ being a contravariant functor and all topological maps commute. Finally, define $\tilde{K}(f)\coloneq \restr{f^*}{\tilde{K}(Y)}$ and we are done, since  the naturality of the
splittings, i.e.\ commutativity of the diagram in the corollary, then
follows from commutativity of each square separately: the left square
controls the $\tilde{K}$-summand and the right square (together with
the sections $c_X^*, c_Y^*$) controls the $K(x_0)$-summand.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
	For a map $f:(X,x_0)\to (Y,y_0)$ we denote $\tilde{K}(f)$ and $K(f)$ by $f^*$. The context will make clear if we work in the reduced theory or not.
\end{remark}
\begin{corollary}
	$\tilde{K}$ is a functor from $\TopCPoint$ to $\Ring$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	The proof follows immediately from the naturality of the splitting in Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-splittingReducedGroupNatural}.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-unpointedReducedIsNonReduced}
	Let $X$ be a compact Hausdorff space, and let 
	\begin{equation*}
		F:\TopC \to \TopCPoint
	\end{equation*} be the functor that sends $X$ to $(X,[\emptyset])$ with obvious homomorphisms. Then there is a natural isomorphism between the functors $\tilde{K}\circ F$ and $K$. 
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	This follows  from the fact that disjoint unions give rise to direct sums of semirings: $\Vect(X\sqcup \{\emptyset\})\cong \Vect(X)\oplus \Vect(\{\emptyset\})$ and the isomorphism is given by gluing the bundles and hence natural. Furthermore, the Grothendieck ring of a direct sum gives the direct sum of rings (again in a natural manner). (This can be verified using the universal property). Hence we get the commutative diagram
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r]\arrow[d,equal]&\tilde{K}(F(X)) \arrow[r,"inclusion"] & K(F(X)) \arrow[d,"\Phi"]\arrow[r,"i^*"] & K([\emptyset]) \arrow[r] \arrow[d,equal] &0\arrow[d,equal] \\
			0 \arrow[r]&K(X) \arrow[r,"i_1"] & K(X)\oplus K([\emptyset]) \arrow[r,"\pi_2"]&K([\emptyset]) \arrow[r] &0
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
 But then we can define the map $\restr{\Phi}{\tilde{K}(F(X))}$ and diagram yoga shows that this is an isomorphism. Furthermore, since $\Phi$ is natural, the new map is also natural and defines a natural transformation between the two functors.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
	Let $F:\TopCPair \to \TopCPoint$ be the functor that collapses the second space. Then the composition $\tilde{K}\circ F$ defines a contravariant functor.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
	Let $(X,x_0)$ and $(Y,y_0)$ be two pointed spaces. Then we define the \textbf{pointed sum} as: 
	\begin{equation*}
		X \vee Y \coloneq (X\sqcup Y) \big/ (x_0\sim y_0) \,.
	\end{equation*} With this, we can define the \textbf{smash product} as:
	\begin{equation*}
		X\wedge Y \coloneq (X\times Y)\big/ (X\vee Y)
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
	For the sake of intuition, I want to give a bit of insight into tensor products without being too technical and opening Pandora's box of monoidal categories. We define a tensor product in a category simply as the space over which bilinear maps factor uniquely. To capture bilinearity in such a category we define the following. Let $\pi_i:X_0\times X_1 \to X_i$ be the canonical projection for $i=0,1$.  Any reasonable product should come with such surjective maps. Since those are surjective, the $\pi_i$ admit a family of right inverses $r_i: X_i\to X_0\times X_1$. We call such a right inverse \enquote{product preserving} if the compositions $\pi_{2}\circ r_1$ and $\pi_1\circ r_2$ are constant. Hence the right inverse maps into just one fibre. Now bilinearity in this general setting states, that for any product preserving right inverse of the projections $r_i$, the composition $f\circ r_i$ is structure preserving.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}[What is the Smash Product]
	We can think of the smash product as the tensor product in the pointed topological spaces as follows. Let $f:(X,x_0)\times (Y,y_0)\to (Z,z_0)$ be bilinear. For any $y\in Y$ we have the product preserving right inverse of $\pi_1$ given by
	\begin{equation*}
		r_1^y: (X,x_0)\to (X,x_0)\times (Y,y_0),\quad x \mapsto (x,y)\,.
	\end{equation*} Analogously we define $r_2^x$ for any $x\in X$ as
	\begin{equation*}
		r_2^x: (Y,y_0)\to (X,x_0)\times (Y,y_0),\quad y \mapsto (x,y)\,.
	\end{equation*}
	 Now for any $x\in X$ we have $f(x,y_0)=f\circ i^x_Y(y_0)=z_0$ as the composition is said to be basepoint preserving. Similarly we see that $f(x_0,y)=z_0$ for any $y\in Y$. Hence $f$ factors through the quotient space \begin{equation*}
		X\times Y \big/ (X\times \{y_0\} \cup (Y\times \{x_0\})) \,,
	 \end{equation*} where the numerator is the canonically embedded $X\vee Y$ into the product. Furthermore by the universal property of quotient spaces, this factorisation is unique.
\end{remark}
\begin{example}
	Let $S^n$ be the $n$-dimensional sphere given by all points of norm one in $\R^{n+1}$. Then a general fact is that $S^n\cong I^n\big/ \partial I^n$ and hence for all $n,m>0$ we have $S^n\wedge S^m\cong S^{n+m}$. Later \todo{ref} we will make this homeomorphism canonical.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[From Real Space to the Sphere]\label{def:orient-fromRelaSpaceToTheSphere}
    We now want to construct a canonical homeomorphism between $S^m$ and $\overline{\R^m}$. First we use the stereographic projection from the South Pole: If $m=0$ we define this to be 
	\begin{equation*}
		\pi_s:S^0=\{-1,1\} \setminus \{-1\} \to \R^0\,,\quad 1\mapsto 0\,.
	\end{equation*} For $m>0$ we define:
	    \begin{align*}
        \pi_S:S^m\setminus \{S\}\subseteq \R^{m+1} &\to       \R^m\\
        (x_1,\dots,x_m,x_{m+1})                            &\mapsto   \left(\frac{x_1}{1+x_{m+1}},\dots, \frac{x_m}{1+x_{m+1}} \right)
    \end{align*} with its inverse: 
    \begin{align*}
        \pi_S^{-1}:\R^m       &\to     S^m\setminus\{S\}\subseteq \R^{m+1}\\
        (y_1,\dots y_m)       &\mapsto  \left(\frac{2y_1}{1+\norm{y}^2},\dots,\frac{2y_m}{1+\norm{y}^2},\frac{1-\norm{y}}{1+\norm{y}} \right)
    \end{align*}
    Those are inverse maps and they give rise to maps between their one-point-compactifications. Hence $\overline{\R^m}\cong S^m$. Lets calculate $\pi_S^{-1}\circ \pi_s$, since we will later need a little bit of insight in those calculations:
    \begin{align*}
        \pi_S^{-1}\circ \pi_s(x_1,\dots ,x_{m+1})
        &= \pi_S^{-1}\left(\frac{x_1}{1+x_{m+1}},\dots, \frac{x_m}{1+x_{m+1}} \right)\\
        &= \left(\frac{2\frac{x_1}{1+x_{m+1}}}{1+\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})},\dots, \frac{2\frac{x_m}{1+x_{m+1}}}{1+\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})},\frac{1-\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})}{1+\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})} \right)\\
    \end{align*} Before we keep calculating, we notice the denominator can be rewritten as:
    \begin{align*}
        1+\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})=\frac{(1+x_{m+1})^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2}
    \end{align*} And hence:
    \begin{align}
        \frac{2\frac{x_j}{1+x_{m+1}}}{1+\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})}& = \frac{2\frac{x_j}{1+x_{m+1}}}{\frac{(1+x_{m+1})^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2}} \\
        & =\frac{2x_j(1+x_{m+1})}{(1+x_{m+1})^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2} \\
        & =\frac{2x_j(1+x_{m+1})}{(1+x_{m+1})^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}\\
        & = \frac{2x_j(1+x_{m+1})}{1+2 x_{m+1}+\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{m+1} x_i^2}_{=1}} \\
        & = x_j
    \end{align}
    And the calculation of the last term is:
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1-\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})}{1+\sum_{i=1}^m(\frac{x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2})} 
        & = \frac{\frac{(1+x_{m+1})^2-\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2}}{\frac{(1+x_{m+1})^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2}}\\
        & =\frac{(1+x_{m+1})^2-\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}{(1+x_{m+1})^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2} \\
        & = \frac{1+2x_{m+1}+x_{m+1}^2-\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}{1+2x_{m+1}+\underbrace{x_{m+1}^2+\sum_{i=1}^m x_i^2}_{=1}} \\
        & = \frac{1+2x_{m+1}+2x_{m+1}^2-\sum_{i=1}^{m+1} x_i^2}{2+2x_{m+1}} \\
        & = \frac{x_{m+1}(2+2x_{m+1})}{2+2x_{m+1}}=x_{m+1}
    \end{align*}
This concludes the calculation of $\pi_S^{-1}\circ \pi_s=\id$ and $\pi_s\circ\pi_S^{-1}=\id$ can be calculated in a similar manner.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}[One-Point Compactification]
	For a topological space $X$ we define $\overline{X}\coloneq X\sqcup \{\infty\}$, where open sets are either open subsets of $X$, or sets of the form $\{\infty\} \sqcup X\setminus C$ where $C$ is compact and closed in $X$. For non compact Hausdorff spaces this is the one-point compactification that is characterised by being the unique (up to homeomorphism) compactification that contains $X$ and one more point. If $X$ is already compact, then $\{\infty\}$ is an open subset of $\overline{X}$ (just choose $C=K$) and thereby isolated. Hence $ \overline{X}$ is the pointing of $X$ given by $(X,[\emptyset])$ from Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-unpointedReducedIsNonReduced}.
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}[Open subspaces of compact spaces]\label{lem:orient-quotientIsCompactification}
	Let $Z$ be a compact Hausdorff space and $U\subseteq Z$ open. Then the map
	\begin{equation*}
		Z\big/ (Z\setminus U)\to \overline{U}\,,\quad [z]\mapsto z \text{ for } z\in U\,,\quad [Z\setminus U]\mapsto \infty
	\end{equation*}
	is a homeomorphism.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The map is the quotient of the function $\phi:Z\to \overline{U}$ that is the identity on $U$ and sends $Z\setminus U$ constant to $\infty$. Hence, we only need to prove continuity for $\phi$. Nor $O\subseteq \overline{U}$ is open, if it is open in $U$, or the complement of a compact set $C\subset U$ in $\overline{U}$. In both cases, $\phi^{-1}(U)$ is open and hence $\phi$ is continuous. Since the quotiont map is now a continuouos bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space, it is a homeomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Compactification of a product]\label{lem:orient-compactificationOfProduct}
	Let $X$ and $Y$ be locally compact Hausdorff spaces, and point $\overline{X}$ and
	$\overline{Y}$ at their respective points at infinity. Then there is a
	homeomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		\Xi_{X,Y}:\overline{X\times Y}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ } \overline{X}\wedge \overline{Y}
	\end{equation*}
	which is the identity on $X\times Y$ and sends $\infty$ to the base point.
	It is natural in the sense that for proper maps $f:X\to X'$ and $g:Y\to Y'$ the
	square
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			\overline{X\times Y} \arrow[r, "\Xi_{X,Y}"] \arrow[d, "\overline{f\times g}"'] & \overline{X}\wedge\overline{Y} \arrow[d, "\overline{f}\wedge\overline{g}"] \\
			\overline{X'\times Y'} \arrow[r, "\Xi_{X',Y'}"']                               & \overline{X'}\wedge\overline{Y'}
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	commutes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $Z\coloneq \overline{X}\times \overline{Y}$. Then $Z$ is a compact Hausdorfspace that contains $U\coloneq X\times Y$ as an open subset. The complement of $U$ consists of those points where one of the coordinates is infinit and hence
    \begin{equation*}
        Z\setminus U\,=\,(\{\infty\}\times \overline{Y})\cup (\overline{X}\times \{\infty\})\,=\, \overline{X}\vee \overline{Y}\,.
    \end{equation*}Therefore, by definition of the smash product, $ Z\big/(S\setminus U)=\overline{X}\wedge \overline{Y}$ and by Lemma \ref{lem:orient-quotientIsCompactification} we can conculde that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{X\times Y} \cong (Z\big/ (Z\setminus U)=\overline{X}\wedge \overline{Y}\,.
    \end{equation*}
    For the naturality notice that the product of proper maps is propper and hence all maps are continouos. The commmutation then comes done to checking where infinity gets mapped which is always the basepoint of the wedge product.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}[Associativity and Kommutativity]\label{lem:kTheo-SmaschIsAssociative}
	For pointed compact spaces $(A,a_0),(B,b_0),(C,c_0)$ there is a natural homeomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		(A\wedge B) \wedge C \to A\wedge (B\wedge V)
	\end{equation*}That restricts to the identity over $(A\setminus \{a_0\})\times( B\setminus \{b_0\}) \times (C\setminus \{c_0\})$.
	Furthermore, there is a natural homeomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		A\wedge B\to B\wedge A
	\end{equation*}that restricts to the change of coordinates over 
	\begin{equation*}
		(A\setminus \{a_0\}\times B\setminus\{b_0\})\to (B\setminus \{b_0\}\times A\setminus\{a_0\})
	\end{equation*}

\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Notice how for all compact pointed spaces $(X,x_0)$ the identyty (and $x_0\mapsto \infty)$ is a natural homeomorphism $\overline{X\setminus \{x_0\}}\cong X$ and we denote $X^\circ \coloneq X\setminus \{x_0\}$. Furthermore, the smash product of compact spaces is again compact (as it is a quotient of a closes space). Hence we can just calculate
	\begin{align*}
		(A\wedge B) \wedge C 
		& \cong \overline{(A\wedge B)^\circ} \wedge \overline{C^\circ}\\
				& \cong \overline{(A^\circ\times B^\circ)} \wedge \overline{C^\circ}\\
		& \cong \overline{(A^\circ \times B^\circ) \times C^\circ} \\
		& \cong \overline{A^\circ \times( B^\circ \times C^\circ)} \\
		& \cong A\wedge (B\wedge V)\,.
	\end{align*} 

		For the kommutativity we can proceed in the same way:
	\begin{align}
		A\wedge B \cong \overline{(A\wedge B)^{\circ}}\cong \overline{(B\wedge A)^{\circ}}\cong B \wedge A
	\end{align}
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
	The statement about associativity can be extended to the category of $\mathbf{CGWH}^*_{\mathbf{cof}}$, but not all pointed topological spaces. \todo{ref einfügen}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition} [Convention]\label{def:ktheo-wedgeOfShoperesIsSphere}
	Let $\pi_s^n$ denote the compactified stereographical projection $\overline{S^n}\to \overline{\R^n}$ 
	With those methods we can now define the homeomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Upsilon_{n,m}\, \coloneq \,(\pi_s^{n+m})^{-1}\circ \Xi^{-1}\circ (\pi_s^n)\wedge (\pi_s^m) :S^n\wedge S^m \to S^{n+m}\,.
	\end{equation*} Often we will identify smash product of spheres and for those identification we will always use this homeomophism. For the composition to make sence we identify $\R^n\times \R^m$ with $\R^{n+m}$ (also for $n$ or $m$ equal zero.)
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}[Assoziativity of $\Upsilon$]\label{rem:kTheo-AssoziativityOfUspilon}
	Let $\Upsilon$ be the map from Definition \ref{def:ktheo-wedgeOfShoperesIsSphere}, and 
		\begin{equation*}
			AS:(S^k\wedge S^l)\wedge S^m \to S^k\wedge ( S^l\wedge S^m)
		\end{equation*}
	denote the associativity map of the wedge product from Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-SmaschIsAssociative}. Then for all $k,l,m\in \N$ we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\Upsilon_{k+l,m}\circ \big(\Upsilon_{k,l}\wedge \id_{S^m}\big)\,=\, \Upsilon_{k,l+m}\circ \big(\id_{S^k}\wedge \Upsilon_{l,m}\big)\circ AS\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	We will pointwise compare the maps. Both sides map the basepoint to the basepoint. Now assume that $x\in (S^k\wedge S^l)\wedge S^m$ is not the basepoint. Then $x=[[u,v],w]$ with $u,v,w \nequal S$ where $S$ is the South pole. Let $a=\pi_S(u),\, b=\pi_S(v),\,c=\pi_s(w)$. Since $\Xi$ is the identity outside of the base points we know that the left side reads
	\begin{equation*}
		\pi_S^{-1}((a,b),c)=\pi_S^{-1}(a,b,c)\,,
	\end{equation*}whilst the right side reads
	\begin{equation*}
		\pi_S^{-1}(a,(b,c))=\pi_S^{-1}(a,b,c)\,.
	\end{equation*}(Remember, that we omitted the identification of $\R^n\times \R^m\to \R^{n+m})$.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
	Let $(X,x_0)$ be a pointed topological space. We define the \textbf{reduced suspension} of $X$ to be 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Sigma X\coloneq S^1\wedge X \,.
	\end{equation*} 
	Furthermore, we define inductively $\Sigma^nX\coloneq \Sigma (\Sigma^{n-1}X)\cong S^n\wedge X$ and using the principle of permanence we extend this definition to $\Sigma^0X\coloneq S^0\wedge X\cong X$. The last homeomorphism is canonical.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
	Let $(X,x_0)$ be a pointed topological space. We define the \textbf{suspension} of $X$ to be the space
	\begin{eqnarray}
		S(X)\coloneq X\times I \big/ \sim
	\end{eqnarray} where $(x,t)\sim(x',t')$ if $t=1$ or $t=0$. Here we identify the top to a point and the bottom to a different point. ($(x,1)\nsim (0,0)$). We view $(S(X),(x,1))$ as a pointed space.
\end{definition}
In general the reduced and unreduced suspension differ. However, under the following condition there will be a natural homotopy equivalence:
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:kTheo-wellpointedImpliesCylinderCofibration}
If $(X,x_0)$ is well-pointed, then the inclusion 
$\{x_0\}\times I \hookrightarrow S(X)$ is a cofibration.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This proof is omitted but is Proposition 5.1.16 in \cite{tomdieck2008algebraic}.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary} \label{cor:kTheo-reducedSuspensionIsSuspension}
	If $(X,x_0)$ is well pointed, then $S(X)$ is homotopically equivalent to $\Sigma(X)$. 
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	By lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-wellpointedImpliesCylinderCofibration} we conclude that $(S(X),{x_0}\times I)$ satisfies the homotopy extension property. Hence, by Lemma \ref{lem:topPre-hepImpliescontractingisHomotopicalInvariant} we know that 
	$S(X)\simeq S(X)\big/ {x_0}\times I$ and the homeomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		S(X)\big/{x_0}\times I \cong \Sigma(X)
	\end{equation*} concludes the proof.
\end{proof}





\begin{definition}[Lower K-groups] \label{def:kTheo-lowerKGroub}
	For $n\geq 0$ we define:
	\begin{align*}
		\begin{array}{lclll}
			\Tilde{K}^{-n}(X,x_0)&\coloneq &\Tilde{K}(\Sigma^n X)& \text{for } &(X,x_0)\in Ob(\TopCPoint),\\
			K^{-n}(X,Y)&\coloneq &\Tilde{K}^{-n}(X\slash Y,[Y]) &\text{for } &(X,Y)\in Ob(\TopCPair)\\
			K^{-n}(X) &\coloneq &K^{-n}(X,\emptyset) &\text{for } &X\in Ob(\TopC)~.
		\end{array}
	\end{align*}
	All those definitions give contravariant and homotopically invariant functors into the category of rings.
\end{definition}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-K(X)istK(X,nix)}
	Let $X$ be a topological space. Then we have a natural isomorphism between the functors $K^0(X)$ and $K(X)$. Furthermore, if $(X,x_0)$ is a pointed topological space, we have a natural isomorphism between $\tilde{K}$ and $K^0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	When disentangling the definitions, we see that 
	\begin{equation*}
		K^0(X)=\tilde{K}(\Sigma^0(X,[\emptyset]))\cong \tilde{K}(X,[\emptyset])\cong K(X)
	\end{equation*} where the first isomorphism comes from $\Sigma^0$ being canonically isomorphic to the identity and the second isomorphism is given in Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-unpointedReducedIsNonReduced}. The natural isomorphism between $K^0$ and $\tilde{K}$ is given by definition and the canonical isomorphism $X\cong S^0 \wedge (X)$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
	If we have pointed topological spaces $(X,x_0)$ or pairs $(X,A)$ and want to denote $K^0(X,pt)$ or $K^0(X,A)$ we will often omit the exponent and just write $K(X,x_0)$ and $K(X,A)$.
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}[Excision]
	For any pair $(X,A)$ and $U\subseteq A$ open such that $\bar{U}\subseteq \mathring{A}$ the inclusion 
	\begin{equation*}
		i: (X\setminus U,A\setminus U) \to (X,A)
	\end{equation*} induces an isomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		i^*:  K^{-n}(X,A)\to K^{-n}(X\setminus U,A\setminus U)
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	Since $U$ is open and $A$ is closed, $(X\setminus U,A\setminus U)$ is a compact pair. The inclusion $i$ induces a continuous bijection 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi:(X\setminus U)\Big/(A\setminus U)\to X\big/ A
	\end{equation*} which is a homeomorphism as a continuous bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space. Hence $i^*$ is an isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Homotopy-Theoretic definition of $K$}

\begin{definition}\label{def:kTheo-trivialBundleAndLimitInclusinos}
	For any natural number $n\in \N$ we define $\underline{n}$ to be the $n$-dimensional trivial bundle over a base space $X$. With this we define the inclusions
	\begin{equation*}
		i_n: \Vect_n(X)\to \Vect_{n+1}(X)\,,\quad \xi \mapsto \xi \oplus [\underline{1}],
	\end{equation*} and this gives rise to the direct limit $\colim_n \Vect_n(X)$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}[Stable Equivalence]
	Two vector bundles $E,F$ over the same base space are called \textbf{stably equivalent} if there exists an $n$ such that $E$ and $F$ become isomorphic after adding the trivial bundle: 
	\begin{equation*}
		E\oplus \underline{n}\cong F\oplus \underline{n}\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
	In $K(X)$ two equivalence classes $[E]$ and $[F]$ are the same, if and only if $E$ and $F$ are stably equivalent.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Assume that two vector bundles satisfy
	\begin{equation*}
		E\oplus \underline{n}\cong F\oplus \underline{n}\,,
	\end{equation*} for some $n\in \N$. By abuse of notation we let $E$ (and $\underline{n}$) denote a vector bundle and its isomorphism class simultaneously. Then by Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-grothendieckEquivalence}
	\begin{equation*}
		[E]=\overline{(E,0)}=\overline{(E+\underline{n},\underline{n})}=\overline{(F+\underline{n},\underline{n})}=\overline{(F,0)}=[F]\,.
	\end{equation*}
	On the other hand, if $[E]=[F]$, then by the same Remark we can conclude:
	\begin{equation*}
		(E,0)\sim (F,0)\\
		\Leftrightarrow \text{ there exists } G \text{ such that} E\oplus G=F\oplus G \,.
	\end{equation*} Now by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-splittingTrivial} we can find a vector bundle $G'$ such that $G\oplus G'=\underline{n}$ for some $n$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		E\oplus G\oplus G'=F\oplus G \oplus G' \Rightarrow E\oplus \underline{n}=F\oplus \underline{n}
	\end{equation*}
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
	We call two vector bundles $E,F$ over the same base space \textbf{eventually equivalent}, if there exist $n,m\in \N$ such that $E\oplus \underline{n}\cong F\oplus \underline{m}$.
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
	The set of equivalence classes using the notion of eventual equivalence is the limit 
	\begin{equation*}
		\colim_n \Vect_n(X)
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}Let $\mathrm{EVect}(X)$ be the set of equivalence classes using the notion of eventual equivalence. This satisfies the universal property of the direct limit. To see this let $f_m:\Vect_m(X)\to Y$ be a family of maps, that commute with the inclusions $i_n$ from Definition \ref{def:kTheo-trivialBundleAndLimitInclusinos}. From those we define the map 
	\begin{equation*}
		f: \mathrm{EVect}(X) \to Y\,, \quad [\xi] \mapsto f_{\rank(\xi)}(\xi)\,.
	\end{equation*} This map is well defined, because $f$ commuting with the inclusions $i_n$ is equivalent to $f$ being invariant under eventual equivalence. Uniqueness follows from every element of $\mathrm{EVect}(X)$ being represented by a vector bundle.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}
	The set of equivalence classes using the notion of eventual equivalence  together with the direct sum is an abelian group
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Everything except inverses is clear. The neutral element is the class of $\underline{0}$. For inverses we take a bundle $E$ and by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-splittingTrivial} there exists a $G$ such that $E\oplus G\cong\underline{n}$. Now $[E]+[G]=[\underline{n}]=[\underline{0}]$.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:kTheo-reducedKisEventually}
	For a compact connected space $X$ there is a natural group isomorphism.
	\begin{equation*}
		K(X) \cong \Z\times \mathrm{EVect}(X) \cong\Z \times \colim_n \Vect_n(X)\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Furthermore if $(X,p)$ is pointed, there is a natural group isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(X)\cong \mathrm{EVect}(X) \cong \colim_n \Vect_n(X)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $K$ be an element of $K(X)$. Then by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-ConstructionOfGrothendiekGroup} we can write $K$ as the difference of two classes of vector bundles $K=[E]-[F]$. We abuse the notation of equivalence classes in this proof meaning we won't differentiate between isomorphism classes of vector bundles in $\Vect_n$, eventually or stable isomorphism classes or equivalence classes of naturally included vector bundles in $K(X)$. The context should always be clear.  With this we define the function
	\begin{equation*}
		\rank(K): K(X)\to \Z\,,\quad K=[E]-[F] \mapsto \rank(E)-\rank(F)\,.
	\end{equation*} This is well defined as if $K=[E]-[F]=[E']-[F']$, then by the relation in $K(X)$ we know that there is an $n$ such that
	\begin{equation*}
		E\oplus F' \oplus \underline{n}\cong E' \oplus F \oplus \underline{n}\,.
	\end{equation*} and hence:
	\begin{equation*}
		\rank(E)-\rank(F)=\rank(E')-\rank(F')
	\end{equation*} Furthermore, $\rank$ has a natural right inverse given by
	\begin{equation*}
		r: \Z \to K(X)\,,\quad m\mapsto \begin{cases}
			[m]-[0]& \text{ if }m\geq 0\\
			[0]-[-m]& \text{ if }m<0\,.
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation*}
	Now we claim that the kernel $\ker(\rank)\cong \mathrm{EVect}(X)$. For this we define the map 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi: \mathrm{EVect}(X)\to K(X)\,,\quad [E]\mapsto [E] -[\underline{\rank(E)}]
	\end{equation*}To see the well-definedness we assume that $E$ and $E'$ are eventually isomorphic and hence there are $n,n'\in \N$ such that $E\oplus \underline{n}\cong E'\oplus \underline{n'}$. But then in $K(X)$ we have:
	\begin{equation*}
		[E]-[\underline{\rank(E)}]=[E\oplus \underline{n}]-[\underbrace{\underline{\rank(E)}\oplus\underline{n}}_{=\underline{\rank(F)}\oplus \underline{n'}}]=[E'\oplus \underline{n'}]-[\underline{\rank(F)}\oplus \underline{n'}]=[F]-[\underline{\rank(F)}]\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Obviously $\rank\circ \phi \equiv 0$. To get the other inclusion we first notice the following: Let $K\in K(X)=[E]-[F]$ be arbitrary. By Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-splittingTrivial} there exists a $G$ such that $F\oplus G\cong \underline{n}$ for some $n\in \N$. Hence if $K$ is in the kernel of $\rank$ we have:
	\begin{equation*}
		K=[E]-[F]=[E \oplus G]-[\underline{n}]=[E\oplus G]-[\underline{\rank(E\oplus G)}]= \phi([E \oplus G])
	\end{equation*}
	This leads to the naturally split short exact sequence
\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r]& \mathrm{EVect}(X)\arrow[r,"\phi"]&K(X)\arrow[r,"\rank"']&\Z \arrow[r] \arrow[l,bend right,"r"']& 0
		\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
This already proves the isomorphisms \begin{equation*}
		K(X) \cong \Z\times \mathrm{EVect}(X) \cong\Z \times \colim_n \Vect_n(X)\,.
	\end{equation*}
Now we define the group isomorphism 
\begin{equation*}
	l:\Z \to K(p)\,,\quad m\mapsto \begin{cases}
			[m]-[0]& \text{ if }m\geq 0\\
			[0]-[-m]& \text{ if }m<0\,.
		\end{cases}
\end{equation*}
This gives the commutative diagram:
\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r]& \mathrm{SVect}(X)\arrow[r,"\phi"]&K(X)\arrow[d,"\id"]\arrow[r,"\rank"']&\Z \arrow[d,"l"] \arrow[r] \arrow[l,bend right,"r"']& 0 \\
			0 \arrow[r]& \tilde{K}(X)\arrow[r,"inclusion"]&K(X)\arrow[r,"i^*"]&K(p) \arrow[r] \arrow[l,bend left,"c_p^*"]& 0
		\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Where $i$ is the inclusion $\{p\}\to X$ and $c_p$ is the collapse $X\to \{p\}$. Then by some diagram chasing the map 
\begin{equation*}
	\tilde{\phi}: \mathrm{SVect}(X)\to \tilde{K}(X) 
\end{equation*} is well defined and by the five lemma (add zeros to the short sequences on the left) it is an isomorphism.
\end{proof} 
\begin{remark}\label{rem:kTheo-elementsInReducedKtheoryAreGivenByEMionusN}
	In the proof above we have seen that every element $k\in K(X)$ is given as the difference of a vector bundle and a trivial bundle $k=[\xi]-[\underline{n}]$ for some vector bundle $\xi$ and $n\in \N$. We also saw that 
	$\tilde{K}(X)$ is in the kernel of $\rank$ and hence any $k\in \tilde{K}(X)$ is given by $k=[\xi]-[\underline{\rank(\xi)}]$ where $\xi$ is a vector bundle.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
	We define the \textbf{unitary group} $U$ to be the direct limit of the unitary group of rank $n$ under the standard inclusions
	\begin{equation*}
		U(n) \to U(n+1)\,,\quad A \mapsto A \oplus \Idm_1 =  \begin{pmatrix}
			A&0\\ 0&1
		\end{pmatrix}\,.
	\end{equation*} 
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
	We now want to make $BU(n)$ into a direct system. Notationally, this proof will have many maps with two indices. The convention is that lower indices show for which colimit the maps form a directed system. As an example, the maps $i^n_m$ are used for the colimit over $m$. We start with the inclusion
	\begin{equation*}
		j_m: \C^m\to \C^{m+1}\,,\quad (x)\mapsto (x,0)\,,
	\end{equation*}which gives rise to the maps
	\begin{equation*}
		f_n^m:\Gr_n(\C^m) \to \Gr_{n+1}(\C^{m+1})\,,\quad V\mapsto j_m(V)\,.
	\end{equation*} Those inclusions commute with the maps 
	\begin{equation*}
	i_m^n:\Gr_n(\C^m)\to \Gr_n(\C^{m+1})\,,\quad V\mapsto V\oplus \langle e_{m+1} \rangle
	\end{equation*} from the direct system from Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-directGrassmanSystem}. If we denote the inclusion by $\phi_{m}^{n+1}:\Gr_{n+1}(\C^m)\to \colim_m(\Gr_{n+1}(\C^m))$ we get the following commuting diagram, for all $n\leq m$:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			\Gr_n(\C^m) \arrow[d,"i_m"] \arrow[r,"f_n^m"]&\Gr_{n+1}(\C^{m+1}) \arrow[d,"i_m^n"]\arrow[r,"\phi_{m+1}^{n+1}"]&\colim_m(\Gr_{n+1}(\C^m))=BU(n+1)\\
			\Gr_n(\C^{m+1}) \arrow[r,"f^{m+1}_n"]&    \Gr_{n+1}(\C^{m+2})\arrow[ur,"\phi_{m+2}^{n+1}"]&
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} 
	Now for all $n$ we use the universal property of the direct limit $\colim_m(\Gr_n(\C^m))$ on the family 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Big(
			\phi^{n+1}_{m+1}\circ f^m_n: \Gr_n(\C^m) \to BU(n+1)
		\Big)_m
	\end{equation*} to induce maps $f_n:BU(n)\to BU(n+1)$, which in turn give us a directed system and let us define:
	\begin{equation*}
		BU\coloneq \colim_nBU(n)=\colim_n\big(\colim_m \Gr_n(\C^m)\big)\,.
	\end{equation*} 
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
	The maps $f_n:BU(n)\to BU(n+1)$ are closed embeddings.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Since $\Gr_n(\C^m)$ is compact and Hausdorff for any $n\leq m$ the maps $f_n^m$ are closed embeddings. Furthermore, the natural maps $\phi^n_m:\Gr_n(\C^m)\to BU(n)$ are also closed embeddings. Now assume that $f_n(x)=f_n(x')$. Then for $r$ big enough (omitting the inclusions) $f_n^r(x)=f_n^r(x')$ which contradicts the injectivity of the $f_n^r$. Now we want to show that $f_n$ has a closed image. For this we let $C\coloneq \im f_n \subset BU(n+1)$ and call the natural inclusions $\phi^{n+1}_m: \Gr_{n+1}(\C^m)\to BU(n+1)$. Then $C$ is closed, if and only if $C\cap \im \phi_m^{n+1}$ is closed for all $m>n$. To show this we first show that $C\cap \im \phi_m^{n+1}=\im\, \phi^{n+1}_m\circ f_n^{m-1}$. The first inclusion $\subseteq$ is clear. The other inclusion follows directly from the construction of $f_n$ that satisfies $f_n\circ \phi^n_{m-1}=\phi^{n+1}_m\circ f^{m-1}_n$. By Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-staircaseMapsClosedEmbedding} we know that $\phi^{n+1}_m \circ f^{m-1}_n$ is a closed embedding hence $C\cap \im \phi_m^{n+1}$ is closed for all $m>n$ and hence $C$ is closed. Now finally we need to show that $f_n$ are homeomorphisms onto their image. For this we show that they are closed maps, meaning they map closed sets to closed sets. This property together with injectivity implies the closed embedding property.
	Let $U\subset BU(n)$ be closed. By construction of the direct limit we can write $U=\bigcup_m (\im(\phi^n_m)\cap U)=\bigcup_m (\phi^n_m(U_m))$, where $U_m\coloneq (\phi^n_m)^{-1}(U)$. Now by the same argument as before we have the equality $f_n(U)\cap \im\phi^{n+1}_{m+1}= \phi^{n+1}_{m+1}\circ f^m_n(U_m)$. This is closed for all $m$ because the maps $\phi^{n+1}_{m+1}$ and $f_n^m$ are closed embeddings and hence closed. But then $f_n(U)$ is closed in the final topology and hence $f_n$ is a closed embedding.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:kTheo-reducedKgroupAsHomotopy}
	For a connected pointed space $X$ we have a natural homomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(X)\cong [X,BU]
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	We can now interchange the homotopy classes of maps and the limit by Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-colimUndHomVertauschen}: 
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{array}{llr}
			[X,BU]	&=[X,\colim_n\big(BU(n))] &	\\
					&\cong\colim_n \Vect_n(X) & \quad \text{ by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-classifyingSpaceVectorBundles},}\\
					&\cong \tilde{K}(X) 	  & \quad \text{ by Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-reducedKisEventually}.}
		\end{array}
	\end{equation*}
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:kTheo-generalGroupContractsToUnitary}
	The general linear group $\GL_n(\C)$ retracts onto the unitary group $U(n)$ and the retraction commutes with the inclusions $A\mapsto \begin{pmatrix}
		A&0\\
		0&1
	\end{pmatrix}\,.$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Let $A\in \GL_n(\C)$. Then $A^*A$ is a positive-definite Hermitian matrix, since for $v \in \C^n$:
	\begin{equation*}
		v^*A^*Av=(Av)^*(Av)=\norm{Av}^2\geq 0\,.
	\end{equation*}  Hence, it is diagonalisable with real non-negative eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\dots \lambda_n>0$. We define $P_A\coloneq \sqrt{A^*A}$ to be the matrix, that scales all eigenspaces by $\sqrt{\lambda_i}$. Then $P_A^2 =A^*A$, and $\sqrt{A^*A}$ is also positive definite Hermitian, as all eigenvalues are positive and hence, it is invertible. Notice, that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\sqrt{
		\begin{pmatrix}
			A&0\\0&1
		\end{pmatrix}^* 
				\begin{pmatrix}
			A&0\\0&1
		\end{pmatrix}
		}=
				\begin{pmatrix}
			\sqrt{A^*A}&0\\0&1
		\end{pmatrix}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	We then define $U_A\coloneq A  (P_A)^{-1}$. By definition $A=U_A P_A$, and since $P_A$ is Hermitian, $P_A^{-1}$ is also Hermitian letting us conclude that $U_A$ is unitary.
	\begin{equation*}
		U_A^*U_A=((P_A)^{-1})^*\underbrace{A^*A}_{P_A^2}  (P_A)^{-1}=\Idm\,.
	\end{equation*} Notice again, that for $\tilde{A}=A\oplus \Idm_1\in GL_{n+1}(\C)$, we have $U_{\tilde{A}}=U_A\oplus \Idm_1\in U(n+1)$. Now the space of positive-definite Hermitian matrices is convex. This is just a calculation of all the axioms for the matrices on the connecting line of two positive-definite Hermitian matrices. But as a convex space, it is contractible.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:kTheo-reducedSuspendedKGroupHomotopically}
	For a connected pointed space $X$ with well sitting basepoint, we have a natural homomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(\Sigma X)\cong [X,U]
	\end{equation*} The group structure in $[X,U]$ comes from viewing $[X,U]$ as the direct limit of abelian groups $\colim[X,\GL_n(\C)]$, where the group structure is derived from the one in $\GL_n(\C)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
	If the base point is well sitting, we can apply Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-reducedSuspensionIsSuspension}, which gives us the homotopy equivalence
	\begin{equation*}
		\Sigma X \simeq S(X)\,.
	\end{equation*} Now $S(X)$ itself can be cut into two contractible cones 
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{array}{ll}
			C_+(X) \coloneq & \{(x,t)\in S(X) \,|\, t\geq \frac{1}{2} \}\\
			C_-(X) \coloneq & \{(x,t)\in S(X) \,|\, t\leq \frac{1}{2} \} \,.	
		\end{array} 
	\end{equation*}
	Those cones intersect in $C_+\cap C_-= X\times \{\frac{1}{2}\}\cong X$. Now denote the trivial $n$ bundle over $C_+(X)$ by $\underline{n}_+$ and over $C_-(X)$ by $\underline{n}_-$. With this notation we know by the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-gluingAndHomotopyClassesToGLn} that the map
	\begin{align}\label{eq:kTheo-SXtoHomotoy}
		\phi_n: [X,\GL_n(\C)]&\to \Vect_n(\Sigma X)\\
		f&\mapsto \underline{n}_+ \Glue{f} \underline{n}_- \nonumber
	\end{align} is a bijection for all $n$. Furthermore, the inclusion of $\GL_n(\C)$ into $\GL_{n+1}(\C)$ gives an inclusion $[X,\GL_n(\C)]$  into $[X,\GL_{n+1}(\C)]$ which commutes with the inclusion $\Vect_n(\Sigma X)$ into $\Vect_{n+1}(\Sigma X)$ under the bijection (\ref{eq:kTheo-SXtoHomotoy}) meaning that for any $n\leq m$ the diagram
	\begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
            {[X,\GL_n(\C)]} \arrow[r,"\phi_n"] \arrow[d,"i_{nm}"] &\Vect_n(\Sigma X) \arrow[d,"i_{nm}"]\\
            {[X,\GL_m(\C)]} \arrow[r,"\phi_m"]           &\Vect_m(\Sigma X)
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}commutes. Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-reducedKisEventually} gives us 
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(X)\cong \colim_n [X,\GL_n(\C)]\cong [X,\colim_n\GL_n(\C)]\,.
	\end{equation*} The latter bijection follows from Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-colimUndHomVertauschen}.
	Finally, Lemma \ref{lemma:kTheo-generalGroupContractsToUnitary} gives us a retraction $\GL_n(\C) \to U(n)$ that commutes with the inclusions and hence we get the same direct limit.
	\begin{equation*}
		[X,\colim_n\GL_n(\C)]\cong [X,\colim_nU(n)]=[X,U]
	\end{equation*} concluding that we have a natural bijection. To get the group structure we need to look into said mapping. For this we inspect the mapping (\ref{eq:kTheo-SXtoHomotoy}) closer. Let 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi_n:[X,\GL_n(\C)]\to \colim \Vect_n(\Sigma X)
	\end{equation*} be the composition of $\phi_n$ with the inclusion $\Vect_n(\Sigma X) \to \colim \Vect_n(\Sigma X)$. By the commutative diagram above, the maps $\phi_n$ induce a map from $\colim [X,\GL_n(\C)]$ which is a group homomorphism, if all $\Phi_n$ are one. Hence take two functions $f,g:X\to \GL_n(\C)$. Then by definition
	\begin{equation*}
		f\oplus g: X \to GL_n(\C)\,,\quad x\mapsto f(x)\times g(x)
	\end{equation*} Now $f\oplus g$ can be included into $[X,\GL_{2n}(\C)]$:
	\begin{equation*}
		f\oplus g: X \to GL_{2n}(\C)\,,\quad x\mapsto \begin{pmatrix}
			f(x)\times g(x) & 0 \\
			0& \Idm_n\
		\end{pmatrix}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Now we have that $f\oplus g$ is homotopic to 
	\begin{equation*}
		\widetilde{f\oplus g}:X \to GL_{2n}(\C)\,, \quad 		x \mapsto\begin{pmatrix}
			f(x) & 0 \\
			0& g(x)
		\end{pmatrix} 
	\end{equation*} via the homotopy $\rho_t(x)$ for $t\in (0,\frac{\pi}{2})$:
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:kTHeo-homotopyBetweendirectSumAndProduct}
		\rho_t(x)=
		\begin{pmatrix}	f(x)&0		   \\0&\Idm_n 		  \end{pmatrix}
		\begin{pmatrix}	\cos(t)&\sin(t)\\ \sin(t)&\cos(t) \end{pmatrix}
		\begin{pmatrix} \Idm_n &0	   \\0&g(x)			  \end{pmatrix}
		\begin{pmatrix}	\cos(t)&\sin(t)\\ \sin(t)&\cos(t) \end{pmatrix}
	\end{equation} Finally we can look at the image of $[f\oplus g]=[\widetilde{f\oplus g}]$ under $\Phi_{2n}$ which is given by inclusion of
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi_n([\widetilde{f\oplus g}])=\underline{n}_+\oplus \underline{n}_+ \Glue{\widetilde{f\oplus g}} \underline{n}_-\oplus \underline{n}_-=[\phi_n(f)\oplus \phi_n(g)]
	\end{equation*} into the colimit, where the equation
	\begin{equation*}
	[\phi_n(f)\oplus \phi_n(g)]=[\phi_n(f)]\oplus [\phi_n(g)]
	\end{equation*} holds. The second last equality follows from the properties of the gluing construction given in Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-elementaryPropertiesGluingAndClutching}.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
	The proof of Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-reducedSuspendedKGroupHomotopically} gives a slightly more general statement:
	For a connected well pointed topological space $(X,x_0)$ with a splitting into two contractible parts $X^+$ and $X^-$ with $x_0\in A\coloneq X^+\cap X^-$ and $X^+\cup X^-=X$ and the inclusion of $A$ into the two contractible parts are cofibrations, there is a natural group isomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		[A,U]\cong \tilde{K}(X)
	\end{equation*} 
\end{remark}
\begin{corollary}\label{lem:kTheo-inversesInKGivenByHomotopicallyMethods}
		Let $Y$ be a pointed space. Define
		\begin{align*}
			T:S^1 &\to S^1 \\
			t     &\mapsto 1-t
		\end{align*} and let $T\wedge1:SY\to SY$ be the map induced by $T$ on $S^1$ and the identity on $Y$. Then:
		\begin{align*}
			(T\wedge1)^*:\tilde{K}(SY)   &\to \tilde{K}(SY)\\
			y                           &\mapsto -y
		\end{align*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	We have the following commutative diagram: 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			\tilde{K}(SX) \arrow[d, "(\Sigma T)^*"'] & {\colim[Y,\GL(n,\C)]} \arrow[d, "f\mapsto f^{-1} (pointwise)"] \arrow[l] \\
			\tilde{K}(SX)                             & {\colim[Y,\GL(n,\C)]} \arrow[l]                                         
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} To see why this commutes, notice how we defined the map by gluing via $f$, where $\Sigma T$ changes the place of the upper and lower vector bundle. Hence the gluing function needed is now the inverse. 
	The horizontal maps are natural group homomorphisms and on the right side the inverse is taken. Thereby the commutativity tells us that the left side is also the same as taking the inverse.
\end{proof}
\begin{example}\label{ex:kTheo-LineBundlesSatlquadequalslPlusl}
	Let $l$ and $l'$ be line bundles over $S^2$. Then the clutching function for the vector bundle $l\otimes l'$ is given by the tensor product of clutching functions. So let $f$ and $f'$ be two clutching functions
	\begin{equation*}
		f,f':S^1 \to 	  \GL_1(\C)
	\end{equation*} Then the product bundle $l\otimes l'$ is given by the clutching function 
	\begin{equation*}
		f\otimes f': S^1\to \GL(\C\otimes \C)\cong \GL_1(\C)\,,\quad x\mapsto f(x)f'(x)
		\end{equation*}
	Hence, the product in $\tilde{K}(S^2)$ is given by  $f\otimes f'=f\cdot f'$.
	Now, the homotopy (\ref{eq:kTHeo-homotopyBetweendirectSumAndProduct}) of the proof above shows that 
$f\cdot f'\oplus \id_1\simeq f \oplus f'$ and hence $l\otimes l'\oplus \underline{1}\cong l\oplus l'$.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}
	For compactly generated pointed Hausdorff spaces $(X,x_0),(Y,y_0)$ there is a natural bijection
	\begin{equation*}
		[SX,Y]\to [X,\Omega Y]\,,
	\end{equation*} where $\Omega$ denotes the loop space
	\begin{equation*}
		\Omega Y \coloneq\{f:S^1\to Y\,| \, f \text{ continuous and }f(1)=y_0\}\,.
	\end{equation*} together with the compact open topology.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
 For any three spaces $(X,x_0),(Y,y_0),(Z,z_0)$ we have that a basepoint preserving map
 $f:X\wedge Y\to Z$ can be lifted to a map $\tilde{f}:X \times Y \to Z;f(z)=\tilde{f}(\overline{z})$ that maps the whole $X\vee Y$ to $z_0$. This map is continuous by the universal property of the quotient topology. Hence we get a bijection
 \begin{equation*}
	C(X\wedge Y,Z)\to \{f\in C(X\times Y,Z)\,|\, \restr{f}{X\vee Y} \equiv z_0\}
 \end{equation*} its inverse is given by the quotient induced maps. 
 We can compose this map with the natural currying map $C(X\times Y,Z)\to C(X,C(Y,Z))$. For $Y=S^1$ we gain a map 
 \begin{equation*}
	C(SX,Z)\to C(X,\Omega Z)
 \end{equation*} that is again a bijection, since any $f:X\to \Omega Z$ gives a map $\tilde{f}:X\times S\to Z$ $\tilde{f}(x,s)\mapsto f(x)(s)$. If $(x,s)\in X\vee S\subseteq X\times S$, then either $x=x_0$ or $s=1$. In the first case, $f(x_0)$ is the constant loop and hence $f(x_0)(s)=z_0$ and in the second case we evaluate a based loop at its base and hence $f(x)(1)=z_0$. Hence, the $\tilde{f}$ factors through $X\vee S$ giving a well defined element in $C(SX,Z)$. This is its inverse. The continuity part of the argument follows from Proposition \ref{prop:kTheo-curryingIsContinuous}.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
	By Theorem \ref{thm:kTheo-reducedKgroupAsHomotopy}, and the Proposition above we know that for a connected pointed compact space we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(SX)\cong[SX,BU] \cong [X,\Omega B U]
	\end{equation*} On the other hand we have Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-reducedSuspendedKGroupHomotopically} which tells us that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(SX) \cong [X,U]\,.
	\end{equation*}This hints at a connection that states that 
$\Omega B U$ is homotopically equivalent to $ U$:
\end{remark}
 

\subsection{K-Theory as a Cohomology Theory}
This chapter aims to give the definitions and theorems that make K theory an axiomatic cohomology theory as defined by Eilenberg and Steenrod.
For this we start trying to find a long exact sequence and on the way we will prove the additivity:

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:kTheo-exactShortPartForThePair}
	Let $(X,Y)\in ob(\TopCPair)$ and define $i:(Y,\emptyset) \to (X,\emptyset)$ and $j:(X,\emptyset)\to(X,Y)$ to be the inclusions. Then we have an exact sequence:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"] & K(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & K(Y)
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	Here, we identify $K(X)=K(X,\emptyset)$ and the same with $K(Y)=K(Y,\emptyset)$ such that we have well defined induced functions from the same functor in the sequence. This is just a formal note, as $K(X,\emptyset)\cong K(X)$ by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-K(X)istK(X,nix)}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	First, we show that $\im(i^*)\subseteq \ker(j^*)$. To see this, notice that $i^*\circ j^*=(j\circ i)^*$, and $j\circ i$ factors through $(Y,Y)$:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{(Y,\emptyset)} \arrow[r, "i"] \arrow[d] & {(X,\emptyset)} \arrow[d, "j"] \\
			{(Y,Y)} \arrow[r]                        & {(X,Y)}                       
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	Hence, $j^*\circ i^*$ factors through the trivial ring $K(Y,Y)$ and thereby is trivial.
	Now suppose $k\in \ker(i^*)$. 
	The intuition now is that if we have a vector bundle that gets mapped to $0$ this means that it is trivial over $Y$ which lets us define a vector bundle over the quotient $X\slash Y$ by a trivialisation: 
	We write $k=[\xi]-[\underline{n}]$ for $\xi$ being a vector bundle over $X$. (Compare Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-elementsInReducedKtheoryAreGivenByEMionusN}) Since $i^*\xi=0$ it follows that $i^*([\xi]-[\underline{n}])=[\restr{\xi}{Y}]-[\restr{\underline{n}}{Y}]=0$ and hence $\xi$ and $\underline{n}$ are stably equivalent over $Y$ giving us an $m$ such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\restr{\left(\xi\oplus \underline{m}\right)}{Y}\cong \restr{\xi}{Y}\oplus\underline{m}\cong \underline{n}\oplus \underline{m}
	\end{equation*}
	Hence, there is a trivialisation $\alpha: \restr{\left(\xi\oplus \underline{m}\right)}{Y} \to Y\times \C^{\rank(\xi)+m}$ that defines a bundle $\restr{\left(\xi\oplus \underline{m}\right)}{Y} \big/\alpha$ as shown in the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-trivialisationDefinesABundle} over $X\big/Y$. Now we define 
	\begin{equation*}
		\eta=[\restr{\left(\xi\oplus \underline{m}\right)}{Y} \big/\alpha]-[\underline{n+m}]\in \tilde{K}(X\big/Y)=K(X,Y)
	\end{equation*} and since the trivialisation acts as a right inverse of the pullback of the restriction (compare Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-trivializationIsRightInverse})
	\begin{equation*}
		j^*(\eta)=[\xi \oplus \underline{m}]-[\underline{n+m}]=k\,.
	\end{equation*} 
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:kTheo-pointedUnionExactSequence}
	Let $X,Y$ be pointed topological spaces and define the maps
	\begin{equation*}
		i:X\to X\vee Y\,\text{ the inclusion, and }j:X\vee Y\to Y \text{ the collapse of $X$.}
	\end{equation*} Then the sequence
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r]&\tilde{K}(X)\arrow[r,"j^*"]&\tilde{K}(X\vee Y)\arrow[r,"i^*"]&\tilde{K}(Y) \arrow[r]&0
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} is exact and naturally splits via the map $q^*$, that is a right inverse of $j^*$, where 
	$
		q:X\vee Y \to Y 
	$is the collapse.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Notice how there is a natural homeomorphism $X\cong (X\vee Y)\big/ Y$.  Hence, Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-exactShortPartForThePair} provides the exactness at $K(X\vee Y)$. Now the map $q$ is a left inverse of $j$ as continuous maps, and hence $q^*$ is a right inverse of $j^*$ in the K group, proving the injectivity of $j^*$. Now only the surjectivity of $i^*$ is missing. However the inclusion $l:Y\to X\vee Y$ is a topological right inverse of $i$ and thereby $l^*$ is a left inverse of $i^*$ giving us the injectivity and hence proving the exactness and splitting of the short exact sequence.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}[Pointed Additivity] \label{thm:kTheo-pointedAdditivity}
	Let $X,Y$ be pointed topological spaces and denote the inclusions of $X$ (resp. $Y$) into $X\vee Y$ by $i_X$ (resp. $i_Y$). Then the map 
	\begin{equation*}
		i_X^*\oplus  i_Y^*:\tilde{K}(X\vee Y)\to \tilde{K}(X)\oplus \tilde{K}(Y)
	\end{equation*} is a natural isomorphism with inverse 
	\begin{equation*}
		\psi: \tilde{K}(X)\oplus \tilde{K}(Y) \to \tilde{K}(X\vee Y)\,,\quad (x,y)\mapsto c_X^*(x)+c_Y^*(y)\,,
	\end{equation*}where $c_X$ (resp. $c_Y$) denotes the collapse of $X$ (hence onto $Y$) respectively the collapse of $X$ and hence onto $Y$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	This is a direct consequence of Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-pointedUnionExactSequence} and the algebraic machinery of splitting short exact sequences.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}[Unpointed Additivity] \label{thm:kTheo-unpointedAdditivity}
	Let $X,Y$ be topological spaces, then there is a natural isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		K(X\sqcup Y)\to K(X)\oplus K(Y)
	\end{equation*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	This follows from $K(A)\cong \tilde{K}(A,[\emptyset])$ for all topological spaces. Furthermore, $(X\sqcup Y,[\emptyset])\cong ((X,[\emptyset])\vee (Y,[\emptyset]))$. Hence, using those isomorphisms, the statement is a direct consequence of the pointed additivity.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-exactnesOfTheReducedInclusionSequence}
	If $(X,Y)\in ob(\TopCPair)$ and $Y\in ob(\TopCPoint)$ such that $X$ is pointed by using the base point $y_0$ from $Y$. Then the sequence 
	\begin{center}
		% https://tikzcd.yichuanshen.de/#N4Igdg9gJgpgziAXAbVABwnAlgFyxMJZABgBpiBdUkANwEMAbAVxiRAGkAKADVIE0AlCAC+pdJlz5CKAIzkqtRizYAdFXgaxg7YTyGjx2PASIAmedXrNWiEGo1adnQSIUwoAc3hFQAMwBOEAC2SGQgOBBIcorWbABWAHoAVCJiIAHBUdQRSOYxyrZYya7CQA
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"] & \tilde{K}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & \tilde{K}(Y)
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} is exact.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	We have the commutative diagram with exact top row:
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K(X,Y)} \arrow[r] \arrow[rdd] & K(X) \arrow[r] \arrow[d, "\cong"]                        & K(Y) \arrow[d, "\cong"]             \\
			& \tilde{K}(X)\oplus K(y_0) \arrow[d, two heads] \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}(Y)\oplus K(y_0) \arrow[d] \\
			& \tilde{K}(X) \arrow[r]                                   & \tilde{K}(Y)                       
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{equation*} The middle horizontal map is a map that respects the grading, since we have the same base point. With this we can do a bit of diagram yoga concluding the statement.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}\label{def:kTheo-coneOverSubspace}
	Let $X$ be a topological space and $I=[0,1]$ be the unit interval. We define the cone over $X$ to be the space
	\begin{equation*}
		CX\coloneq (X\times I)\big/ X\times\{0\}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Let $(X,Y)$ be a pair of compact topological spaces. We define the cone over the subspace $Y$ to be 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Big(X\sqcup CY\Big)\Big/ Y\sim Y\times \{ 1\}
	\end{equation*} and denote that with $(X\cup CY)$.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition} \label{prop:kTheo-coneIsFunctorAndCYoverYIsRedSuspension}
	If we define for $f:X\to Y$ the induced maps on the cones $Cf$ by
	\begin{equation*}
		Cf:CX\to CY\,\quad \overline{(x,t)}\mapsto \overline{(f(x),t)},
	\end{equation*}the cone defines a functor $\Top\to \TopP$. 
	The cone over $Y\subseteq X$ defines in similar fashion a functor from $\mathbf{CGWH}^2_{\mathbf{cof}}$ to $\TopP$.
	There is a natural isomorphism:
	\begin{equation*}
		T:(X\cup CY)\big/ X \to CY\big/Y
	\end{equation*} Here, the left and right hand side both define functors from $\mathbf{CGWH}^2_{\mathbf{cof}}$ to $\TopP$ with the obvious morphisms. Furthermore, we have that $CY\big/Y$ is homotopically equivalent to $\Sigma Y$ (via the map induced from $\overline{(x,t)}\mapsto \overline{(t,x)}$ ) which is homotopically equivalent to $SY$ as long as $(Y,y_0)$ is well pointed. All the equivalences are natural up to homotopy.  
\end{proposition}
\begin{corollary}
	From the considerations above we get a natural isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\theta: K(X\cup CY,Y)\to \tilde{K}(SY)=\tilde{K}^{-1}(Y)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{corollary}

\begin{definition}[Connecting Homomorphism]\label{def:kTheo-connectingHomomorphism}
Let $(X,Y)$ be a topological pair of compact spaces and $Y$ be pointed. Define the two maps
\begin{align}
	m: (X \cup CY,\emptyset)&\to (X\cup CY,X)\\
	\pi: (X\cup CY,\emptyset)& \to X\big/Y
\end{align} where $m$ is the inclusion in the second space, and $\pi$ is the collapse of $CY$.
