Since $CY$ is contractible, the induced map $\pi^*:\tilde{K}(X\big/ Y)\to K(X \cup CY,\emptyset)$ is an isomorphism by Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-collapsingOfContractibleSubspace}. Together with the isomorphism  
	\begin{equation*}
		\theta: K(X\cup CY,X)\to \tilde{K}(SY)=\tilde{K}^{-1}(Y)\,.
	\end{equation*}
	We define the connecting homomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta\coloneq (\pi^*)^{-1}\circ m^*\circ \theta^{-1}:\tilde{K}^{-1}(Y)\to K(X,Y)
	\end{equation*} This map defines also the higher degrees connecting homomorphisms by replacing the spaces with their suspensions.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	The map $\delta: K^{-n}(A)\to K^{-n+1}(X,A)$ is a natural transformation between the functors 
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $K^{-n}\circ R$ from $\TopCPair$ to $\Ab$, where $R$ is an endofunctor in $\TopCPair$ sending $(X,A)\mapsto (A,\emptyset)$.
		\item $K^{-n+1}$ from $\TopCPair$ to $\Ab$.
	\end{itemize}
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	Let $f:(X,A)\to (Y,B)$ be a map of pairs. Then we need to check if the diagram 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{(X,A)} \arrow[d, "f"]  & {K^{-n}(A,\emptyset)}  \arrow[r, "\delta"]    & {K^{-n+1}(X,A)}                                           \\
			{(Y,B)}                 &{K^{-n}(B,\emptyset)} \arrow[r, "\delta"] \arrow[u, "(\restr{f}{A})^*"] &  {K^{-n+1}(Y,B)} \arrow[u, "f^*"] 
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} commutes. This however is the case since $\delta$ is defined via the pullback of the inclusion $(m^*)$ together with the natural isomorphisms $\theta$ and $\pi^*$. 
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Long Exact Sequence]\label{thm:kTheo-longExactSequence} For $(X,Y)\in ob(\TopCPair)$ we have a natural exact sequence that is infinite to the left:
\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzcd}
			\cdots \arrow[r] & K^{-2}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"]           & {K^{-1}(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"] & K^{-1}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & K^{-1}(Y) \\
			& \phantom{K^{-2}(Y)} \arrow[r, "\delta"] & {K^0(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"]    & K^0(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"]    & K^0(Y)   
		\end{tikzcd}
\end{center} The maps $i^*$ are induced by the inclusion $i:Y\to X$, $j^*$ are induced by the inclusion $j: (X,\emptyset)\to (X,Y)$ and $\delta$ is the connecting homomorphism. For the lower degrees, $\delta$ is the connecting homomorphism where $X$ and $Y$ are replaced by the reduced suspensions of $X$ and $Y$. 	
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	We will prove the pointed version of the theorem, that states that for a compact pair $(X,Y)$ such that $Y$ is itself compact pointed (and $X$ is pointed with the same basepoint) we have the exact sequence (infinite to the left):
	\begin{center}
			\begin{tikzcd}
			\cdots \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}^{-2}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"]           & {K^{-1}(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-1}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y) \\
			& \phantom{\tilde{K}^{-2}(Y)} \arrow[r, "\delta"] & {K^0(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"]    & \tilde{K}^0(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"]    & \tilde{K}^0(Y)   
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	The pointed sequence induced the statement for unpointed spaces because for any topological space $A$ we have a natural isomorphism $\tilde{K}(A,[\emptyset])\cong K(A)$.

	We will furthermore just prove the exactness of the segment:
	\begin{equation}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			\tilde{K}^{-1}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"] & {K^0(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"] & \tilde{K}^{0}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & \tilde{K}^{0}(Y)
		\end{tikzcd} \label{eq:kTheo-proofSesShortAndreducedSequence}
	\end{equation} From this we regain the long sequence by replacing $(X)$ and $Y$ with $S^n(X)$ and $S^nY$.
	
	In $\tilde{K}^0(X)$, the exactness was shown in Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-exactnesOfTheReducedInclusionSequence}.
	To get the exactness at the other spots we apply the same corollary to the pairs:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $(X\cup CY,X)$
		\item $((X\cup CY)\cup CX,X\cup CY)$
	\end{itemize}
	For the middle part we have the commutative diagram where the naming of the maps is the same as in the definition of the connecting homomorphisms (Definition \ref{def:kTheo-connectingHomomorphism}).
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K(X\cup CY,X)} \arrow[d, "\theta"] \arrow[r, "m^*"] & \tilde{K}(X\cup CY) \arrow[r, "k^*"]         & \tilde{K}(X) \arrow[d, "\id"] \\
			K^{-1}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"]                        & {K(X,Y)} \arrow[u, "\pi^*"] \arrow[r, "j^*"] & \tilde{K}(X)                 
	\end{tikzcd}    \end{center}
	The commutativity in the left square is the definition of $\delta$ and in the right square follows from $\pi\circ k=j:X\to X\big/Y$. Since all vertical maps are isomorphisms, Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-exactnesOfTheReducedInclusionSequence} implies exactness in the bottom row $K(X,Y)$. Now only the exactness in the first three terms is missing. For this we look at the pair $((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X,X\cup C_1Y)$ , where the  $C_i$ are cones and denoted with the index to distinguish the cones. First we get again an exact sequence:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X,X\cup C_1Y\big)} \arrow[r, "l^*"] & \tilde{K}\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X\big) \arrow[r, "s^*"] & \tilde{K}(X\cup C_1Y)
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	Our goal is now to show that we commute with the start of the exact sequence (\ref{eq:kTheo-proofSesShortAndreducedSequence}), hence to find vertical maps between the top rows (denoted with ${\psi}$ in the diagram below) such that the diagram commutes:
	\begin{fdiagram} \label{diag:kTheo-proofConnectingHomo}
	\begin{tikzcd}
			{K\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X,X\cup C_1Y\big)} \arrow[r, "l^*"] \arrow[d, "{\psi}_1"] & \tilde{K}\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X\big) \arrow[r, "s^*"] \arrow[d, "{\psi}_2"] & \tilde{K}(X\cup C_1Y) \arrow[d, "{\psi}_3"] \\
			\tilde{K}^{-1}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"]                                            & \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"]                                    & {K(X,Y)}                             \\                                 
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{fdiagram}
	 \todo{The bottom row is added for clarification on what inclusion induces $i^*$.. besser erklähren} 
	We start with the right square and notice that by definition we get a commutative diagram:
	\begin{center}
		% https://tikzcd.yichuanshen.de/#N4Igdg9gJgpgziAXAbVABwnAlgFyxMJZABgBoAmAXVJADcBDAGwFcYkQBpAPWAFoBGAL4AKAJoBKEINLpMufIRRl+1Ok1bsOwgBoAdXQGNmaAAQBhAPr9RpbZOmzseAkX6kVNBizaIQ+vIywwBwieobG5lYSUjIgGE4KrhSqXhq+Wtqk0Q5xcs6KJKTEKeo+nDr6RqaW1pURluR2MY7yLihuxZ6l7P5YgTDBoXXVUfaqMFAA5vBEoABmAE4QALZIZCA4EEgAzF3ePbqwjDj0zSCLK0huG1uI66ll-gAWMCcgNCd97JBgbDQv9CgSDAzEYjByF1WiGumyQ5D2aRAyy4ACozpCkAAWD63a4PdjCXr9YD6NBYQSo8Q8ASCdFLKEAVhxcIRj10WCBEPpLJuO1ZBNJWEpXAEdMuiGxvMQTLU+18cFRUkogiAA
		\begin{tikzcd}
			K(X\cup C_1Y\cup C_2X) \arrow[d, "(\tilde{\pi}^*)^{-1}"] \arrow[r, "s^*"] & \tilde{K}(X\cup C_1Y) \arrow[d, "\id"]        \\
			{K(X\cup C_1Y,X)} \arrow[d, "\theta"] \arrow[r, "m^*"]                    & \tilde{K}(X\cup C_1Y) \arrow[d, "(\pi^*)^-1"] \\
			K^{-1}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"]                                             & {K(X,Y)}                                     
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	The bottom square commutes by definition. In the top square, the map $\tilde{\pi}$ denotes the collapsing map $X\cup C_1Y\cup C_2X\to (X\cup C_1Y)\big/X$ which is invertible, as the quotiented out space $C_2X$ is contractible. To see the commutativity in the top square notice how $\tilde{\pi}^*\circ s^*=(s\circ \tilde{\pi})^*$ and the maps $m$ and $s\circ \tilde{\pi}$ coincide and hence the top square commutes. With ${\psi}_2\coloneq \theta \circ (\tilde{\pi}^*)^{-1}$ and ${\psi}_3\coloneq (\pi^*)^{-1}\circ \id$.
	For the left square, a natural choice is the homotopical equivalence given by quotienting the second space of the pair $\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X,X\cup C_1Y\big)$ to get:
	\begin{equation*}
		\Big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X\Big/X\cup C_1Y\Big)\cong (C_2X\big/X)\simeq S(X)\,.
	\end{equation*} We now have all the unknown maps, but
	we run into a problem, since the map $l^*$ induces a map $\lambda$ by the “wrong” inclusions (compared to $i^*$ which is induced by the inclusion $C_2Y\to C_2X$, whereas we have $C_1Y$). This is depicted in the non-commutative diagram:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X,X\cup C_1Y\big)} \arrow[r, "l^*"] \arrow[d,"{\psi}_1"] & \tilde{K}\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X\big) \arrow[d,"{\psi}_2"] \\
			\tilde{K}(C_2X\big/ X \big) \cong \tilde{K}^{-1}(X) \arrow[r, "\lambda"] & \tilde{K}(C_1 Y\big/ Y)\cong \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y)    \\
						{K(C_2X,X)} \arrow[u] \arrow[r, "i^*"]                                        & {K(C_2Y,Y)} \arrow[u] 
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	

	To resolve that problem we introduce the double cone on $Y$ namely $C_1Y\cup C_2Y$, that fits into the commutative diagram where all unnamed maps are the natural ones:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			X\cup C_1Y\cup C_2 X \arrow[rr, Rightarrow] \arrow[dd] &                                                                                               & C_1Y\slash Y \arrow[r, Rightarrow]            & \Sigma Y  \arrow[dd,dotted,"\Sigma T"]\\
			& C_1Y\cup C_2Y \arrow[ru, Rightarrow] \arrow[rd, Rightarrow] \arrow[ld] \arrow[lu, Rightarrow] &                                               &    \\
			C_2X \slash X                                          &                                                                                               & C_2Y\slash Y \arrow[ll,"i"] \arrow[r, Rightarrow] & \Sigma Y
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} The inclusion induced map $i$ is the one, that induces the bottom left map in Diagram \ref{diag:kTheo-proofConnectingHomo}. The double arrows always induce isomorphisms in the $K$-rings. To see that all those maps induce isomorphism we note that we always, with one exception, collapse something contractible. The exception is the map $C_1Y\cup C_2Y \to X \cup C_1Y \cup C_2X$. Here, we get that the induced map is invertible, by noticing the commutative triangle on the top, where two maps induce isomorphisms and thereby also the third. The map $\Sigma T$ is one we want to construct below.  For said construction we formulate the following Lemma: 
	\begin{lemma}\label{lem:kTheo-doubleConeFlip}
		Let $Y$ be a well-pointed space and denote the points of $CY$ and $\Sigma Y$ by $(x,t)$ such that $CY$ has the apex $[\{0\}\times Y]$ and base $\{1\}\times Y\cong Y$. Let 
		\begin{equation*}
			D\coloneq C_1Y \cup C_2Y = \big(C_1Y \sqcup C_2 Y\big) \Big/ \big((x,1)_1\sim (x,1)_2\big)
		\end{equation*} denote the double cone glued along a common base. Now define the maps
		\begin{itemize}
			\item $c_i:D \to C_i Y \big/Y$ the collapse of the complementary cone $C_jY$ $j\neq i$.
			\item $\overline{\pi_1}:C_iY\big/Y \to \Sigma Y\,\quad [(x,t)]\mapsto (x,t)$.
			\item $p_i\coloneq \overline{\pi_1}\circ c_i: D\to \Sigma Y$.
			\item $R:D\to D\,\quad (x,t)_i\mapsto (x,t)_j$ where $j\neq i$, the cone swap.
			\item $\Sigma T: \Sigma Y \to \Sigma Y\,,\quad (x,t)\mapsto (x,1-t)$ the suspension flip.
			\item $\Phi: D\to \Sigma Y\,,\quad (x,t)_1\mapsto (x,1-\frac{t}{2}),\quad (x,t)_2\mapsto (x,\frac{t}{2})$.
		\end{itemize} Then we have that 
		\begin{equation*}
			p_2=p_1\circ R\quad \text{and}\quad \Phi\circ R=\Sigma T\circ \Phi\,.
		\end{equation*}
	\end{lemma}
	\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-doubleConeFlip}]
		The first statement $p_2=p_1\circ R$ is clear, as:
		\begin{align*}
			p_2(x,t)_2&=[(x,t)]=p_1(x,t)_1=p_1\circ R (x,t)_2\,,\quad \text{ and }\\
			p_2(x,t)_1&=[\{1\} \times Y]=p_1(x,t)_2=p_1 \circ R (x,t)_1\,.
		\end{align*}
Similarly, the second statement is also just a calculation:
\begin{align*}
	\Sigma T\circ \Phi (x,t)_1&=\Sigma T (x,1-\frac{t}{2})=(x,\frac{t}{2})=\Phi(x,t)_2=\Phi \circ R (x,t)_1 \\
	\Sigma T\circ \Phi (x,t)_2&=\Sigma T (x,\frac{t}{2})=(x,1-\frac{t}{2})=\Phi(x,t)_1=\Phi \circ R (x,t)_2\,.
\end{align*}
\end{proof}
By the lemma above, we know that $\Sigma T$ makes the diagram commute and by Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-inversesInKGivenByHomotopicallyMethods} we know that 
\begin{equation*}
	(\Sigma T)^* :\tilde{K}(SY)\to \tilde{K}(SY)
\end{equation*} is multiplication by minus one. Hence, the diagram where all maps are either topologically induced or topologically induced and inverted:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			\tilde{K}(X\cup C_1Y\cup C_2 X) \arrow[rd]\arrow[rr,"{\psi}_2"]&& \tilde{K}(C_1Y\slash Y) \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y)  \\
			&\tilde{K}(C_1Y\cup C_2Y) \arrow[dr]&                            &                                                         \\
			\tilde{K}^{-1}(X) \arrow[uu,"\rho"] \arrow[rr,"i^*"]& & \tilde{K}(C_2Y\slash Y) \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y)      \arrow[uu, equal, Rightarrow,"(-1)^*"]                            
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} is commutative. By construction (just composition of collapsing maps) we have that 
	\begin{equation*}
		l^*=\rho \circ {\psi}_1\,,\quad  \text{ and by the diagram } \quad {\psi}_2\circ \rho  =- i^*\,.
	\end{equation*}
	 Which gives us in total:
	 \begin{equation*}
		i^*\circ {\psi}_1=-{\psi}_2 \circ \rho \circ {\psi}_1=-{\psi}_2 \circ l^* 
	 \end{equation*} and thereby the diagram commutes up to sign:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X,X\cup C_1Y\big)} \arrow[r, "l^*"] \arrow[d,"{\psi}_1"] & \tilde{K}\big((X\cup C_1Y)\cup C_2X\big) \arrow[r, "s^*"] \arrow[d,"{\psi}_2"] & \tilde{K}(X\cup C_1Y) \arrow[d,"{\psi}_3"] \\
			\tilde{K}^{-1}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"]                                      & \tilde{K}^{-1}(Y) \arrow[r, "\delta"]                               & {K(X,Y)}                       
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} The exactness in the top row now proves exactness in the bottom.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Triple Sequence] \label{ktheo:cor-tripleSequence}
	
\definecolor{cXY}{RGB}{20,60,220}   % Paar (X,Y)
\definecolor{cXZ}{RGB}{0,0,0}       % Paar (X,Z)
\definecolor{cYZ}{RGB}{200,20,90}   % Paar (Y,Z)
\definecolor{cT}{RGB}{245,140,0}    % Triple-Sequenz

	Let $(X,Y,Z)$ be a topological triple, meaning that all inclusions are cofibrations. Then we can inspect the three long exact sequences from the pairs $\textcolor{cXY}{(X,Y)}$, $\textcolor{cYZ}{(Y,Z)}$ and $\textcolor{cXZ}{(X,Z)}$ that are in the diagram below. The map $\delta_{triple}$ is defined to be the composition $\textcolor{cXY}{\delta}\circ \textcolor{cYZ}{j^*}$, $q*$ is defined to be induced by the inclusion $(X,Z)\to (X,Y)$ and $s^*$ is also given by the inclusion $(Y,Z)\to (X,Z)$. Then, the whole diagram becomes commutative by the naturality of the maps appearing in the long exact sequences. 

\begin{center}

\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&, column sep=1.2em, row sep=2.2em]
 \& K^{-n-1}(X,Y) \arrow[rd, cT, "q^*"] \arrow[rr, bend left, cXY, "j^*"]
 \& \& K^{-n-1}(X) \arrow[rd, cXY, "i^*"] \arrow[rr, bend left, cXZ, "i^*"]
 \& \& K^{-n-1}(Z) \arrow[rd, cXZ, "\delta"] \& \\
K^{-n-2}(Y) \arrow[ru, cXY, "\delta"] \arrow[rd, cYZ, "i^*"']
 \& \& K^{-n-1}(X,Z) \arrow[ru, cXZ, "j^*"] \arrow[rd, cT, "s^*"]
 \& \& K^{-n-1}(Y) \arrow[ru, cYZ, "i^*"] \arrow[rd, cXY, "\delta"']
 \& \& K^{-n}(X,Z) \\
 \& K^{-n-2}(Z) \arrow[ru, cXZ, "\delta"'] \arrow[rr, bend right, cYZ, "\delta"']
 \& \& K^{-n-1}(Y,Z) \arrow[ru, cYZ, "j^*"'] \arrow[rr, bend right, cT, "\delta_{triple}"']
 \& \& K^{-n}(X,Y) \arrow[ru, cT, "q^*"'] \&
\end{tikzcd}%
}
\end{center}
Furthermore, the yellow sequence is a chain complex which we need to verify at three locations:
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item At $K^{-n-1}(X,Z)$ we can see that the composition $\textcolor{cT}{s^*\circ q^*}$ is the pullback of the inclusion $(Y,Z)\to (X,Y)$ which factors through $(Y,Y)$ and thereby the pullback factors through the trivial ring $K^{-n-1}(Y,Y)$.
		\item At $K^{-n-1}(Y,Z)$  we get that the composition is zero as it factors through two consecutive maps in the sequence of $(X,Y)$: $\textcolor{cT}{\delta_{triple}\circ s^*}=\textcolor{cXY}{\delta\circ i^*} \circ j^*=0$.
		\item At $K^{-n}(X,Y)$ we get $\textcolor{cT}{q^*\circ \delta}=0$ as it factors through $\textcolor{cYZ}{i^*\circ j^*}$. 
	\end{enumerate}
	Then, by the braid lemma (which is typical diagram yoga \todo{referenz einfügen: Munkres, p. 148 of Elements of algebraic topology?} ), we know that the \textcolor{cT}{orange} sequence is exact. We call this sequence the \textbf{triple exact sequence} and it is natural with respect to maps of triples, meaning that for two such triples and a map of triples, we get two sequences and an induced mao of sequences.
\end{corollary}


\begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-retractionsInduceSplittingSequences}
	If $(X,Y)$ is a compact pair such that $Y\subseteq X$ is a retract, i.e. the inclusion has a continuous up to homotopy left inverse. Then for all $n\geq 0$, the sequence 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			{K^{-n}(X,Y)} \arrow[r, "j^*"] & K^{-n}(X) \arrow[r, "i^*"] & K^{-n}(Y)
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} is a splitting short exact sequence giving us an isomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		K^{-n}(X)\cong K^{-n}(X,Y)\oplus K^{-n}(Y)\,.
	\end{equation*} This splitting is naturally dependent on the retraction map. 
\end{corollary}
\subsection{New Perspective on the K-Connecting Homomorphism}
\begin{definition}[Homotopy Cofiber]
	For a based map $f:X\to Y$ beween two based spaces $(X,x_0)$ and $(Y,y_0)$ we define the \textbf{homotopy cofiber} $Cf$ to be 
	\begin{equation*}
		Cf\coloneq Y\cup_f CX\,,
	\end{equation*}meaning $Y$ glued to the base of the cone over $X$ via $f$. In other words, 
	\begin{equation*}
		Cf=\big(Y\sqcup I\times X\big)\big/ (x,1)\sim (x_0,1)\text{, and }\,(x,0)\sim f(x)\; \forall x\in X
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition}
Out notation of $(X\cup CY)$ for a pair $(X,Y)$ is the same as the homotopy cofiber $Ci_Y$ with respect to the inclusion $i_Y:Y\to X$.

\begin{remark}
	The inclusion $i:Y\to Cf$ is a cofibration for all $f$.  
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	For a proof compare chapter 4 in \cite{May1999}. The argument here is that $Cf$ is a pushout of $f$ and the cofibration $x\to CX$. 
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
	Let $f:X\to Y$ be a continuous map of pointed spaces, $i:Y\to Cf$ be the inclusion and $\pi:Cf\to Cf/Y \cong \Sigma X$ be the projection composed with the homeomorphism $Cf/Y\to \Sigma X$ that is given by the collaps of $Y$ (respectifely the collapse of $X\times \{0\}$). Then the sequence 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{adjustbox}{width=\textwidth}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			X \arrow[r,"f"]&Y\arrow[r,"i"]&Cf\arrow[r,"\pi"]& \Sigma X \arrow[r,"-\Sigma f"]&\Sigma Y
			\arrow[r,"-\Sigma i"]&\Sigma C f \arrow[r,"-\Sigma \pi "]&\Sigma^2 X \arrow[r,"\Sigma ^2 Y"]&\Sigma ^2 Y \arrow[r]&\cdots
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{adjustbox}
	\end{center} 
	where $-\Sigma f(x,t)=(f(x),1-t)$ is called the \textbf{Puppe Sequence}.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Product Structures}
\begin{corollary}
	Let $X,Y$ be objects in $\TopCPoint$. Then we have the projection maps 
	\begin{align*}
		\pi_X:X\times Y\to X \, , \quad \pi_Y:X\times Y\to Y\, .
	\end{align*} Those maps induce isomorphisms for all $n\geq0:$
	\begin{align*}
		\tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y)\cong \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\wedge Y)\oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(X)\oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(Y)
	\end{align*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
	$X$ is a retract of $X\times Y$ as the projection yields a continuous left inverse of the natural inclusion, and $Y$ is a retract of $(X\times Y)\slash X$ (for the quotient to be natural, we include $X$ at the base point of $Y$). With this we apply corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-retractionsInduceSplittingSequences} twice:
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{array}{lll}
			\tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y)&\cong \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y \slash X) & \oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(X) \\
			&\cong \tilde{K}^{-n}(Y) \oplus \underbrace{\tilde{K}^{-n}((X\times Y \slash X )\slash Y)}_{\tilde{K}^{-n}(X\wedge Y)}&\oplus\tilde{K}^{-n}(X)  
			    \end{array}
	\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:kTheo-splittingAndConnectionWedgeAndProduct}
	Let's inspect the isomorphism induced by the splitting:  Since we apply the splitting twice, we know that the final isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi: \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y) \to \underbrace{\tilde{K}^{-n}((X\times Y \slash X )\slash Y)}_{\tilde{K}^{-n}(X\wedge Y)}\oplus\tilde{K}^{-n}(X) \oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(Y)
	\end{equation*} is of the form 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi=(\,g\circ l\,,\,\tilde{i_Y}^* \circ l\,,\, {i_X}^*\,)\,,
	\end{equation*} where:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $i_Y:Y\to X\times Y$
		\item $\tilde{i}_Y:Y\to (X\times Y\slash X)$.
		\item $j:(X\times Y)\to (X\times Y\slash X)$.
		\item $l:\tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y) \to \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y\slash X)$ is the unique left inverse of $j^*$ such that $j^*\circ l=\id-\pi_X^* \circ i_X^*$.
		\item $g: \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y\big/ X)\to K(X\times y\big/ X,Y)$ is a left inverse of the map $ K(X\times Y\big/ X,Y)\to \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y\big/ X)$.
	\end{itemize}



	We now want to show that $\tilde{i_Y}^* \circ l$ is induced by the inclusion of $Y$ into $X\times Y$.

	We have that $\tilde{i}_Y=j\circ i_Y$ and with this we can deduce:
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{i_Y}^* \circ l={i_Y}^*\circ j^* \circ l ={i_Y}^*\big(\id-\pi_X^* \circ i_X^*\big)=i_Y^*-{i_Y}^*\circ \pi_X^* \circ i_X^*=i_Y^*-(\underbrace{\pi_X\circ i_Y}_{\text{constant}})^*\circ {i_X}^*=i_Y^*
	\end{equation*}
	Hence, in the proof above the isomorphism has the form:
	\begin{align*}
		\Phi: \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y)\to \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\wedge Y)\oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(Y)\oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(X) \\ 
		\Phi=g\oplus i_Y^*\oplus i_X^*
	\end{align*}or said differently, the short exact sequence that splits looks like: 
	\begin{center}
	
		\begin{tikzcd}
			0 \arrow[r] & \tilde{K}^{-n}(X \wedge Y) \arrow[r, "q^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-n}(X\times Y) \arrow[r, "(i_X)^*\oplus (i_Y)^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-n}(X)\oplus \tilde{K}^{-n}(Y) \arrow[r] & 0
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
\end{corollary}




Before we start constructing a cohomology theory, we extend the K groups to be defined not only over the negatives, but over the whole $\Z$ which is done via an extension. The extension relies on periodic behaviour. So-called Bott periodicity.
\begin{definition}[External Product]
	The \textbf{external product } $\mu:K(X)\otimes K(Y)\to K(X\times Y)$ is defined via the two projections $\pi_X:X\times Y\to X$ and $\pi_Y:X\times Y\to Y$ by:
	\begin{align*}
		\mu:K(X)\otimes K(Y)&\to K(X\times Y)\\
		(a\otimes b)&\mapsto (\pi_X)^*(a)\cdot (\pi_Y)^*(b) \, ,
	\end{align*} where $\cdot$ denotes the multiplication in the K-group, which was induced by the tensor product. This $\mu$ is a ring homomorphism, since:
	\begin{align*}
		\mu((a \otimes b)(c \otimes d)) &= \mu(ac \otimes bd) \\
		&= \pi_X^*(ac)\pi_Y^*(bd) \\
		&= \pi_X^*(a)\pi_X^*(c)\pi_Y^*(b)\pi_Y^*(d) \\
		&= \pi_X^*(a)\pi_Y^*(b)\pi_X^*(c)\pi_Y^*(d) \\
		&= \mu(a \otimes b)\mu(c \otimes d)
	\end{align*}
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:kTheo-reducedExternalProduct} 
Let $(X,x_0)$ and $(Y,y_0)$ be well-pointed compact spaces and let
$q\colon X\times Y\to X\wedge Y$ denote the quotient map that appears in the exact sequence of Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-splittingAndConnectionWedgeAndProduct}. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item For $a\in\tilde K(X)$ and $b\in\tilde K(Y)$ we have
	$\mu(a\otimes b)\in q^*\tilde K(X\wedge Y)$. Since $q^*$ is injective,
	this defines the \textbf{reduced external product}
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde\mu\coloneq(q^*)^{-1}\circ
		\restr{\mu}{\tilde K(X)\otimes\tilde K(Y)}\colon
		\tilde K(X)\otimes\tilde K(Y)\to\tilde K(X\wedge Y)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\item With respect to the decompositions
	\begin{align*}
		K(X)\otimes K(Y)
		&\cong \big(\tilde K(X)\otimes\tilde K(Y)\big)\oplus
		\tilde K(X)\oplus\tilde K(Y)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\,,\\
		K(X\times Y)
		&\cong q^*\tilde K(X\wedge Y)\oplus\pi_X^*\tilde K(X)
		\oplus\pi_Y^*\tilde K(Y)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\!\cdot\!1_{X\times Y}
	\end{align*}
	the external product is block diagonal:
	\begin{equation*}
		\mu=(q^*\circ\tilde\mu)\oplus\restr{\pi_X^*}{\tilde K(X)}
		\oplus\restr{\pi_Y^*}{\tilde K(Y)}\oplus\id_{\mathbb{Z}}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	In particular $\mu$ is an isomorphism if and only if $\tilde\mu$ is.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\emph{(i):} Let $i_X\colon X\to X\times Y,\ x\mapsto(x,y_0)$, and
$i_Y$ analogously. Then
\begin{equation*}
	i_X^*\,\mu(a\otimes b)
	= i_X^*\big(\pi_X^*(a)\cdot\pi_Y^*(b)\big)
	= (\underbrace{\pi_X\circ i_X}_{=\,\id_X})^*(a)\cdot
	  (\underbrace{\pi_Y\circ i_X}_{=\,\text{constant}})^*(b)=0\,,
\end{equation*}
since $b\in\ker\big(i_{y_0}^*\colon K(Y)\to K(\{y_0\})\big)$ and the
constant map $\pi_Y\circ i_X$ factors through
$i_{y_0}\colon\{y_0\}\hookrightarrow Y$. Analogously
$i_Y^*\,\mu(a\otimes b)=0$, so by exactness of the sequence from
Corollary~\ref{cor:kTheo-splittingAndConnectionWedgeAndProduct}
\begin{equation*}
	\mu(a\otimes b)\in\ker\big((i_X^*,i_Y^*)\big)
	=\operatorname{im}(q^*)=q^*\tilde K(X\wedge Y)\,.
\end{equation*}
\emph{(ii):} The first decomposition arises by tensoring the splittings
$K(X)\cong\tilde K(X)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\!\cdot\!1_X$, the second by combining
the analogous splitting of $K(X\times Y)$ with the splitting
$\pi_X^*\oplus\pi_Y^*$ of the exact sequence from
Corollary~\ref{cor:kTheo-splittingAndConnectionWedgeAndProduct}.
On the first summand $\mu$ equals $q^*\circ\tilde\mu$ by (i) and the
definition of $\tilde\mu$. Further
$\mu(a\otimes 1_Y)=\pi_X^*(a)\cdot 1_{X\times Y}=\pi_X^*(a)$, and
$\restr{\pi_X^*}{\tilde K(X)}$ is an isomorphism onto
$\pi_X^*\tilde K(X)$. The third summand is
symmetric, and $\mu(1_X\otimes 1_Y)=1_{X\times Y}$. Finally, a direct sum
of homomorphisms is an isomorphism if and only if each block is one, and
$q^*\circ\tilde\mu$ is an isomorphism onto $q^*\tilde K(X\wedge Y)$ if and
only if $\tilde\mu$ is one.
\end{proof}


By example \ref{ex:kTheo-LineBundlesSatlquadequalslPlusl} we know that for any line bundle $l$, $[l\otimes l \oplus \underline{1}]=[l]^2+[\underline{1}]=[l]+[l]$ which is equivalent to $([l]-1)^2=[l^2]-[l]+[l]+[\underline{1}]=0$. Now $\{[\underline{1}],[l]\}$ is an additive basis of $\Z\big/([l]^2-[\underline{1}])$. The mapping that is defined on a basis of the tensor product as follows
	\begin{align*}
		\Big(\Z([l])\Big/ ([l]^2-[\underline{1}])\Big) \otimes K(X) &\to K(S^2 \times X)\\
		[\underline{1}] \otimes [\xi]  &\mapsto \mu( [\underline{1}],[\xi])\\
		 [l] \otimes [\xi] &\mapsto \mu( [l], [\xi])
	\end{align*} is well defined. 

\begin{definition}
	We denote the external product of two elements by
	\begin{equation*}
		\zeta \boxtimes \xi \coloneq \tilde{\mu}(\zeta \otimes \xi)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}[Naturality]
	Let $f:X\to X'$ and $g:Y\to Y'$ be pointed maps, and $a\in \tilde{K}(X')$, $b \in \tilde{K}(Y')$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		f^*(a)\boxtimes g^*(b)=(f \wedge g)^*(a \boxtimes b)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
	We start unreduced. Let $f:X\to X'$ and $g:Y\to Y'$ be continuous maps. then $\pi_{X'}\circ (f\times g)=f\circ \pi_X$ and similar $\pi_{Y'}\circ (f\times g)=g\circ \pi_{Y}$. Using that $(f\times g)^*$ is a ring homomorphism we calculate
	\begin{align*}
		(f\times g)^*\mu(a \otimes b)
		&=(f\times g)^*\Big((\pi_{X'})^*(a)\cdot (\pi_{Y'})^*(b)\Big)\\
		&=\big((f\times g)^*(\pi_{X'})^*(a)\big)\cdot \big((f\times g)^*(\pi_{Y'})^*(b)\big)\\
		&=\big(\pi_X^*\circ f^*(a)\big)\cdot \big(\pi_Y^*\circ g^*(b)\big)\\
		&=\mu(f^*(a)\otimes g^*(b))\,.
	\end{align*} Now assume that $f$ and $g$ are pointed and denote the projections $q':X'\times Y' \to X' \wedge Y'$ and $q:X\times Y \to X\wedge Y$. Then 
	$q'\circ (f\times g)=(f\wedge g )\circ q$ by definition of the wedge product of maps. But then we can calculate
	\begin{equation*}
		q^*(f\wedge g)^*(a\boxtimes b)=(f\times g)^*{q'}^*(a\boxtimes b)=(f\times g)^* \mu(a\otimes b)=\mu(f^*a\otimes g^*b)=q^*(f^*a\boxtimes g^*b) \,.
	\end{equation*}finally since $q^*$ is injective we are done.
\end{proof}


\begin{proposition}[Associativity of the Reduced External Product] \label{prop:kTheo-associativityExternalProduct}
	Let $A,B,C$ be pointed spaces and $a\in \rK(A)$,
	 $b\in \rK(B)$ and 
	 $c\in \rK(C)$. 
	Let $\psi: A\wedge (B\wedge C)\to (A\wedge B)\wedge C$ be the homeomorphism from Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-SmaschIsAssociative}. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\psi^*((a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c)=a\boxtimes (b\boxtimes c) \,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
	Write $q_{X,Y}\colon X\times Y\to X\wedge Y$ for the quotient maps and
	$\pi_A,\pi_B,\pi_C$ for the projections of $A\times B\times C$. Let
	\begin{equation*}
		Q\coloneq q_{A\wedge B,C}\circ (q_{A,B}\times \id_C)\colon A\times B\times C\to (A\wedge B)\wedge C
	\end{equation*}
	and analogously $Q'\coloneq q_{A,B\wedge C}\circ(\id_A\times q_{B,C})$. Both are
	quotient maps, and by the construction of $\psi$ in Lemma
	\ref{lem:kTheo-SmaschIsAssociative} we have
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:kTheo-assocQuotients}
		\psi\circ Q'=Q\,,
	\end{equation}
	since both sides are induced by the identity on $A\times B\times C$.
	Furthermore $Q^*$ is injective, being the composite of the injective maps
	$q_{A\wedge B,C}^*$ and $(q_{A,B}\times\id_C)^*$.

	We first compute $Q^*$ of the left hand side. By definition of $\boxtimes$ we
	have the equialities 
	\begin{align}
		q_{A\wedge B,C}^{*}\bigl((a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c\bigr)
	\,&=\,\mu\bigl((a\boxtimes b)\otimes c\bigr)\in \tilde{K}((A\wedge B)\times C)\label{eq:kTheo-assozExPro1}\\
		\pi_C\circ (q_{A,B}\times \id_C)\,&=\,\pi_C\,:(A\times B )\times C \to C \label{eq:kTheo-assozExPro2}\\
\pi_{A\wedge B}\circ (q_{A,B}\times \id_C)\,&=\,q_{A,B}\circ \pi_{A\times B}\,:(A\times B )\times C \to C \label{eq:kTheo-assozExPro3}
	\end{align} hence
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{array}{llr}
			Q^{*}\bigl((a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c\bigr)
		&=(q_{A,B}\times\id_C)^{*}\Bigl(\pi_{A\wedge B}^{*}(a\boxtimes b)\cdot \pi_C^{*}(c)\Bigr)& \text{using (\ref{eq:kTheo-assozExPro1})}\\
		&=(q_{A,B}\times\id_C)^{*}\pi_{A\wedge B}^{*}(a\boxtimes b)\cdot \pi_C^{*}(c)& \text{using (\ref{eq:kTheo-assozExPro2}) and (\ref{eq:kTheo-assozExPro3})}\\
		&=(q_{A,B}\times\id_C)^{*}\big(\pi_A^*(a)\cdot \pi_B^*(b)\big)\cdot \pi_C^{*}(c)&\text{ similar to (\ref{eq:kTheo-assozExPro1})}\\
		&=(\pi_A^{*}(a)\cdot\pi_B^{*}(b))\cdot\pi_C^{*}(c)& \pi_A\circ \pi_{A\times B}=\pi_A \text{ and for $\pi_B$}
		\end{array}
	\end{equation*}
	
		The same computation on the other side gives
	\begin{equation*}
		Q'^{*}\bigl(a\boxtimes(b\boxtimes c)\bigr)=\pi_A^{*}(a)\cdot\pi_B^{*}(b)\cdot\pi_C^{*}(c)\,,
	\end{equation*}
	using associativity of the multiplication in $K(A\times B\times C)$. Combining
	this with \eqref{eq:kTheo-assocQuotients},
	\begin{equation*}
		Q'^{*}\psi^{*}\bigl((a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c\bigr)
		=Q^{*}\bigl((a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c\bigr)
		=Q'^{*}\bigl(a\boxtimes(b\boxtimes c)\bigr)\,,
	\end{equation*}
	and by corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-splittingAndConnectionWedgeAndProduct} we know that $q_{A,B}$ is injective. Hence, $Q'^{*}$ is injective, which proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Lower External Product] \label{def:kTheo-lowerExternalProduct}

	Let $a\in \rK^{-n}(X)=\rK(S^n\wedge X)$ and $b\in \rK^{-m}(Y)=\rK(S^m\wedge Y)$. 
	Then we define $a\boxtimes_{n,m}b\in \rK^{-n-m}(X\wedge Y)$ in three steps:
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item First we apply the classical reduced external product:
		\begin{equation*}
			a\boxtimes b \,\in \,\rK(S^n\wedge X\wedge S^m\wedge Y)
		\end{equation*}
		\item Let $\tau: S^n\wedge X\wedge S^m\wedge Y \to S^n\wedge  S^m\wedge X \wedge Y$ be the map that swaps $S^m$ and $X$. Then:
			\begin{equation*}
				\tau^*(a\boxtimes b)\,\in\,\rK(S^n\wedge  S^m\wedge X\wedge Y)
			\end{equation*}
		\item Lastly we apply $\Upsilon_{n,m}^{-1}\wedge\id_{X\wedge Y}$ where $\Upsilon:S^n\wedge S^m\to S^{n+m}$ is the map from Definition \ref{def:ktheo-wedgeOfShoperesIsSphere}, to get:
			\begin{equation*}
				a\boxtimes_{n,m}b\,\coloneq\, \big(\Upsilon_{n,m}^{-1}\wedge\id_{X\wedge Y}\big)^*\circ  \tau^* \,(a\boxtimes b) \,\in \rK^{-(n+m)}(X\wedge Y)
			\end{equation*}
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
	The lower external product is associative , meaning that for $a\in \rK^{-k}(X),\,b\in \rK^{-l}(Y)\,,c\in \rK^{-m}(Z)$ we have
	\begin{equation*}
		(a\boxtimes_{k,l}b)\boxtimes_{k+l,m}c\,=\,a\boxtimes_{l,k+m}(b\boxtimes_{k,m}c)\,,
	\end{equation*}
	and natural, meaning that for $f:X\to X'$ and $g:Y\to Y'$
	\begin{equation*}
		f^*(a)\boxtimes_{n,m}g^*(b)\,=\, (f\wedge g)^*(a \boxtimes_{n,m} b)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	We start with the associativity: Let $a\in \rK^{-k}(X),\,b\in \rK^{-l}(Y)\,,c\in \rK^{-m}(Z)$. Let
	\begin{equation*}
		\Phi_{k,l}\coloneq \tau\circ \left(\Upsilon^{-1}_{k,l}\wedge \id_{X\wedge Y}\right):(S^{k+l}\wedge X\wedge Y)\to (S^k\wedge X \wedge S^l \wedge Y)\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Then we get the shorter form of the lower external product $a\boxtimes_{k,l}b=\Phi_{k,l}^*(a\boxtimes b)$, helping us to calculate:
\begin{align*}
	(a\boxtimes_{k,l}b)\boxtimes_{k+l,m}c 
		&=\Phi_{k+l,m}^*\big(\Phi_{k,l}^*(a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes (\id_{S^m\wedge Z})^*c\big)\\
		&=\big(\underbrace{(\Phi_{k,l}\wedge \id_{S^m\wedge Z})\circ \Phi_{k+l,m}}_{\eqcolon L}\big)^*((a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c)\,.
\end{align*} The last equiality is the naturality of the reduced external product.
Similarly, we rewrite the other order:
\begin{equation*}
	a\boxtimes_{k,l+m}(b\boxtimes_{l,m}c)=\big(\underbrace{(\id_{S^k\wedge X}\wedge \Phi_{l,m})\circ \Phi_{k,l+m}}_{\eqcolon R}\big)^*(a\boxtimes (b\boxtimes c))\,.
\end{equation*}
By the associativity of the reduced external product we know that $(a\boxtimes b)\boxtimes c=AS^*(a\boxtimes (b\boxtimes c))$. 
So what we need now is to show that $AS\circ L=R$ as maps 
\begin{equation*}
	S^{k+l+m}\wedge X\wedge Y \wedge Z\to S^k\wedge X\wedge S^l \wedge Y\wedge S^m\wedge Z
\end{equation*} modulo the identification $(X\wedge X)\wedge Z=X\wedge (Y\wedge Z)$. This can be done pointwise by calculating the image and using the associativity of $\Upsilon$ from Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-AssoziativityOfUspilon}. Both sides of the equation map a point $[p,x,y,z]$ that is not the basepoint with $\pi_S(p)=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in \R^k\times \R^l\times \R^m$ to
\begin{equation*}
	[\pi^{-1}_S (\alpha),x,\pi_S^{-1}(\beta),y,\pi_S^{-1}(\gamma),z]\,.
\end{equation*}

Finally, the naturality is shorter and follows from the naturality of $\boxtimes$ and $\Phi_{k,l}$, which in turn follows from the naturality of $\Xi$, together with the naturalitys of the swapping $\tau$.
\end{proof}


\begin{remark}\label{rem:kTheo-neutralELementExternalProduct}
	Let $\zeta$ be the vector bundle  over $S^0=\{-1,+1\}$ that is $\underline{1}$ over $-1$ and $\underline{0}$ over $+1$. Then $[\xi]$ is a generator of $\tilde{K}(S^0)$, as any bundle that is in the cernal of the inclusion of $+1$ is $\underline{0}$ over $+1$, and trivial over $\{-1\}$. Let $\xi\in \tilde{K}(Y)$ be represented by $\xi=[\xi_1]-[\xi_2]$ such that $\xi_i$ are vector bundles $(i=1,2)$ over a pointed topological space $Y$. Then for $i=1,2$:
	\begin{equation*}
		[\zeta]\boxtimes [\xi_i]=(q^*)^{-1}(\mu([\zeta]\otimes [\xi_i]))=(q^*)^{-1}\pi_{S^0}^*(\zeta)\cdot \pi_Y^*([\xi_i])=(q^*)^{-1}([\pi_{S^0}^*(\zeta)\otimes  \pi_Y^*(\xi_i]))
	\end{equation*}
	The bundle $\pi_{S^0}^*(\zeta)$ is $\underline{1}$ over $\{-1\}\times Y$ and $\underline{0}$ over $\{1\}\times Y$. $\pi_Y^*(\xi_i]$ on the otherhand is the pullback of $\xi_i$ via the homeomorphism $h_-:\{- 1\}\times Y \to Y$ over $\{-1\}\times Y$ and similar over $\{+1\}\times Y$. Hence, the tensor product $\pi_{S^0}^*(\zeta)\otimes  \pi_Y^*(\xi_i]$ is given by $h_-^*(Y)$ over $$\{-1\}\times Y$$ and $\underline{0}$ over $\{+1\}\times Y$. This bundles equivalence class in $\tilde{K}$ is given by the bundle $h_-^*$ viewed over the pointed $S^0\wedge Y=\{-1\}\times Y $ with basepoint $[(-1,1)]$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[The Fundamental Product Theorem]\label{thm:kTheo-fundamentalProductTheorem}
	For any compact $X$, and the tautological line bundle $H$ on $\C\PP^1\cong S^2$, the external product map 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Big(\Z([H])\Big/ ([H]-[\underline{1}])^2\Big) \otimes K(X) \to K(S^2 \times X)
	\end{equation*} is an isomorphism of rings. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	The proof is omitted here. There are elementary proofs given in Chapter 2.1 \cite{hatcher2002algebraic} or Chapter 2.3 \cite{atiyah1989k}. 
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}[The Easy Spaces]\ref{cor:kTheo-PointAndEasySphere}
	We already know that $\Vect(pt)\cong\N$ as a semiring, where $n\mapsto [\underline{n}]$. Hence, as a Ring $K(\{pt\})\cong \Z$. Since pointing the unpouinted space $\{pt\}$ results in $S^0$ we know by Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-K(X)istK(X,nix)} that $\K^0(S^0)\cong \Z$ and generated by the trivial bundle of degree one over $\{-1\}$ and degree $0$ over $\{+1\}.$ We call this generator $\bottg_0$.
	With the fundamental product Theorem, this tells us, that $K(S^{2})\cong \Big(\Z([H])\Big/ ([H]-[\underline{1}])^2\Big)$ as a ring. Hence, we are isomorphic to the ring $\Z\times \Z$ with componentwise multiplication.  $K(S^2)$ is generated by $\{[\underline{1}],[H]\}$. Furthermore, together with the map 
	\begin{equation*}
		\rank: \Big(\Z([H])\Big/ ([H]-[\underline{1}])^2\Big)\to \Z
	\end{equation*} that maps the generators to their rank, and 
	\begin{equation*}
		\nu: \Z \to K(pt);\,,\quad n \mapsto [\underline{n}]
	\end{equation*} we get the commutative diagram:
	\begin{center}
	\begin{tikzcd}
		{\Z([H])\Big/ ([H]^2-[\underline{1}]) }\arrow[r,"\rank"] \arrow[d] & \Z\arrow[d,"\nu"]\\
 		K(S^2) \arrow[r,"i_{pt}^*"] & K(pt) 	
	\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	By the commutativity, we get an isomorphism induced by the map from the product theorem: 
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(S^2)=\ker(i_{pt}^*) \cong \ker(\rank)=\mathrm{span}_{\Z}([H]-[\underline{1}])
	\end{equation*}
	Hence the Abelian group $\tilde{K}(S^2)\cong \Z$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{definition}\label{def:kTheo-BottGenerator}
	We call the generator $([H]-[\underline{1}])$ that generates $\tilde{K}(S^2)$ the \textbf{Bott generator} denoted by $\bottg$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Bott Periodicity] \label{thm:Ktheo-BottPeriodicity}
	Let $X$ be a well-pointed compact space.
	The isomorphism from the fundamental product theorem applied in its reduced form gives a natural isomorphism:
	\begin{equation*}
		\bottmap: {K}^{-n}(X)\to {K}^{-n}(S^2\wedge X)\cong {K}^{-n-2}( X)
	\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof} We start with $n=0$.
	By Proposition \ref{prop:kTheo-reducedExternalProduct} we know that the reduced external product applied to the spaces $\tilde{K}(S^2)\cong \mathrm{span}_{\Z}([H]-[\underline{1}]) $ and $\tilde{K}(X)$ is an isomorphism, as applying the non-reduced external product gives an isomorphism by the fundamental product theorem. Hence, we have that
	\begin{equation*}
		\Z([H]-[\underline{1}])\otimes \tilde{K}(X)\to {K}^{-2}(X)
	\end{equation*} is an isomorphism. And since $\tilde{K}(X)\cong \Z(\bottg)\otimes \tilde{K}(X)$ via $[\xi]\mapsto (\bottg) \otimes [\xi]$ we have the isomorphism
	\begin{equation*}
		\bottmap: {K}^{-2}(X) \otimes \tilde{K}(X) \,,\quad [\xi] \mapsto \tilde{\mu}(\bottg \otimes [\xi])\,.
	\end{equation*}Naturality follows from $\mu$ being natural, $q^*$ being natural (and thereby its inverse) and $\tilde{\mu}$ being the composition of both of them. Furthermore, for \todo{natürlich im zweiten faktor mit festem ersten} Replacing $X$ with $S^nX$ proves the lower degree.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}[Spheres] \label{cor:kTheo-kGroupsOfTheSpheres}
	Let $S^n$ be pointed spheres. We already saw, that $K^0(S^2)=\tilde{K}(S^2)\cong \Z$. 
	Hence, the Bott periodicity together with the homotopy equivalence $S^2\wedge S^{2{k-1}}\simeq S^{2k}$ lets us deduce that $K^0(S^2)\cong K^0(S^4)\cong \cdots \cong K^0(S^{2k})\cong \Z$, and a generator of $ K^0(S^{2k})$ is given by $\underbrace{\beta\boxtimes \cdots \boxtimes  \beta}_{k\text{-times}}$. 

	On the other hand we can inspect $\tilde{K}(S^1)$. By Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-reducedSuspendedKGroupHomotopically} we know that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde{K}(S^1)\cong \tilde{K}(\Sigma\{pt\})\cong[\{pt\},U]=\pi_0(U)\,.
	\end{equation*} However $U$ is pathconnected and thereby the reduced K group of the one sphere is trivial. The path connectedness of $U$ follows from all $U(n)$ being path connected which again can be deduced from $\GL_n(\C)$ being path connected and contracting onto $U(n)$. Then, by the Bott periodicity we can conclude that $\tilde{K}(S^{2k+1})=0$ for all $k\in \N$.
\end{corollary}



\begin{comment}

With this we have the exact sequence of six terms  (in the top row we identified $K^0\equiv K^{-2}$ and the vertical map on the left is $\beta \circ \delta$):
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			K^0(X,Y) \arrow[r] & K^0(X) \arrow[r] & K^0(Y)\arrow[d]\\
			K^1(Y)\arrow[u] & K^1(X) \arrow[l]&K^1(X,Y)\arrow[l]
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} We now define the whole long exact sequence to be the one from Theorem \ref{thm:kTheo-longExactSequence} for negative indices and the sequence of six terms above, for positive indices. With this we have gained a cohomology theory over all whole numbers.\todo{sauberer definieren}
\end{definition}
\end{comment}

\begin{definition}[Convention]\label{def:ktheo-higherBottGenerators}
    Let $\bottg_0\in \tilde{K}^{0}(S^0)$ be the canonical generator from Corollary \ref{cor:kTheo-PointAndEasySphere},
    $\bottg_1\in \tilde{K}^{-1}(S^1)$ be the Bott generator of $\tilde{K}(S^2)$,
    viewed in $\tilde{K}^{-1}(S^1)=\tilde{K}^{0}(S^1\wedge S^1)$ via Definition
    \ref{def:ktheo-wedgeOfShoperesIsSphere}. Writing
    \begin{equation*}
        \Upsilon_{n,m}\coloneq \left(\overline{\pi_S^{n+m}}\right)^{-1}\circ
        \Xi_{\R^n,\R^m}^{-1}\circ\left(\overline{\pi_S^{n}}\wedge\overline{\pi_S^{m}}\right)
        \;:\;S^n\wedge S^m\to S^{n+m}
    \end{equation*}
    for the homeomorphism of Definition \ref{def:ktheo-wedgeOfShoperesIsSphere},
    we define inductively
	\begin{equation*}
		\beta_n\coloneq (\id_{S^n}\wedge \Upsilon^{-1}_{1,n-1})\beta_1\boxtimes_{1,n-1}\beta_{n-1}\in \tilde{K}^{-n}(S^n)
	\end{equation*}
 Or alternatively, using the associativity of $\Upsilon$ from Remark \ref{rem:kTheo-AssoziativityOfUspilon} we can rewrite this to be 
	\begin{equation*}
		\bottg_n=\Upsilon^n(\underbrace{\beta_1\boxtimes \cdots \boxtimes \beta_1}_{n\text{-times}}),
	\end{equation*} where $\Upsilon^n:\underbrace{S^2\wedge \cdots \wedge S^2}_{n\text{-times}}\to S^{2n}$ denotes iterative application of $S^1\wedge S^l\to S^{1+l}$ that keeps the order of the coordinates.

    By Theorem \ref{thm:Ktheo-BottPeriodicity}, the external product
    with $\bottg_1$ is an isomorphism, so each $\bottg_n$ is a generator.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:ktheo-bottGeneratorsAreMultiplicative}
    For all $n,m\geq 0$ we have
    $\Upsilon_{n,m}^{*}\left(\bottg_{n+m}\right)=\bottg_n\boxtimes \bottg_m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Set $b_i\coloneq \left(\overline{\pi_S^{i}}\right)^{*}(\bottg_i)\in\rK^{-i}\left(\overline{\R^{i}}\right)$.

	\emph{Reduction.} Since $\overline{\pi_S^{n}}\wedge\overline{\pi_S^{m}}$ is a
	homeomorphism, naturality of $\boxtimes$ gives
	$\left(\overline{\pi_S^{n}}\wedge\overline{\pi_S^{m}}\right)^{*}
	\left(\bottg_n\boxtimes\bottg_m\right)=b_n\boxtimes b_m$. Applying
	$\left(\overline{\pi_S^{n}}\wedge\overline{\pi_S^{m}}\right)^{*}$ to the claim and
	using $\left(\overline{\pi_S^{n}}\wedge\overline{\pi_S^{m}}\right)^{*}\Upsilon_{n,m}^{*}
	=\left(\Upsilon_{n,m}\circ\left(\overline{\pi_S^{n}}\wedge\overline{\pi_S^{m}}\right)\right)^{*}
	=\left(\left(\overline{\pi_S^{n+m}}\right)^{-1}\circ\Xi_{n,m}^{-1}\right)^{*}$, the
	claim becomes
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:kTheo-multInR}
		\Xi_{n,m}^{*}\left(b_n\boxtimes b_m\right)=b_{n+m}\,,
	\end{equation}
	and by definition $b_n=\Xi_{1,n-1}^{*}\left(b_1\boxtimes b_{n-1}\right)$.

	\emph{Base case $n=0$.} Here $\Xi_{0,m}$ is the canonical homeomorphism
	$\overline{\R^{m}}\to\overline{\R^{0}}\wedge\overline{\R^{m}}$ and
	\eqref{eq:kTheo-multInR} is Remark
	\ref{rem:kTheo-neutralELementExternalProduct}.

	\emph{Induction step.} By definition of $\psi$ in Lemma
	\ref{lem:kTheo-SmaschIsAssociative} the diagram above commutes, i.e.
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:kTheo-coherence}
		\left(\Xi_{1,n-1}\wedge\id\right)\circ\Xi_{n,m}
		=\psi\circ\left(\id\wedge\Xi_{n-1,m}\right)\circ\Xi_{1,(n-1)+m}\,.
	\end{equation}
	Now
	\begin{equation*}
    \begin{array}{llr}
        \Xi_{n,m}^{*}\left(b_n\boxtimes b_m\right)
        &=\Xi_{n,m}^{*}\left(\Xi_{1,n-1}^{*}\left(b_1\boxtimes b_{n-1}\right)\boxtimes\id^{*}(b_m)\right)
        & \text{definition of } b_n\\[2pt]
        &=\Xi_{n,m}^{*}\left(\Xi_{1,n-1}\wedge\id\right)^{*}\left(\left(b_1\boxtimes b_{n-1}\right)\boxtimes b_m\right)
        & \text{naturality of }\boxtimes\\[2pt]
        &=\left(\left(\Xi_{1,n-1}\wedge\id\right)\circ\Xi_{n,m}\right)^{*}\left(\left(b_1\boxtimes b_{n-1}\right)\boxtimes b_m\right)
        & \text{functoriality}\\[2pt]
        &=\Xi_{1,(n-1)+m}^{*}\left(\id\wedge\Xi_{n-1,m}\right)^{*}\psi^{*}\left(\left(b_1\boxtimes b_{n-1}\right)\boxtimes b_m\right)
        & \text{using (\ref{eq:kTheo-coherence})}\\[2pt]
        &=\Xi_{1,(n-1)+m}^{*}\left(\id\wedge\Xi_{n-1,m}\right)^{*}\left(b_1\boxtimes\left(b_{n-1}\boxtimes b_m\right)\right)
        & \text{Proposition \ref{prop:kTheo-associativityExternalProduct}}\\[2pt]
        &=\Xi_{1,(n-1)+m}^{*}\left(\id^{*}(b_1)\boxtimes\Xi_{n-1,m}^{*}\left(b_{n-1}\boxtimes b_m\right)\right)
        & \text{naturality of }\boxtimes\\[2pt]
        &=\Xi_{1,(n-1)+m}^{*}\left(b_1\boxtimes b_{(n-1)+m}\right)
        & \text{induction hypothesis}\\[2pt]
        &=b_{n+m}
        & \text{definition of } b_{n+m}
    \end{array}
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

%%%%%% ===== orientations.tex ===== %%%%%%

\section{Orientations}
The idea of Morse homology is now to find an invariant under manipulations of the Morse--Smale function. Obviously neither the number of critical points nor the number of flow lines stays invariant. However certain linear combinations stay invariant, when one establishes a sophisticated way to count flow lines. For this count we want to introduce signs to the flow lines between critical points with index one apart where the signs rely on orientations of over the flow lines.
\subsection{Oriented Vector Spaces and Bundles}




\begin{definition}[Orientation]
	Let $V$ be a finite dimensional $\R$ vector space and $\det(V)\coloneq \Lambda^{\dim(V)}$ the maximal exterior algebra (or Grassmann algebra). By the principle of permanence we define $\Lambda^0(V)=\R$. An orientation of $V$ is a choice of one of the two connected components of $\det(V)\setminus \{0\}$. we call the touple $(V,[\mathfrak{o}])$ where $\mathfrak{0}$ is a non-zero element of $\det(V)$ and hence $[\mathfrak{o}]$ is an orientation, an \textbf{oriented vector space}. Often we will just write $V$ oriented, and omitt the orientation.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}[Convention]
    If we use $\R^n$ as an oriented vector space we fix $e\coloneq e_1\wedge \cdots \wedge e_n$ to induce the orientation.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	Classically, an orientation of a vector space $V$ of dimension $m$ is given by an ordered basis $\mathfrak{b}=(b_1,\dots b_m)$ of $V$ and hence the product $b_1\wedge \dots \wedge b_m$ is an element of the maximal exterior product $\det(V)=\Lambda^mV$. Two ordered bases define different elements in $\det(V)$ and their difference is given by the determinant of the base change matrix. (This is just a calculation using multilinearity and the alternating property). Hence a choice of equivalence class of bases modulo base change matrix of positive determinant is the same as a choice of one connected component of $\det(V)\setminus \{0\}$.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}[Determinant of an endomorphism]
	Let $f:V\to V$ be an endomorphism. Then we define 
	\begin{align}
		\Lambda^{\dim(V)}(f):\det(V)&\to \det(V)\\
v_1\wedge \dots \wedge v_{\dim(V)}&\mapsto f(v_1)\wedge \dots \wedge f(v_{\dim(V)})\,.
	\end{align} Since $\dim(\det(V))=1$, the function $\Lambda^{\dim(V)}(f)$ is given by a scaler multiplication with a nonzero element $\lambda \in \R$. We define the determinant $\det(f)\coloneq \lambda$  to be said scalar. Obviously, if $\det(V)$ is positive, then $f$ is orientation preserving in the sence, that $f$ applied to the ordered basis gives again an ordered basis that is compatible.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:orient-calculateDeterminatViaMatrixDeterminant}
	From linear algebra we know, that we can calculate $\det(f)=\det(M^{\mathfrak{b}}_\mathfrak{b}(f))$, where $\mathfrak{b}$ is an ordered basis of $V$. Since the determinant of a matrix commutes with taking products, we have that the determinant is invariant under conjugation of linear endomorphisms.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition} \label{def:oeient-SignOfAAbstractLinearMap}
    Let $(V,[\mathfrak{o}])$ and $(W,[\mathfrak{p}])$ be oriented vector spaces. We call a linear isomorphism $f:V\to W$ between two orineted vector spaces \textbf{orientation preserving}, if  $[\Lambda^{\dim(V)}(f)(\mathfrak{o})]=[\mathfrak{v}]$. Let $o$ and $p$ denote the basis induced from $\mathfrak{o}$ resp. $\mathfrak{p}$. Then by the Remark \ref{rem:orient-calculateDeterminatViaMatrixDeterminant} the map $f$ is orientation preserving, if and only if $\det(M^\mathfrak{o}_\mathfrak{p}(f))$ is positive. We define the sing of $f$ (and sometimes even the seign of $\det(f)$) to be the sign of $\det(M^\mathfrak{o}_\mathfrak{p}(f))$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}[Orientation and Exact Sequences]\todo{FIX}
A short exact sequence of vector spaces 
\begin{center}

	\begin{tikzcd}
		0 \arrow[r] & A \arrow[r, "f"] & B \arrow[r, "g"] & C \arrow[r] & 0
	\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
induces a canonical isomorphism 
	\begin{align*}
		 \det(A)\otimes \det(C)& \to \det(B)\\
		 (a_1\wedge \dots\wedge a_l) \otimes (c_1\wedge \dots \wedge c_k) &\mapsto (f(a_1)\wedge \dots f(a_l)\wedge \tilde{c_1}\wedge \dots \wedge \tilde{c_k})\, ,
	\end{align*} where $\tilde{c_j}\in g^{-1}(c_j)$ . The well-definedness of this map (invariance under chosen lift of $c$) can be calculated, since two different choices differ in an element in the kernel of $g$ which equals the image of $f$ and hence the different choices vanish in the wedge product. By this isomorphism we get an orientation induced in the middle from the outside. Notice the convention, that the basis from $A$ is placed in front of the basis from $C$. 
	Notice how the definition is consistent with the edge cases of trivial vector spaces $A$ or $C$. If $A=0$ we get an isomorphism $B\cong C$ and a basis from $C$ induced to $B$, that is either the induced one from $g$, or exactly the opposite one, if the trivial vector space $A$ is negatively oriented.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	Let 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
		0 \arrow[r] & A \arrow[r, "f"] & B \arrow[r, "g"] & C \arrow[r] & 0
	\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}

be a short exact sequence of vector spaces and $\mathfrak{a}$ be a basis of $A$, $\mathfrak{c}$ be a basis of $C$ and $\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}$ be a lift of $\mathfrak{c}$ via $g$. Replacing the basis $\mathfrak{a}$ with $P\mathfrak{a}$ and $\mathfrak{c}$ with $Q\mathfrak{c}$ where $P,Q$ are invertible matrices, gives us a new induced basis on $B$, which differs from the original one by a matrix $\begin{pmatrix}
	P&*\\
	0&Q
\end{pmatrix}$, where $*$ depends on the chosen lift. Hence the corresponding elements in $\det(B)$ differ by the product $\det(P)\det(Q)$. 
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}\label{def:orient-orientedVectorBundle}
	For a rank $r$ vector bundle $E\to X$ we set $\det(E)\coloneq \Lambda^r(E)$. An orientation is an equivalence class of a non-vanishing section into the determinant bundle, where two sections are equivalent if they differ by multiplication of a positive scalar valued function. $E$ is orientable if such a section exists, i.e. if $\det(E)$ is trivial. An orientation of a bundle restricts to an orientation in each fibre.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:morse-OrientationsInAPointInduceOrientationsOfBundle}
	Let $E\to X$ be an orientable vector bundle and $X$ be connected. For each $x\in X$ we get the bijection induced from the restriction to the fibre:
	\begin{equation*}
		\{\text{Orientation on }E\} \to \{\text{Orientation on } E_x\}\,.
	\end{equation*} Hence, an orientation in one fibre can introduce an orientation in every other fibre.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Two orientations over $E$ are given as nowhere-vanishing sections $s,s'$. The quotient $x\mapsto \frac{s}{s'}$ is continuous and its image is contained in $\R\setminus \{0\}$. Hence the sign of the quotient is a continuous function into $\{-1,1\}$. Since $X$ is connected we conclude that the sign of said quotient is a constant function. Thereby, if the sections induce the same orientation, they differ by multiplication of the positive number $\frac{s(x)}{s'(x)}$ and then the sign of $\frac{s}{s'}$ is globally one. The surjectivity is clear by mapping the sections $s$ and $-s$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    Let $\pi: E\to X$ and $\pi':E'\to X'$ be two oriented vector bundles, $f:X\to X'$ continouos, and $\Phi:E\to E'$ a morphism of vector bundles over $f$ such that for all $x$, $\Phi_x$ is an isomorphism of vector bundles. Then we call $\Phi$ orientation preserving, if and only if $\Phi_x:E_x\to E'_{f(x)}$ is an orientation preserving map between oriented vector spaces, that inherit their orientation from the bundles via Lemma \ref{def:orient-orientedVectorBundle} for all $x\in X$. 
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}[Equivalent Definition]\label{def:orient-orientedMorphismOfBundlesEquivalentDef}
    Let $s$ and $t$ be non-vanishing sections into the determinant bundle that represent orientations of the oriented vector bundles $\pi: E\to X$ and $\pi':E'\to X'$, $f:X\to Y$ be continuous and $\Phi: E\to E'$ be a bundle morphism over $f$. Then for all $x\in X$ there exists a $\lambda(x)\in \R\setminus \{0\}$ such that $\Lambda^{\rank(E)}(\Phi)(s(x))=\lambda(x)t(f(x))$. The map $x\mapsto \lambda(x)$ is continouos, as $\lambda=\frac{\Lambda^{\rank(E)}(\Phi)\circ s}{t\circ f}$ is continouos (one can check this in a local frame of $\det(E')$) and non-vanishing. Thereby its sign is locally constand. Hence, if $X$ is connected, then $\Phi$ is oriented, if for \textbf{any} $x\in X$ $\Phi_x$ is an orientation preserving map between oriented vector spaces, that inherit their orientation from the bundles via Lemma \ref{def:orient-orientedVectorBundle}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proposition}
	Let 
	\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
        0\arrow[r] & E'\arrow[r,"f"] & E \arrow[r,"g"] & E''\arrow[r] & 0
    \end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} be a short exact sequence of orientable vector bundles. Then the fibrewise
    isomorphisms $\det(E'_p)\otimes\det(E''_p)\to\det(E_p)$ from the
    vector space case assemble into an isomorphism of line bundles
    \[
        \det(E')\otimes\det(E'')\;\xrightarrow{\;\cong\;}\;\det(E)\,.
    \]
    In particular, orientations of $E'$ and $E''$ induce a canonical
    orientation of $E$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
	Fibrewise the map is the canonical isomorphism from the vector
    space case: a lift $\tilde c\in g_p^{-1}(c)$ exists since $g_p$ is
    surjective, and the resulting element
    $f(a_1)\wedge\dots\wedge f(a_l)\wedge\tilde c_1\wedge\dots\wedge
    \tilde c_k$ is independent of the choice of lift.

	 It remains to
    check that this bundle map is continuous (resp.\ smooth) to ensure that it maps sections smoothly into sections, which is
    a local statement: locally choose a frame $(a_1,\dots,a_l)$ of $E'$
    and a frame $(c_1,\dots,c_k)$ of $E''$; since $g$ is an epimorphism
    it admits local sections, so we may choose local lifts
    $\tilde c_j$ of the $c_j$ which are (smooth) local sections of $E$.
    Then the map is given in the induced local frames of the
    determinant bundles by wedging these sections, hence continuous
    (resp.\ smooth). By this, two non-vanishing sections get mapped to a non-vanishing section and hence, orientations on $E'$ and $E''$ which are represented by sections give a orientation over $E$. 
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
	We call a manifold orientable, if its tangent bundle is \textbf{orientable}. We call a touple $(M,[\omega])$ where $[omega]$ denotes an orientation of $TM$ an \textbf{oriented manifold}. We will often omitt the orientation and just call $M$ oriented.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Orientation Preserving Charts and Maps]
Let $(M,[\omega])$ be an oriented $n$-dimensional manifold and let $\phi:U\to \R^n$ be a chart. We call $\phi$ \textbf{orientation preserving} if for every $x\in U$ $\partial_1(x)\wedge\cdots \wedge \partial_n(x)$ and $\omega(x)$ lie in the same connected component of $\det(T_xM)\setminus\{0\}$. More generalyly, let $(N,[\eta])$ be another oriented $n$-dimensional manifold and let $h:M\to N$ be differentiable at $x$ with $\diff h_x$ invertible. We define 
\begin{equation*}
    \sign \det(\diff h _x)\coloneq \begin{cases*}
        +1& if $\Lambda^n(\diff h_x)(\omega (x))$ and $\eta (x)$ induce the same orientation in $T_h(x)N$\\
        -1& otherwise.
    \end{cases*}
\end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{corollary}
    A chart $\phi:U\to \R^n$ is orientation preserving, if for all $x\in U$, and $Q_{f(x)}:T_{\phi(x)}\R^m \to \R^m$ (from Definition \ref{def:vb-linearIdentification}) the map $Q_{f(x)}\circ \restr{\diff \phi}{x}:T_xU\to \R^m$ is an oriented map of vector spaces.
\end{corollary}
\begin{remark}
    One easyly checks that $  \sign \det(\diff h _x)$ is well-defined and reversing both orientations doesn't affect the sign. In particular, for $N=M$ the number $  \sign \det(\diff h _x)$ does not depend on the choosen orientation. 
\end{remark}
\begin{lemma}[Computing the Sign in Charts]\label{lem:orient-computingSignInCharts}
    Let $(M,[\omega])$ and $(N,[\eta])$ be orinted $n$-dimensional manifolds and $\phi$ be an orinetation preserving chart arround $x$ in $M$ and $\psi$ one arround $h(x)$. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \sign\det(\diff h_x)=\sign \det \left(\restr{\diff\left(\psi\circ h\circ \phi^{-1}\right)}{\phi(x)}\right)\,,
    \end{equation*} wherre the right hand side calculates the sign of the determinant of a matrix.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Define $A\coloneq \restr{\diff\left(\psi\circ h\circ \phi^{-1}\right)}{\phi(x)}=\restr{\diff \psi}{h(x)}\circ \restr{\diff h}{x}\circ (\restr{\diff \phi}{x})^{-1}$ and denote the standart orientation of $\R^n$ by $e$. Since $\phi$ is orientation preserving and $\Lambda^n(\restr{\diff \phi}{x})(\partial_1(x)\wedge\cdots\wedge\partial_n(x))=e$, we have by linearity $\Lambda^n(\restr{\diff \phi}{x})(\omega(x))=a\cdot e$ where $a>0$. Similar $\Lambda^n(\restr{\diff \psi}{h(x)})(\eta(h(x)))=b\cdot e$ with $b>0$. Hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \Lambda^n\left(\restr{\diff \psi}{h(x)}\right)\circ \Lambda^n\left(\restr{\diff h}{x}\right)(\omega(x))
        =\Lambda^n(A)\circ \underbrace{\Lambda^n\left(\restr{\diff \phi}{x}\right)(\omega(x))}_{=a\cdot e}=a \det(A)\cdot e
    \end{equation*} Now by definition we need to check if $\Lambda^n(\diff h_x)(\omega (x))=c\cdot \eta (x)$ for $c>0$, which is equivalent to 
    \begin{equation*}
       \underbrace{ \Lambda^n\left(\restr{\diff \psi}{h(x)}\right)\circ \Lambda^n\left(\restr{\diff h}{x}\right)(\omega(x))}_{=a \det(A)\cdot e}=c\cdot \underbrace{\Lambda^n\left(\restr{\diff \psi}{h(x)}\right)(\eta (x))}_{=b\cdot e}\,.
    \end{equation*} which is given if and only if $\det(A)>0$.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:orient-existsOrientationPreservingAtlas}
    A smooth manifold $M$ of dimension $n$ is orientable, if and only it has an atlas such that the determinants of the Jacobians of the transition functions are positive.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
   Let $[\omega]$ be an orientation on $M$ and let $U_\alpha$ be an open cover of $M$ with all $U_\alpha$ connected and the charts $\phi_\alpha:U_\alpha\to \R^n$. Let  $\partial_1^\alpha\wedge \cdots \wedge\partial_n^\alpha$ be the induced local section into the determinant bundle. Then 
   \begin{equation*}
   \restr{\omega}{U_\alpha}=g_\alpha \partial_1^\alpha\wedge \cdots \wedge\partial_n^\alpha
   \end{equation*} whith $g_\alpha$ non vanishing and $U_\alpha$ connected. If $g_\alpha$ is negative, replace $\phi_\alpha$ with $\tilde{\phi_\alpha}(x)=(-(\phi_\alpha(x))_1,\dots,\phi_\alpha(x)_n)$, which changes the sign og $g_\alpha$. Doing this in all charts gives a orientaion preserving atlas. Hence, by defininition $\sign \det(\restr{\diff \id_M}{x})=1$ and by Lemma \ref{lem:orient-computingSignInCharts} we can calculate $\sign \det(\restr{\diff \id_M}{x})$ in this atlass by the determinants of the Jacobians of the transition functions. Hence those determinants are positive.

 Fot the other direction we want to glue together the local sections $\partial_1^\alpha\wedge \cdots \wedge\partial_n^\alpha$ using a smooth partition of unity subordinate to the open cover $\{U_i\}$. This yealds something non-vanishing, as all local section are pointwise positive multiples of each other where they intersect. 
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary} \label{cor:orient-transitionFunctionPositiveDeterminantAlsoOrientationPreserving}
    The proof of Corollary \ref{cor:orient-existsOrientationPreservingAtlas} also shows that if one chart is orientation preserving, then also another chart that intersects with the first and has positive determinant of the Javobian of the transition function.
\end{corollary}

\begin{lemma}
    Let $M$ be a smooth manifold that can be covered with two charts such that their intersection is connected. Then $M$ is orientable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Just ensure that the two charts are compatible such that they build an atlas such that the determinants of the Jacobians of the transition functions are positive. To achive this one just manipulates one of the charts.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    $S^n$ for $n\geq 2$ is orientable, as it is coverd by the two stereographic projections. $S^1$ and $S^0$ are also orientable which can be seen by picking charts such that they fit into corollary \ref{cor:orient-existsOrientationPreservingAtlas}.
\end{corollary}







\subsection{K-theoretic Orientation}
K theoretic orientation relies on bases induced generators of the K group of the sphere. Hence, we want to fix a canonical map from $R^n$ to $S^n$:



\begin{lemma}[Reflection in one variable]\label{lem:reflection-is-minus-one}
	Let $R_1:\R\to\R,\ x\mapsto -x$, and let $Z$ be a pointed compact Hausdorff
	space. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(\overline{R_1}\wedge \id_Z\right)^*=-\id \quad\text{ on } \tilde{K}\left(\overline{\R}\wedge Z\right)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Realise $S^1$ as $\R\big/\Z$ with base point $[0]$, so that the map $T$ of
	Corollary \ref{lem:kTheo-inversesInKGivenByHomotopicallyMethods} is
	$T([t])=[-t]$. Define
	\begin{equation*}
		h:\overline{\R}\to \R\big/\Z\,,\quad h(x)=\left[\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{1}{\pi}\arctan(x)\right]\,,\quad h(\infty)=[0]\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $x\mapsto \tfrac12+\tfrac1\pi\arctan(x)$ is a strictly increasing bijection
	onto $(0,1)$, the map $h$ is bijective; it is continuous on $\R$ and also at
	$\infty$, because $\arctan(x)\to\pm\tfrac{\pi}{2}$ gives $h(x)\to[1]=[0]$ resp.\
	$h(x)\to[0]$. Being a continuous bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff
	space, $h$ is a pointed homeomorphism. It intertwines the two reflections:
	\begin{equation*}
		h(-x)=\left[\tfrac12-\tfrac{1}{\pi}\arctan(x)\right]=\left[-\tfrac12-\tfrac{1}{\pi}\arctan(x)\right]=T(h(x))\,,
	\end{equation*}
	using $[-\tfrac12]=[\tfrac12]$, and $h(\overline{R_1}(\infty))=[0]=T(h(\infty))$.
	Hence $h\wedge \id_Z$ is a pointed homeomorphism with
	\begin{equation*}
		(h\wedge\id_Z)\circ\left(\overline{R_1}\wedge\id_Z\right)=(T\wedge 1)\circ (h\wedge \id_Z)\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Applying $\tilde{K}$ and Corollary
	\ref{lem:kTheo-inversesInKGivenByHomotopicallyMethods} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\left(\overline{R_1}\wedge\id_Z\right)^*=(h\wedge\id_Z)^*\circ (T\wedge1)^*\circ\left((h\wedge\id_Z)^*\right)^{-1}=-\id\,,
	\end{equation*}
	as all maps are ring homomorphisms and thereby commute with multiplicatino by minus one.
\end{proof}




\begin{definition}[K-theoretic orientations]
     We define a \textbf{K-theoretic orientation} of a finite dimensional real vector space $V$ to be a generator of the group $\tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{V})$. As a sainity check we can confirm: since $\overline{V}\cong S^m$ we are looking at a generator of a group isomorphic to $\tilde{K}(S^{2m})\cong \Z$, which has two possible generators.
\end{definition}




\begin{lemma}[Linear Maps on Smash Products]\label{lem:orient-linearMapsOnSmashProducts}
	Let $A\in \GL(V)$ and let $Z$ be a pointed compact space. Then
\begin{equation*}
	(\overline{A}\wedge \id_Z)^*=\sign \det(A)\cdot \id \quad \text{on }\tilde{K}(\overline{V}\wedge Z)\,.
\end{equation*}
In particular, if $\overline{\R^{m_1}}\wedge \cdots \wedge\overline{\R^{m_r}}$ is identified with $\overline{R^m}$ by Lemma \ref{lem:orient-compactificationOfProduct}, a permutation od the smash factors acts on $\tilde{K}(\overline{\R^{m_1}}\wedge \cdots \wedge\overline{\R^{m_r}}\wedge Z)$ by the sign of the induced permutation of the $m=m_1+\cdots m_r$ coordinates.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
 Since the determinant of an endomorphism is invariant under conjugation we can choose a basis and assume that $V=\R^m$. The group $\GL_n(\R)$ as exactly two path components, seperated by the sign of the determinant. Hence, there is a contiuous path $A_{\bullet}:[0,1]\to \GL_m(\R)$  with $A_0=A$ and $A_1=\id$ if $\det(A)>0$ and $R$ if $\det(A)<0$ with $R$ being the reflection $(x_1,\dots,x_m)\mapsto (-x_1,x_2\dots,x_m)$. The map 
    \begin{equation*}
        H:\R^m\times[0,1]\to \R^m\,,\quad H(v,t)\coloneq A_tv
    \end{equation*} is a proper homomorphism into a locally compact space: If $K\subseteq \R^m$ is compact and $A_tv\in K$, then by definition of the operator norm we have that $\norm{v}\leq (\sup_t\norm{A^{-1}_t})\cdot \max_{k\in K}\norm{k}$. Hence $H^{-1}(K)$ is bounded and closed by continuity. Thereby the the extension $\tilde{H}:\overline{V\times I}\to \overline{V}$ is continuous. Let $q:\overline{V}\times I\to \overline{V\times I}$ be the collaps of $\{\infty\}\times I$ to $\infty$. Then we have that $\overline{H}=\tilde{H}\circ q$ and hence $\overline{H}$ is continuous. If $\det(A)>0$, we are done. Let $\overline{R}$ denote the extension of $R$ to the compactifications. What is left to show is that $(\overline{R}\wedge \id_Z)^*$ is given by multiplication with minus one.


    For this we write $R=R_1\times \id_{\R^{n-1}}$ where $R_1$ denotes the reflection $x\mapsto -x$ on $\R$. Let $\Xi:\overline{\R^n}\to \overline{\R}\wedge \overline{\R^{n-1}}$ be the natural homeomorphism from Lemma \ref{lem:orient-compactificationOfProduct}. By the naturality for proper maps we have that $  \Xi\circ (\overline{R})=(\overline{R_1}\wedge \overline{\id_{\R^{n-1}}})\circ \Xi$, giving us:
    \begin{equation*}
     (\Xi\wedge \id_Z)(\overline{R}\wedge \id_Z)=(\Xi\circ \overline{R})\wedge \id_Z=\big((\overline{R_1}\wedge \overline{\id_{\R^{n-1}}})\circ \Xi \big)\wedge \id_Z 
    \end{equation*}
    If we now define $L\coloneq \overline{R^{m-1}}\wedge Z$ and let $AS:(\overline{\R^1}\wedge \overline{\R^{m-1}})\wedge Z\to \overline{\R^1}\wedge (L)$ be the assozoiativity of the wedge product. Then we have 
    \begin{equation*}
       AS\circ (\Xi\wedge \id_Z)(\overline{R}\wedge \id_Z)=(\overline{R_1}\wedge \id_L)\circ AS\circ \Xi\wedge \id_Z
    \end{equation*}
Hence,if we define $\Theta \coloneq AS\circ \Xi\wedge \id_Z$ we get
\begin{equation*}
    \overline{R}\wedge \id_Z=\Theta^{-1}\circ (\overline{R_1}\wedge \id_L)\circ \Theta
\end{equation*} or as maps of K-groups:
\begin{equation*}   ( \overline{R}\wedge \id_Z)^*=\Theta^*\circ \underbrace{(\overline{R_1}\wedge \id_L)^*}_{=-\id\text{ by Lemma \ref{lem:reflection-is-minus-one}}}\circ (\Theta^{-1})^*
   =-\id\,.
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:linearyTheDeterminandDertermines}
    Let $V$ be an $n$-dimensinoal real vector space and $A\in \GL(V)$. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{A}^*=\sign \det(A)\cdot \id \quad \text{ on }\tilde{K}^{-n}(\overline{V})\,.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The statement is almost a corollary of Lemma \ref{lem:orient-linearMapsOnSmashProducts}:
    We care for the map $\overline{A}^*$ which is defined as the map
    \begin{equation*}
        (\id_{S^n}\wedge A)^*:\tilde{K}^{-n}(V)=\tilde{K}(S^n\wedge V) \to \tilde{K}(S^n\wedge V)=\tilde{K}^{-n}(V)
    \end{equation*}
    Let $\Theta:S^n\wedge V\to V\wedge S^n$ be the reordering from Lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-SmaschIsAssociative}. Then we have that
    \begin{equation*}
       \id_{S^n}\wedge A= \Theta^{-1}\circ (A \wedge \id{S^n}) \circ \Theta\,.
    \end{equation*}Hence, lemma \ref{lem:orient-linearMapsOnSmashProducts} gives us:
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{A}^*=(\Theta^{-1}\circ (A \wedge \id{S^n}) \circ \Theta)^*=\Theta^*\circ \underbrace{(A \wedge \id{S^n})^*}_{=\sign \det(A)\cdot \id}\circ (\Theta^{-1})^*=\sign \det(A)\cdot \id\,.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

    



\begin{comment}

\begin{remark} \label{rem:orient-OrientationsInducesKOrientation}
An orientation of $V$ induces a K-theoretic orientation.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
Let $v$ and $v'$ be two equivalent orientations over $V$. Then $M^v_{v'}\simeq \id_V$, since $\GL_{\R}(V)$ has exactly two connected components seperated by the sign of the determinant. (This is a standart theorem for the isomorphic $\GL_m(\R)$). Let $H$ be a homotopy from $M^v_{v'}\simeq \id_V$ given by a continouos path $A:I\to \GL(V)$ with $A(0)=\id$ and $A(1)=M^v_{v'}$.I.e.\ $H:V\times I \to V$ is of the form $H(x,t)=A(t)(x)$. Now extend this homotopy to the following:
\begin{equation*}
	\overline{H}:\overline{V}\times I \to \overline{V}\,,\quad (\infty,t)\mapsto \infty\,.
\end{equation*} Now $H$ is a proper homomorphism into a locally compact space and hence the extension $\tilde{H}:\overline{V\times I}\to \overline{V}$ is continuous. Let $q:\overline{V}\times I\to \overline{V\times I}$ be the collaps of $\{\infty\}\times I$ to $\infty$. Then we have that $\overline{H}=\tilde{H}\circ q$ and hence $\overline{H}$ is continuous. Now let $\kappa_v$ be the coordinate map $V \to \R^m$ that maps each vector to its coordinate representation. Then we have the up to homotopy commutative diagram:
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
\overline{V} \arrow[r, "\id"] \arrow[d, "\kappa_v"']                                  & \overline{V} \arrow[d, "\kappa_{v'}"] \\
\overline{\R^m} \arrow[r, "M^v_{v'}", bend left] \arrow[r, "\id", bend right=49] & \overline{\R^m}                 
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Hence, the diagram commutes, 
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
\tilde{K}^{-m}(S^m) \arrow[r, "(\pi_S^{-1})^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{\R^m}) \arrow[r, "\kappa_v^*"] \arrow[d, "\id"] & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{V}) \arrow[d, "\id"] \\
\tilde{K}^{-m}(S^m) \arrow[r, "(\pi_S^{-1})^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{\R^m}) \arrow[r, "\kappa^*_{v'}"]               & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{V})                 
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
Thereby the induced generators agree.
\end{proof}






\begin{lemma}[K-Theoretic Orientations Detect Orientations]\label{lem:orient-kTheoreticOrientationDetecsOrientation}
    Assume, that $v'$ and $v$ are non-equivalent orientations. Hence, $\det(M^v_{v'})<0$. Then they induce different generators.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    With a similar argument to above we can conclude, that $M^v_{v'}\simeq \Big(x_1,\dots x_m)\mapsto (x_1,\dots x_{m-1},-x_m)\big)$ and again there extensions to the one point compactifications are homotypic.
    Now we define two maps: 
    \begin{align*}
        T: S^m &\to S^m \\
        (x_1,\dots,x_{m+1})&  \mapsto (-x_1,\dots,x_{m+1})
    \end{align*}
    and
    \begin{align*}
        S:\overline{R^m} & \to \overline{R^m} \\
        (x_1,\dots x_m)  &\mapsto (-x_1,\dots x_m)
    \end{align*}
    With those maps we have the commutative diagram: 
    \begin{center}
        % https://tikzcd.yichuanshen.de/#N4Igdg9gJgpgziAXAbVABwnAlgFyxMJZABgBpiBdUkANwEMAbAVxiRAGUA9AWxAF9S6TLnyEUZAIxVajFmy68BQ7HgJEJ5afWatEIADr6INGACcGWMDGCGASjz79BIDCtHrSU6trl7DxswsrG317bkc+aRgoAHN4IlAAM1MIXkQAJmocCCQyGR02QzQsAH04TmAAWgkI52TUpABmLJzEDXzfA31isorq2qSUtPbs3O9ZXRAAFSdBhsRmkFGM8YK9dn4KPiA
\begin{tikzcd}
S^m                & \overline{\R^m} \arrow[l, "\pi_s^{-1}"]                \\
S^m \arrow[u, "T"] & \overline{\R^m} \arrow[l, "\pi_s^{-1}"] \arrow[u, "S"]
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    And hence since $S\simeq \overline{M^v_{v'}}$ we get the diagram, is commutative up to homotopy:
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
S^m                & \overline{\R^m} \arrow[l, "\pi_s^{-1}"]                & \overline{V} \arrow[l, "\kappa_{v'}"]                 \\
S^m \arrow[u, "T"] & \overline{\R^m} \arrow[l, "\pi_s^{-1}"] \arrow[u, "S"] & \overline{V} \arrow[u, "\id"] \arrow[l, "\kappa_{v}"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
    This induces the commutative diagramm 
\begin{equation} \label{eq: Comutativ diagramm -1 is orientation change}
\begin{tikzcd}
\tilde{K}^{-m}(S^m) \arrow[d, "T^*"'] \arrow[r, "(\pi_S^{-1})^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{\R^m}) \arrow[d, "S^*"] \arrow[r, "\kappa_{v'}^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{V}) \arrow[d, "\id"] \\
\tilde{K}^{-m}(S^m) \arrow[r, "(\pi_S^{-1})^*"']                  & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{\R^m}) \arrow[r, "\kappa_v^*"']                & \tilde{K}^{-m}(\overline{V})                 
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{equation}
Now we want to see that $T^*=-\id$.
For this, we start by looking at $K^{-m}(S^m)=K(S^{2m})$. In this case we define 
\begin{align*}
    T': S^m\wedge S^m =S^{2m}       & \to S^{2m}\\
    (x_1,\dots x_m,y_1,\dots, y_m)  & \mapsto (x_1,\dots x_m,-y_1,\dots, y_m)
\end{align*} By definition $T^*=(T')^*$ and we have a homotopy between $T'$ and 
\begin{align*}
    R\wedge \id :S^{2m} & \to       S^{2m}\\
    (x_1,\dots x_{2m})  & \mapsto   (-x_1,\dots x_{2m})
\end{align*} that is given by the rotation $\rho_t$ in the $x_1,y_1$ plane by $t$ degree. This rotation for 90 degree maps $x_1\mapsto y_1$ and $y_1 \mapsto -x_1$, and thereby conjugating $R\wedge \id$ with $\rho$ (for 90 degree) gives $T'$. The homotopy is then given by $\rho_t\circ  R\wedge \id\circ \rho_t^{-1}$. Now the way we defined $S^{2m}\subseteq \R^{2m+1}$ the map $ R\wedge \id$ really is the wedge product of the identity on $S^{2m-1}$ with the map $R$, that exchanges the south and north pole. Hence by the lemma \ref{lem:kTheo-doubleConeFlip} this agrees with the multiplication with $-1$, and hence the map $T^*$ is the multiplication with $-1$. 
Finally, we can compare the induced generators: for this we denote $\beta_v\coloneq (\kappa_v)^*\circ (\pi^{-1}_S)^*(\beta)$ to be the generator given by the oriented basis $v$ and $\beta_{v'}\coloneq (\kappa_{v'})^*\circ (\pi^{-1}_S)^*(\beta)$ given by $v'$. Now we have that 
\begin{align*}
     -\beta_{v'} =\id \circ (\kappa_{v'})^*\circ \underbrace{(\pi^{-1}_S)^*}_{(S^*)^{-1}\circ (\pi^{-1}_S)^*\circ T^*}(-\beta)=\underbrace{\id \circ (\kappa_{v'})^*\circ (S^*)^{-1}}_{=\kappa_v^*}\circ (\pi^{-1}_S)^*\circ \underbrace{T^*(-\beta)}_{=\beta}
    =\beta_v
\end{align*}
\end{proof}
\end{comment}


\begin{corollary}\label{cor:orient-vectorspaceOrientationInducesKtheoreticOrientation}
Any finite dimensional real vector space $V$ is homeomoprhic to $\R^n$ via the coordinate map $\kappa_v$ that maps each vector to its coordinate representation. With this we can introduce a K-theoretic orientation from an ordered basis via the composition: 
   \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
\tilde{K}^{-n}(S^n) \arrow[r, "\overline{(\pi_S^{-1})}^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-n}(\overline{\R^n}) \arrow[r, " \overline{\kappa}_v^*"] & \tilde{K}^{-n}(\overline{V})
\end{tikzcd}
   \end{center} With this we can map the bott generator $\beta$ from Definition \ref{def:kTheo-BottGenerator} to a generator of $\tilde{K}^{-n}(\overline{V})$ and hence induce a K-Theoretic orientation. This orientation only depends on the orientation of $V$ and two different orientations induce different K-theoretic orientations
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    The statement follows imidiatly from Proposition \ref{prop:linearyTheDeterminandDertermines}.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:orient-mapsbetweenabstractVectorSpacesOnKOrientations}
    Let $f:V\to W$ be a linear isomorphism between oriented vector spaces $(V,\mathfrak{a})$ and $(W,\mathfrak{b})$. Let $\beta_V$ be the K-Theoretic orientation of $V$ and $\beta_W$ of $W$. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
       \overline{f}^*(\beta_W)\,=\, \sign \det(f)\cdot (\beta _V)\,,
    \end{equation*} where $\sign \det (f)$ denotes the sign of $f$ as in Definition \ref{def:oeient-SignOfAAbstractLinearMap}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\kappa_V:V\to \R^n$ and $\kappa_W:W\to \R^n$ be positive coordinate maps.
    By Proposition \ref{prop:linearyTheDeterminandDertermines} we know that \begin{equation*}
        (\overline{\kappa_W}\circ \overline{f} \circ \overline{\kappa_V}^{-1})^*\,=\,\sign \det(f)\cdot \id
    \end{equation*} Now $\beta_V=\overline{\kappa_V}^*(\beta_{\R^n})$ and  $\beta_W=\overline{\kappa_W}^*(\beta_{\R^n})$. This concludes the proof, as
    \begin{align*}
        \overline{f}^*(\beta_W)
        &=(\overline{\kappa_V}^*\circ (\overline{\kappa_V}^*)^{-1})\circ \overline{f}^*\circ\big(\overline{\kappa_W}^*(\beta_{\R^n})\big)\\
        &=\overline{\kappa_V}^*\circ (\overline{\kappa_W} \circ \overline{f} \circ \overline{\kappa_V}^{-1})^*\beta_{\R^n}\\
        &=\sign \det(f)\,\overline{\kappa_V}^*\,\beta_{\R^n}=\sign \det(f)\,\beta_V\,.
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Multiplication]\label{lem:orient-multiplication}
    Let $V,W$ be oriented real vector spaces of dimension $n,m$ and give $V\oplus W$ the orientation by placing the orientation of $V$ infront of the one of $W$. 
    Let $\beta_\mathfrak{o}$ be the K Theoretic orientation ov $V$ induced by the oreintation of $V$ and similar $\beta_\mathfrak{p}$ be the one on $W$, and finaly $\beta_{\mathfrak{o}\oplus \mathfrak{p}}$ be the K-thoretic orientation of $V\oplus W$.    Then under the homeomorphism  
    \begin{equation*}
        \Xi: \overline{V\oplus W}\to \overline{V}\wedge \overline{W}
    \end{equation*} from Lemma \ref{lem:orient-compactificationOfProduct} we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \Xi^{*}\left(\beta_{\mathfrak o}\boxtimes\beta_{\mathfrak p}\right)=\beta_{\mathfrak o\oplus\mathfrak p}\,.
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof} Let $\kappa_v:V \to \R^n$ and $\kappa_w:\R^n \to W$ be two basis maps from ordered basis compatible with the orientations ov $V$ and $W$.
    By definition and naturality of the reduced external product:
    \begin{align*}
         \Xi^{*}\left(\beta_{\mathfrak o}\boxtimes\beta_{\mathfrak p}\right)
         &= \Xi^* \circ \Big(\left(\overline{(\pi_S^n)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_v\right)^*(\beta_n) \boxtimes \left(\overline{(\pi_S^m)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_w\right)^*(\beta_m) \Big) \\
         &= \Xi^* \circ \left(\left(\overline{(\pi_S^n)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_v\right) \wedge \left(\overline{(\pi_S^m)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_w\right)\right)^*(\beta_n \boxtimes \beta_m)
    \end{align*}
Now, the function $(\left(\overline{(\pi_S^n)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_v\right) \wedge \left(\overline{(\pi_S^m)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_w\right):\overline{V}\to S^n$ is the compactification of $(\pi_S^n)^{-1}\circ \kappa_v$ Hence, by naturality of $\Xi$ we conclude:
\begin{align*}
    \Xi^* \circ \left(\left(\overline{(\pi_S^n)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_v\right) \wedge \left(\overline{(\pi_S^m)}^{-1}\circ \overline{\kappa}_w\right)\right)^*
    &= \overline{
