		\Phi: \bigoplus_{i+j=k}C^i(M,\mathfrak{A},\Lambda)\otimes_\Lambda C^j(M',\mathfrak{A}',\Lambda) \to C^k(M\times M',\mathfrak{A}\times \mathfrak{A}',\Lambda)
	\end{equation*} This isomorphism ,however, is clear and given on the generators: Let $p$ and $p'$ be critical points of index $i$ and $j$ with respect to $f$ and $f'$. Let $\eta$ and $\eta'$ be the corresponding generators of $C^i(M,\mathfrak{A},\Lambda)$ and $C^j(M',\mathfrak{A}',\Lambda)$. Then we have the bijection 
	\begin{equation*}
		\eta \otimes \eta' \leftrightarrow (\eta,\eta')\,.
	\end{equation*} To get a chain isomorphism we need to check the compatibility with the coboundary maps. For each $q_l\in \Crit_{i+1}(f)$ let $\xi_l$ be the corresponding form and similarly for each ${q'}_k\in \Crit_{j+1}(f')$ let ${\xi'}_k$ be the corresponding form. Then we calculate
	\begin{align*}
	\Phi\left(
	 \partial^f\otimes 1+(-1)^i 1\otimes \partial^{f'}
	\right) (\eta \otimes \eta')
	&= \Phi\left(
	 \partial^f(\eta)\otimes \eta'+(-1)^i \eta\otimes \partial^{f'}(\eta')
	\right)\\
		&= \Phi\left(
	\sum_{l} n(q_l,p)\xi_l \otimes \eta'+\sum_k (-1)^i n({q'}_k,p')\eta\otimes {\xi'}_k
	\right)\\
		&= 
	\sum_{l} n(q_l,p)(\xi_l , \eta')+\sum_k (-1)^i n({q'}_k,p')(\eta, {\xi'}_k)
	\\
	\end{align*} and the other way around, if we define for $(q,q')$ with $\ind_{f+f'}(q,q')=\ind_{f+f'}(p,p')+1$ the corresponding form to be $\nu$ we have:
	\begin{align*}
		\partial^{f+f'}\Phi(\eta\otimes \eta')
		&=\partial^{f+f'}(\eta,\eta')\\
		&=\sum_{(q,q')\in \Crit_{i+j+1}(f+f')}n\left(
													(q,q'),(p,p')
											   \right)(\nu)\\
		&=\underbrace{\sum_l n(q_l,p)(\xi_l,\eta')}_{q'=p'}+
		  \underbrace{\sum_k (-1)^{i}n({q'}_k,p')(\eta,{\xi'}_k)}_{q=p}
	\end{align*}
	Now finally we can apply the Algebraic Künneth Formula for Cohomology and we are done. The latter formular is almost the same as the homological one but with a different index shift in the $\Tor$ functor. Again this is standard homological algebra.
\end{proof}



\begin{comment}






Now we want to understand the flow in an unstable set and analyse two maps:
\begin{remark}\label{rem:morse-theMapTau}\todo{Bild malen!}
Let $q$ be a critical point and $c<f(q)$ such that no other critical point lies in $f^{-1}[c,f(q)]$ then, the map
	\begin{align*}
		\tau: W(q\to)\setminus \{q\} &\to \R\\
		x                  &\mapsto \tau(x) \text{ such that }f\circ \phi_{\tau(x)}(x) =c\,
	\end{align*}
	is smooth.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	We know that such a map exists and is well defined since every flow line passes through $f^{-1}(c)$. Now let $x\in W(q\to)\setminus \{q\}$, $V$ be a neighbourhood of $x$ and  $U$ be a neighbourhood of $\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)$. Define the map
	\begin{align*}
		G:V\times I &\to \R\\
		(x,t)&       \mapsto f\circ \left(\phi_t(x)\right) \, .
	\end{align*}
	Now the partial derivative reads
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t}G\big|_{x,t}=\diff f\big|_{\phi_t(x)}\circ \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \phi_t(x)\big|_t
		=  \diff f\big|_{\phi_t(x)}\circ \left(-\grad_{\phi_t(x)}(f)\right) \,.
	\end{equation*} Since the flow line is transversal to the level set or in our case, the gradient is even orthogonal to the level sets, it is not contained in $T_{\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)}f^{-1}(c)=  \ker\diff f \big|_{\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)}$ and hence:
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\partial}{\partial t}G\big|{x,\tau(x)}=\diff f \big|_{\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)}\left(-\grad_{\phi_{\tau(x)}(x)}(f)\right)\neq 0 \, .
	\end{equation*} This lets us use the implicit function theorem to deduce that the level set $G=c$ is locally the graph of a function $\tau: U_x\to \R$ where $U_x$ is a neighbourhood of $x$. Hence, the function $\tau$ is globally smooth since this argument can be done everywhere.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma} Let $\gamma_j\in \mathcal{M}(q\to)$ and let $x_j=\gamma_j\cap f^{-1}(c)$ denote the intersection of the image of $\gamma_j$ with the levelset $f^{-1}(c)$. Then there exists a frame $(l_1,\dots, l_m)$ of the restricted bundle $\restr{T W(q\to)}{\gamma_j}$ such that for all $x\in \gamma_j$ $l_1(x)=-\grad_x(f)$ and 
	\begin{equation*}
		\diff \restr{\tau}{x}(-\grad_x(f))=-1\,,
	\end{equation*} and for any $i>1$ we have that
\begin{equation*}
		\diff \restr{\tau}{x}(l_i(x))=0\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof} We denote the connected component of $f^{-1}(c)$ that contains $x_j$ by $V_j$. Now we choose a chart of $W(q\to)$ around $x_j\in W(q\to)\cap V_j$ such that the induced basis of $T_{x_j}W(q\to)=\langle -\grad_{x_j}(f)\rangle \oplus T_{x_j}V_j$ respects that splitting by setting $\frac{\partial}{ \partial x^1}\big|_{x_j}= -\grad_{x_j}(f) $ and $\frac{\partial}{ \partial x^i}\big|_{x_j}\in T_{x_j}V_j$ for $i$ bigger than one. With this we can start our calculations:

	Notice how $x\mapsto f\circ \phi_{\tau(x)}(x)$ is a constant function for all $x\in W(q\to)$ and hence its differential is $0$. For the next calculations we denote the derivative in $x$ by $\diff_x$ and in $t$ by $\diff _t$:
	\begin{align*}
		0=\diff_x\left(f\circ \phi_{\tau}\right)&=\overbrace{\diff f }^{1\text{-form}}\left( \diff\phi_\tau\right)=\diff f \left( \diff_t \phi_t \circ\diff_x\tau(x)+\diff_x\phi_{\tilde{t}=\tau(x)}\right)\\
		&=\diff f \left( \diff_t \phi_t\circ \diff_x\tau(x)\right)+\diff f \left( \diff_x\phi_{\tilde{t}=\tau(x)}\right)\\
		&=\diff_t \left(f \circ\phi_t \right) \circ\diff_x\tau +\diff f \left( \diff_x\phi_{\tilde{t}=\tau(x)} \right)
	\end{align*}
	Hence we know that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: differential of tau}
		\diff_x \tau =-\frac{\diff f \left( \diff_x\phi_{\tilde{t}=\tau(x)} \right)}{\diff_t \left(f \circ\phi_t \right)}=-\frac{\diff f \left( \diff_x\phi_{\tilde{t}=\tau(x)} \right)}{-\norm{\grad_x(f)^2}}
	\end{equation}  Now we can calculate in $x_j$ where $\tau(x_j)=0$
	\begin{align*}
		\diff_x \tau \big|_{x_j}\frac{\partial}{ \partial x^i}\big|_{x_j}
		=\frac{
			\diff f \big|_{x_j} \frac{\partial}{ \partial x^i}\big|_{x_j}}
		{\norm{\grad(f)^2}}
		=\frac{
			g\left(\grad_{x_i}(f), \frac{\partial}{ \partial x^i}\big|_{x_j}\right)}
		{\norm{\grad(f)^2}}=
		\begin{cases}
			-1 & \text{ if }i=1\\
			0 & \text{ else. }
		\end{cases}
	\end{align*}
	This result can be extended to also work outside of $x_j$ as follows: Notice the identity for all $s\in \R$ and $x\in W(q\to)\setminus \{q\}$:
	\begin{equation*}
		\tau(\phi_s(x))=\tau(x)-s\,.
	\end{equation*} Differentiating this in the variable $s$ in the point $s'$ yields:
	\begin{align*}
		\restr{\diff \tau(\phi_{s}(x))}{s'}\,&=\,\restr{\diff (\tau(x)-s)}{s'}=-1 \\
		\text{ and }\quad 		\restr{\diff \tau(\phi_{s}(x))}{s'}\,&=\, \restr{\diff \tau}{\phi_{s'}(x)}\circ \underbrace{\restr{\diff \phi_s(x)}{s=s'}}_{=-\grad_{\phi_s(x)}(f)}
	\end{align*}
	 And hence in $s'=0$ we have for all $x\in W(q\to)\setminus \{q\}$:
	\begin{equation*}
		\restr{\diff \tau }{x}(-\grad(x)(f))=-1\,.
	\end{equation*}
	We can furthermore define a basis of $T_x W(q\to)$ with $\mathfrak{b}_1\coloneq -\grad_x(f)$ and $\mathfrak{b}_i=\big(\restr{\diff \phi_{\tau(x)}}{x}\big)^{-1}({w_i})$ where $(w_i)$ denotes a basis of $T_{x_j}V_j$. Hence $w_i$ is orthogonal on $-\grad_{x_j}(f)$ for all $i>1$. Hence by the equation (\ref{eq: differential of tau}) we can calculate:
	\begin{equation*}
		\diff_x \tau(\mathfrak{b}_i)=-\frac{\diff f \left( \diff_x\phi_{\tilde{t}=\tau(x)}(\mathfrak{b}_i) \right)}{-\norm{\grad(f)^2}}=-\frac{\diff f \left( w_i \right)}{-\norm{\grad(f)^2}}=0\quad \text{ for all }i>1\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Finally after choosing a basis $(w_i)$, the map $x\mapsto \mathfrak{b}_i$ is smooth for all $i$ and any $x\in W(q\to)\setminus \{q\}$ and hence altogether we get the desired frame.
\end{proof}


\begin{definition}
	Let $q$ be a critical point and $c$ such that $c<f(q)$ and $f^{-1}[c,f(q)]=q$. Then we define the map
	\begin{align*}
		\rho: W(q\to)\setminus \{q\}&\to W(q\to)\cap f^{-1}(c)\\
		x\mapsto \phi_{\tau(x)}(x)\,.
	\end{align*} 
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem: Rho and Tau induces a Morse boundary orientation}
	Assume that $\mathfrak{d}=(\mathfrak{d}_i)$ is a basis of $T^u_qM$. Let $y\coloneq{E^u_q}^{-1}(x_j)$ and $y'\in (\rho\circ E^u_q)^{-1}(x_j)$. Then the map
	\begin{align*}
		\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y}^{-1}\circ \diff \rho \circ \restr{\diff E^u_q}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}\circ B_{\mathfrak{d}}&: \R^{\lambda_q}\to T_yT^u_qM
	\end{align*} can be written as
	\begin{align*}
		\pi_{-\grad_{x_j}(f)^\top}\circ(B_{\mathfrak{b}^q_{x_j}}):
		\R^{\lambda_q}&     \to T_{x_j}V_j\\
		e_i           & \mapsto
		\begin{cases}
			0                                       & \text{ for }i=1\\
			({\mathfrak{b}^q_{x_j}})_i & \text{ for }i\neq 1\,.
		\end{cases}
	\end{align*} where $({\mathfrak{b}^q_{x_j}})_j$ is basis of $T_{x_j}W(q\to)$ that is induced from $\mathfrak{d}$ and $({\mathfrak{b}^q_{x_j}})_1=-\grad_{x_j}(f)$. In other words, this is a basis compatible with the one used in the counting of flow lines in the Morse boundary operator.
\end{lemma}

\begin{Iprove}
	To prove this, we first remind ourselves how the basis $({\mathfrak{b}^q_{x_j}})_{j>1}$ is constructed. It is a basis that when extended with $-\grad_{x_j}(f)$ as the first vector is compatible with $\mathfrak{d}$. Hence, we can construct it from $\mathfrak{d}$, by assuming that $\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y}\circ Q_y\circ \mathfrak{d}_1=-\grad_y(f)$. Then, if we push $\mathfrak{d}$ to $T_{x_j}W(q\to)$ via $\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y}\circ Q_y$ and delete the first vector, we get the basis we wanted. So what we need to check in order to prove this lemma is, if the maps
	\begin{align*}
		\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y}\circ Q_y\circ B_{\mathfrak{d}}: &\R^{\lambda_q-1}\to \left(-\grad_{x_j}(f)\right)^\top\\
		\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y}^{-1}\circ \diff \rho \circ \restr{\diff E^u_q}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}\circ B_{\mathfrak{d}}: &\R^{\lambda_q-1}\to \left(-\grad_{x_j}(f)\right)^\top
	\end{align*} induce the same orientation when restricted to the same domain such that they become vector space isomorphisms. 
	To prove this we will work from the bottom up: We adapt the basis $\mathfrak{d}$ to work with the lower map and show, later, that the adapted basis fits the requirements above:
\end{Iprove}


\begin{proof}
	
	We start by defining the space $\left(\tau\circ E^u_q\right)^{-1}(\tilde t)$, where $\tilde{t}\coloneq \tau\circ E^u_q(y')$. As a level set, this is a submanifold of $T^u_qM$ and its tangent space in $y'$ is given by the basis $\restr{E^u_q}{y'}^{-1}(\mathfrak{b_i})$ for $i>1$, where $\mathfrak{b}_i=\restr{\diff \phi_{\tilde{t}}}{y'}^{-1}({w_i})$ where $(w_i)$ denotes a basis of $T_{x_j}V_j$. Now we have the equality of maps:
	\begin{align*}
		\restr{A_{\tilde{t}}}{\left(\tau\circ E^u_q\right)^{-1}(\tilde t)}=\restr{\left({E^u_q}^{-1}\circ \rho \circ E^u_q\right)}{\left(\tau\circ E^u_q\right)^{-1}(\tilde t)}
	\end{align*} Hence for a basis $\mathfrak{d}_i$ of $T^u_qM$ such that $\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}(\mathfrak{d_i})$ is $-\grad_{y'}(f)$ for $i=1$ and $\mathfrak{b_i}$ else, we have:
	\begin{equation*}
		\restr{\diff \left({E^u_q}^{-1}\circ \rho \circ E^u_q\right)}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}(\mathfrak{d}_i)=Q_y\circ A_{\tilde{t}}(\mathfrak{d}_i).
	\end{equation*} For $i=1$, the left side maps to $0$, and the right side:
	\begin{align*}
		Q_y\circ A_{\tilde{t}}(\mathfrak{d}_1)
		&=\restr{\diff A_{\tilde{t}}}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}(\mathfrak{d}_1)\\
		&=\restr{\diff A_{\tilde{t}}}{y'} \circ {\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y'}}^{-1}(-\grad_{y'}(f))\\
		&=\restr{\diff A_{\tilde{t}} \circ \diff E^u_q}{y'}^{-1}(-\grad_{y'}(f))\\
		&=\restr{ \diff (E^u_q)^{-1} \circ \phi_{\tilde{t}}}{y'}^{-1}(-\grad_{y'}(f))\\
		&=\restr{ \diff E^u_q}{x_j}^{-1} \circ \restr{\diff \phi_{\tilde{t}}}{y'}^{-1}(-\grad_{y'}(f))\\
		&=\restr{ \diff E^u_q}{x_j}^{-1} \left(-\grad_{x_j}(f)\right)
	\end{align*} The last equation is the general fact proven in remark \ref{rem: jacobian of the flow in refined Morse chart}, that for fixed $t$ the identity holds:
	\begin{equation*}
		\restr{\diff_x \phi_t}{y} (-\grad_y(f))=-\grad_{\phi_t(y)}(f)
	\end{equation*} Hence if $\pi$ denotes the projection onto the complement of $ \restr{ \diff E^u_q}{x_j}^{-1} \left(-\grad_{x_j}(f)\right)$ we have an equality of maps
	\begin{equation*}
		\restr{\diff \left({E^u_q}^{-1}\circ \rho \circ E^u_q\right)}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}=\pi \circ Q_y\circ A_{\tilde{t}}.
	\end{equation*} Or by the above calculation, we can throw out $\pi$, if we exclude $\mathfrak{d}_1$. Furthermore, the equation shows that our requirement $\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y'}\circ Q_{y'}(\mathfrak{d_1})=-\grad_{y'}(f)$ implies that $\mathfrak{d}$ is compatible with a basis where $\restr{\diff E^u_q}{y}\circ Q_y\circ \mathfrak{d}_1=-\grad_y(f)$, since the sign of the determinant of $A_{\tilde{t}}$ is always positive.
	This concludes the proof!
\end{proof}

 \end{comment}


%%%%%% ===== morseFiltration.tex ===== %%%%%%

\section{A Morse-Theoretic Filtration}
\subsection{Conley index}
\begin{definition}[Compact Invariant Isolated Subset] \label{def:morseFiltr-compactInvariantIsolatedSubset}
	For a flow $\phi_t:M\to M$ on a locally compact metric space, we call a subset $S\subseteq M$ an \textbf{invariant subset} if and only if $\phi_t(S)=S$ for all $t\in \R$. For any subset $N\subseteq M$ we define the \textbf{maximal invariant subset}
	\begin{align*}
		I(N)&=\{x\in N \mid \phi_t(x)\in N \forall t\in \R\}\\
		&=\bigcap_{t\in \R} \phi_t(N).
	\end{align*} A \textbf{compact invariant subset} $S$ is called \textbf{isolated} if a compact neighbourhood $N$ exists such that $I(N)=S$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}[Index Pairs] \label{def:morseFiltr-indexPairs}
	Let $S$ be an isolated compact invariant subset. A topological pair $(N,L)$ of compact subsets of $M$ where $L\subseteq N$ is called an \textbf{index pair of }S, if it satisfies the following:
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item $S=I(\overline{N\setminus L})\subseteq \inti({N\setminus L})$.
		\item  $x\in L$ and $\phi_{[0,t]}(x)\subseteq N$ implies that  $\phi_{[0,t]}(x)\subseteq L$. We call $L$ \textbf{positively invariant in } $N$. Here $\phi_{[0,t]}(x)\coloneq\{ \phi_{\tilde{t}}(x)|\tilde{t}\in [0,t]\}$.
		\item For all $x\in N$ such that a $t$ exists, with $\phi_t(x)\not\in N$, there exists a $t'$ with $\phi_{[0,t']}(x)\subseteq N$ and $\phi_{t'}(x)\in L$.
	\end{enumerate}
	This definition captures how the flow lines leave the invariant space $S$. Due to the third property we call $L$ the \textbf{exit set}.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:morseFiltr-existenceOfIndexPairs}
	For any isolated compact invariant subset $S$ in a locally compact metric space $M$ with a flow $\phi: M\to M$ there exists an index pair $(N_1,N_0)$ of $S$. 
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
	This is Theorem 4.3 in \cite{Salamon1985}.
\end{proof}


\begin{definition}[Regular Index Pairs]\label{def:morseFiltr-regularIndexPairs} \todo{ needed?}
	We call an index pair $(N,L)$ \textbf{regular} if and only if the inclusion $i: L \hookrightarrow N$ is a cofibration.
\end{definition}
\begin{theorem}[Homotopy Equivalence of Index Pairs]\label{thm:morseFiltr-homotopyEquivalenceOfIndexPairs}
	If $S$ is an isolated compact invariant subset and $(N,L)$ and $(\tilde{N},\tilde{L})$ are two index pairs of $S$, then $N/L$ and $\tilde{N}/\tilde{L}$ are homotopy equivalent as pointed spaces. This homotopy equivalence is canonical, and transitive up to homotopy. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
	For a proof of the homotopy equivalence we refer to \cite{banyaga2004lectures}, Theorem 7.15. The constructed homotopy equivalence is given as follows: Choose $T\geq 0$ such that the following implications hold for $t\geq T$:
\begin{align*}
\phi_{[-t,t]}(x) \subseteq N\setminus L \quad &\Rightarrow \quad x \in \tilde{N}\setminus \tilde{L}\,, \\
\phi_{[-t,t]}(x) \subseteq  \tilde{N}\setminus \tilde{L} \quad &\Rightarrow \quad x \in N\setminus L\,, 
\end{align*} where $\phi_{[a,b]}(x) \coloneq \{\phi_t(x)\mid t\in [a,b]\}$.
Then the map:
\begin{align*}
h:N/L \times [T,\infty) & \to \tilde{N}/\tilde{L} \\
([x],t) & \mapsto 
\begin{cases}
[\phi_{3t}(x)] & \text{ if } \phi_{[0,2t]}(x)\subseteq N\setminus L \text{ and } \phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\subseteq \tilde{N}\setminus \tilde{L}\\
[\tilde{L}] & \text{ otherwise,}
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
is continuous and for any fixed $t$ this gives our homotopy equivalence. Obviously, such a map is canonical and transitive up to homotopy.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Connected Simple System]\label{def:morseFiltr-connectedSimpleSystem}
	A \textbf{connected simple system} consists of a set $I_0$ of pointed spaces along with a set $I_m$ of homotopy classes of maps between these spaces such that 
		\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
			\item $\hom(X,Y)\coloneq \{[f]\in [X,Y]\mid[f]\in I_m\}$ consists of exactly one element for each pair of spaces $(X,Y)\in I_0^2$, where $[X,Y]$ denotes the set of homotopy classes of maps between $X$ and $Y$.
			\item If $X,Y,Z\in I_0,[f]\in \hom(X,Y),$ and $[g]\in \hom(Y,Z)$, then $[g\circ f]\in \hom(X,Z)$.
			\item $\hom(X,X)=\{[\id_X]\}$ for all $X\in I_0$. 
		\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:morseFiltr-connectedSimpleSystemSalamon}
	This definition comes from Salamon in \cite{Salamon1985}.
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}\label{rem:morseFiltr-indexPairConnectedSimpleSystem} Let $S$ be an isolated compact invariant subset of $M$ with respect to the Morse flow $\phi$. Then the collections
\begin{align*}
	I_0&\coloneq \{N  / L\mid (N,L)\text{ is an index pair of }S\}\\
	I_m&\coloneq \{[f] \mid f \text{ is a homotopy equivalence from Theorem \ref{thm:morseFiltr-homotopyEquivalenceOfIndexPairs}}\}\,,
\end{align*} make up a connected simple system. We denote this by $\mathcal{IP}_S$. If $S=\Crit_k(f)$ for some $k\in \N$, we denote $\mathcal{IP}_{\Crit_k(f)}=\mathcal{IP}_k$.
\end{remark}


\begin{definition}[Morphisms of Connected Simple Systems]\label{def:morseFiltr-morphismsOfConnectedSimpleSystems}
A \textbf{morphism} $\Phi:I\to J$ between two connected simple systems $I=(I_0,I_m)$ and $J=(J_0,J_m)$ is a set of homotopy classes of maps between spaces in $I_0$ and $J_0$ such that
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
	\item For every $X\in I_0$ and $Y\in J_0$ the set $\Phi(X,Y)\coloneq \{[\phi]\in [X,Y]\mid[\phi]\in \Phi\}$ consists of exactly one element. 
	\item If $X,\bar{X}\in I_0$ and $Y,\bar{Y}\in J_0$ and if $[\phi]\in \Phi(X,Y)$, $[f]\in \hom(\bar{X},X)$, $[g]\in \hom(Y,\bar{Y})$, then $[g\circ \phi\circ f]\in \Phi(\bar{X},\bar{Y})$
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:morseFiltr-morphismInducedBySingleMap}
	By the second property of a morphism between connected simple systems $I$ and $J$, we can induce such a morphism by a single map $\phi:X\to Y$ where $X\in I_0$ and $Y\in J_0$. 
\end{remark}


\begin{definition}[Canonical Isomorphism Class]\label{def:morseFiltr-canonicalIsomorphismClass}
	A \textbf{canonical isomorphism class} of Abelian groups (or other algebraic structures) is a set of groups $R_0$ together with a set of group homomorphisms $R_m$ such that
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item For $R,L \in R_0$ the set $\hom(R,L)\coloneq \{f:R\to L: f \in R_m\}$ consists of exactly one element. 
		\item If $R,L,S\in R_0$ and $f\in \hom(R,L)$ and $g\in \hom(L,S)$, then $g\circ f\in \hom(L,S)\,$.
		\item $\hom(R,R)=\{\id_R\}$
	\end{enumerate}
	A \textbf{morphism} between two canonical isomorphism classes of Abelian groups $\Psi: (R_0,R_m)\to (S_0,S_m)$ is a collection of homomorphisms such that 
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item For any $R\in R_0$ and $S\in S_0$ the set $\Psi(R,S)\coloneq \{f:R\to S: f\in \Psi\}$ contains exactly one element.
		\item If $R,\bar{R}\in R_0$ and $S,\bar{S}\in S_0$, $f\in \hom(\bar{R},R)$, $g\in \hom(S,\bar{S})$ and $\psi\in \Psi(R,S)$, then $g\circ \psi\circ f\in \Psi(\bar{R},\bar{S})\,$.
	\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	Again the second property lets us induce a morphism between two canonical isommorphism classes of Abelian Groups $\Psi: (R_0,R_m)\to (S_0,S_m)$ by a single isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\psi: R\to S
	\end{equation*} for $R\in R_0$ and $S\in S_0$. We denote the induced morphism by $\psi_{\#}\coloneq \Psi$. 
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
	Any pointed topological space $X$ defines a simple connected system $(\{X\},{[\id_x]})$. Similar, any Abelian group $G$ defines a caninical isomorphism class of Abelian groups $(\{G\},\{\id_R\})$.
	Let $\phi :S \to L$ be a second homomorphism that induces a morphism of canonical isomorphism classes. It is then obvious that 
	\begin{equation*}
		(\phi\circ \psi)_{\#}=\phi_{\#}\circ \psi_{\#}\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}\label{def:morseFiltr-systemCategories}
We denote the category of connected simple systems with their morphisms by $\CSS$ and similar we call the category of canonical isomorphism classes of Abelian groups by $\CIAB$, respectively, the category for rings by $\CIRing$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
	Let $a\in A$ be an element of an abelien group $A$, which is part of a canonical isomorphism class $A\in A_{\#}$. Then we a collection of elements 
	\begin{equation*}
		a_{\#}\coloneq \{f(a)\mid f\in \hom(A,A')\text{ for all }A'\in A_{\#}
	\end{equation*} an \textbf{induced element of $a$ in $A_{\#}$}. Notice that for all $A'\in A_{\#}$ there is exactly one $a'\in a_{\#}$ that is contained in $A'$. 
\end{definition}

\begin{definition} \label{def:morseFiltr-postcomposedFunctor}
	Let $G$ be an Abelian group and $X$ be any set. We can \textbf{tag} $G$ with $X$ by defining the set $G\times \{X\}$ and endowing it with the algebraic information from $G$. (for example we define $(g,X)\cdot (g',X)=(g\cdot_Gg',X)$). Let $f:G\to L$ be a homomorphism. Then $f$ induces a homomorphism between the tagged $G\times \{X\}$ and $L\times \{Y\}$ by sending $(g,X)\mapsto (f(g),Y)$.
	Let $F:\TopP\to \Ab$ be a (contravariant) functor from pointed topological spaces to Abelian groups that factors over the homotopy category, i.e. is homotopy invariant. We first define the tagged functor $F^{tag}$ to map spaces to groups that are tagged with the spaces they come from:
	\begin{align*}
		F^{tag}: \TopP &\to \Ab\\
		X&\mapsto F(X)\times \{X\}\\
		\big(f:X\to Y\big)&\mapsto \big(F^{tag}(f):F(Y)\times \{Y\}\to F(X)\times\{X\}\big)
	\end{align*}
	For covariant functors the procedure is analogous.
	Now we can define the \textbf{postcomposed functor} $F_{\#}$ from the category of connected simple systems to the canonical isomorphism classes as follows:
	\begin{align*}
	F_{\#} : \CSS		&\to   \CIAB \\
	I_0 & \mapsto \{F^{tag}(X)\mid X \in I_0 \} \\
	I_m & \mapsto \{ F^{tag}(g) \mid [g]\in I_m \}\\
	\end{align*}
	Let $\Phi:(I_0,I_m)\to (J_0,J_m)$ be a morphism in $\CSS$. Then we define
	\begin{equation*}
		F_{\#}(\Phi): F_{\#}(J_0,J_m) \to  F_{\#}(I_0,I_m)\,,\quad 
		F_{\#}(\Phi) \coloneq \{F^{tag}(g)\mid [g]\in \Phi \}\,.
	\end{equation*}
This obviously becomes a new functor, where the tagging removed uniqueness problems for non-injective (in the literal sense not up to isomorphism) functors $F$. If $F(X)=F(Y)$ for different $X,Y\in I_0$ with $g:X\to Y\in I_m$, then without tagging there could be two different morphisms $F(X)\to F(X)$: the identity and $F(g)$. Since our functors are almost always injective, we will ignore the tagging from now on. 
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	Obviously, the procedure of postcomposing a functor works for any homotopy invariant functor from pointed topological spaces into any algebraic category and not just Abelian groups.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}[Direct Sum in the Category of Canonical Isomorphism Classes]
	Let $A_{\#}$ and $B_{\#}$ be canonical isomorphism classes of Abelien groups. Then we define the \textbf{direct sum} $A_{\#} \oplus B_{\#}$ as follows: 
	The groups are given as all direct sums:
	\begin{equation*}
		\{A\oplus B \mid A\in A_{\#}\,, B\in B_{\#}\}
	\end{equation*}and for two groups $(A_1\oplus B_1)$ and $ (A_2\oplus B_2)$ together with the morphisms $f_{A,A'}\in \hom(A,A')$ and $g_{B,B'}\in \hom(B,B')$ we define the morphism
	\begin{equation*}
		f=f_{A,A'}\oplus f_{B,B'}: (A_1\oplus B_1) \to (A_2\oplus B_2)
	\end{equation*} that makes up the homomorphism set $\hom((A_1\oplus B_1),(A_2\oplus B_2))$.
	The direct sum comes together with the inclusions $(i_A)_{\#}$ and $(i_B)_{\#}$ that are defined as follows: 
	let $1_B\in B \in B_{\#}$. Then we define the morphism $(i_A)_{\#}=(a\mapsto (a,1_B))_{\#}$ to be the morphism induced by the natural inclsion of $A$ into $A\oplus B$. This is independent of the choosen $B\in B_{\#}$. Let $1_{B'}$ be the neutral element of another $B'\in B_{\#}$. Then the induced morphisms of canonical isomorphism classes agree, as all individual morphisms agree. This follows from the internal homomorphisms (for example $g_{B,B'}$) mapping $1_B\mapsto 1_{B'}$. 
	This constuction together with the inclusions makes up the coproduct in $\CIAB$, as it satisfies the universal property: Let $C_{\#}$ be another canonical isomorphism class of Abelien groups, together with two morphisms $f_{\#}:A_{\#}\to C_{\#}$ and $g_{\#}: B_{\#}\to C_{\#}$. Then there exists a unique morphism $f_{\#}\oplus g_{\#}$ such that the diagram comutes:
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			A_{\#} \arrow[dr,"f_{\#}",bend left=30] \arrow[d,"(i_A)_{\#}"'] &\\
			A_{\#} \oplus B_{\#} \arrow[r,"f_{\#}\oplus g_{\#}"]& C_{\#}\\
			B_{\#} \arrow[u,"(i_B)_{\#}"] \arrow[ur,"g_{\#}"',bend right=30]&
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center} This morphism is induced by the direct sum of morphisms on all elements of the canonical isomorphism classes, meaning that $\pcomp{f}\oplus \pcomp{g}=\pcomp{(f\oplus g)}$. 
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
	Let $A_{\#}=(A_1)_{\#}\oplus (A_2)_{\#}$ be the direct sum of two canonical isomorphism classes. Then $A_{\#}$ comes with two canonical projection morphisms
	\begin{equation*}
		(pr_i)_{\#}: A_{\#} \to (A_i)_{\#} \,,\quad (i=1,2)
	\end{equation*} that are induced by the projection $A_1\oplus A_2\to A_i$ for $i=1,2$ for any $A_1\oplus A_2\in A_{\#}$. Then, for any canonical isomorphism class $C_{\#}$ together with maps $\pcomp{(f_i)}: C_{\#} \to \pcomp{A_i}$ there is a unique morphism $\pcomp{(f_1)}\times \pcomp{f_2}:\pcomp{A}\to \pcomp{C}$ such that the diagram commutes
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			 \pcomp{(A_1)}&\\
			\pcomp{A}\arrow[u,"\pcomp{(pr_1)}"] \arrow[d,"\pcomp{(pr_2)}"']&\pcomp{C}\arrow[ul,"\pcomp{(f_1)}"',bend right=30] \arrow[ld,"\pcomp{(f_2)}",bend left=30] \arrow[l,"\pcomp{(f_1)}\times \pcomp{f_2}"']\\
			\pcomp{(A_2)}&
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	Again, the map $\pcomp{(f_1)}\times \pcomp{f_2}$ is induced by the product of the maps that induce the $\pcomp{(f_i)}$ and hence $\pcomp{(f_1)}\times \pcomp{f_2}=\pcomp{(f_1\times f_2)}$. 
\end{remark}
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:morseFilt-summandsAndFactorsOfMorphisms}
	Let $\pcomp{A}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^n\pcomp{(A_i)}$ be the direct sum of finitly many canonical isomorphism classes and let $\pcomp{C}$ be another canonical isomorphism class. If we are given a morphism $\pcomp{f}: \pcomp{A}\to \pcomp{C}$, we call the morphisms 
	\begin{equation*}
		\pcomp{(f_i)}: \pcomp{(A_i)}\to \pcomp{C}\,,\quad i=1,\dots n
	\end{equation*} given by $\pcomp{f}\circ \pcomp{(i_i)}$ the \textbf{summand functions of $f$}. By the uniqueness of the universal property of the coproduct we know that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\pcomp{f}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^n \pcomp{(f_i)}=\pcomp{\big(\bigoplus_{i=1}^n f_i\big)}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Similar, if we are given a function $\pcomp{g}:\pcomp{C}\to \pcomp{A}$ we call the functions
	\begin{equation*}
		\pcomp{(g_i)}\coloneq \pcomp{(pr_i)} \circ \pcomp{g} :\pcomp{C}\to \pcomp{(A_i)}
	\end{equation*} the \textbf{factor functions of $g$}. Again by uniqueness we have 
	\begin{equation*}
		\pcomp{g}=\bigtimes_{i=1}^n \pcomp{(g^i)}=\pcomp{\big(\bigtimes_{i=1}^n g^i\big)}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Now assume that we are given a function $f:\bigoplus_{i=1}^n\pcomp{(A_i)}\to \bigoplus_{j=1}^m \pcomp{(B_j)}$. Then we can look at all the factor functions of all summand functions to get
	\begin{equation*}
		\pcomp{(\pcomp{(f_i)})^j}=\pcomp{((f_i)^j)}:\pcomp{(A_i)}\to (\pcomp{B_j})\,.
	\end{equation*}Similary, we could have looked at all the summands of all factor functions to get $\pcomp{((f^j)_i)}$. By definition we then have that 
	\begin{equation*}
		f=\bigoplus_{i=1}^n\big(
			\bigtimes_{j=1}^m (f_i)^j
		)=\bigtimes_{j=1}^m\big(\bigoplus_{i=1}^n (f^j)_i\big)
	\end{equation*}
	Note that by associativity of functions we have the equality that allows us to simplify the notation
	\begin{equation*}
		(f_i)^j=\pcomp{pr_j}\circ \pcomp{f}\circ \pcomp{i_i}=(f^j)_i\eqcolon f_i^j\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Critical points as isolated compact invariant subsets] \label{rem:morseFiltr-criticalPointsAsIsolatedCompactInvariantSubsets}
	Let ${f:M\to \R}$ be a Morse function on a smooth Riemannian manifold $(M,g)$. Let $q\in \Crit_k(f)$. Around $q$ we have refined Morse charts $\phi:U \to \R^m\cong \R^{m-\lambda_q}\oplus \R^{\lambda_p}$ where  the splitting of $\R^m$ is taken such that $\diff \phi_q$ is expanding in $\R^{m-\lambda_q}$ and contracting in $\R^{\lambda_p}$. With this we can define the balls:
	\begin{align*}
		D^{s}_\epsilon &~\coloneq~\{v\in \R^{m-\lambda_q} \mid \norm{v}\leq \epsilon \}, \\
		D^{u}_\epsilon &~\coloneq~\{v\in \R^{\lambda_q\phantom{-m}} \mid \norm{v}\leq \epsilon \}. 
	\end{align*}
For epsilon small enough, they give rise to the index pair $N_q\coloneq\phi^{-1}( D^{s }_\epsilon\times  D^{u }_\epsilon)$ and $L_q\coloneq\phi^{-1}( D^{ s}_\epsilon\times \partial D^{u}_\epsilon)$. To see this, notice that in such a refined Morse chart $\phi$, the differential of the flow in the critical point splits the tangent space into $T^u_qM\times T^s_qM$. Finally, since the property of $\diff \phi_x$ being a contraction on $T^s_qM$ is an open condition and thereby there is an $\epsilon>0$ such that $\diff \phi_x$ is a contraction on $T^s_qM$ for all $x$ with $\norm{\phi^{-1}(x)}<\epsilon$. Thereby, the norm of $\phi_t(x)$ projected to $T^s_qM$ is strictly decreasing in $t$. This proves that $L_q$ is an exit set of $N_q$. Similarly, one sees that $L_q$ is positively invariant in $N_q$. 

This index pair is in fact regular, since $(N_q,L_q)\simeq  (D^{ s}_\epsilon\times  D^{u }_\epsilon, D^{s}_\epsilon\times \partial D^{u}_\epsilon) \simeq (D^{u}_{\epsilon},\partial D^{u}_{\epsilon} )$ and the latters regularity is easy to see by showing that it is an NDR pair. We will cal such an index pair a \textbf{standart index pair of $q$}.

Furthermore, the index pair $(N_q,L_q)$ is homotopically equivalent to $\overline{T^u_qM}$, which is clear, as $(D^{u}_{\epsilon},\partial D^{u}_{\epsilon} ) \simeq \overline{T^u_qM}$ by mapping $\R^{\lambda_q}\to T^u_qM$ via $\restr{\diff \phi^{-1}}{0}\circ R_0$, where $R_0:\R^m\to T_0\R^m$ is the linear identification from Derfinition \ref{def:vb-linearIdentification}. Finally, we just need to strech the ball to infinity. 


We know the K-group of such a pair for any $l\in \N$ to be:
	\begin{align} \label{eq: homology of index pair of critical point}
		K^{-i}(N_q,L_q)\cong \rK^{-i}(\overline{T^u_qM})\cong \begin{cases}
			\Z & \text{if } i-k\in 2\Z,\\
			0& \text{else. }
		\end{cases}
	\end{align}
	Notice that for $k=0$ we need to consider $\partial D^k=\emptyset$ to get an index pair. That's why we let $\partial$ denote the manifold boundary instead of the topological boundary. However, now we can identify for all $k$: \begin{equation} \label{ kritical points as direct sum of homology groups}
		C^{k}(M,\mathfrak{A},\tilde{K}^{-l}(pt))=\bigoplus_{ \Crit_k(f)} \Z \cong \bigoplus_{q\in \Crit_k(f)} K^{-k}(N_q,L_q).
	\end{equation}
	Or using the language of connected simple systems, we have the identification 
	\begin{equation} 
		C^{k}(M,\mathfrak{A},\tilde{K}^{-l}(pt))\cong \bigoplus_{q\in \Crit_k(f)} K_{\#}^{-k}(\mathcal{IP}_q)\,,
	\end{equation}where the isomorphism can be read as an isomorphism of canonical isomorphism classes with the left-hand side viewed as an object of $\CIAB$ consists of only one group. 
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:morseFiltr-indexPairForDifferentChart}
	For any chart $\chi$ that is centered at a critical point $q$ and $\epsilon>0$ we define the topological pair 
	\begin{equation*}
		(N_\chi,L_\chi)\coloneq (\chi^{-1}(D^s_{\epsilon}\times D^u_{\epsilon}), \chi^{-1}(D^s_{\epsilon}\times \partial D^u_{\epsilon}))\,.
	\end{equation*}The argument that shows that $(N_\psi,L_\psi)$ is a reglar index pair of $q$ only needed that $\restr{\diff \psi}{q}$ splitts. Hence if $\restr{\diff \chi}{q}=\restr{\diff \psi}{q}$, we can conclude, that there is an $\epsilon>0$ such that $(N_\chi,L_\chi)$ is also a regular index pair.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
	Let $a_{\#}$ be a canonical isomorphism class if Abelien groups. Let $a\in a_{\#}$, and let $S\subseteq a$ be a generating set of $a$. Then we call the set
	\begin{equation*}
		S_{\#}\coloneq \{ G \mid G=f(S)\,,\text{where }f\in \hom(a,a')\text{ for all }a'\in a_{\#}\}
	\end{equation*} a generating set of $a_{\#}$. Notice, that for all $a'\in a_{\#}$ there is a $G\in S_{\#}$ such that $G$ is a generating set of $a'$. 
\end{definition}
\begin{remark} \label{rem:MorseFiltr-ProductStruktureOfKIPk}
	In Remark \ref{rem:morseFiltr-criticalPointsAsIsolatedCompactInvariantSubsets} we established an isomorphism for any $l\in \N$
	\begin{equation*}
		C^k(M,\mathfrak{A},\rK^{-l}(pt))\to \bigoplus_{q\in \Crit_k(f)}K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_q)\,.
	\end{equation*} Now notice, that the disjoint union of all index pairs $\sqcup_{q\in \Crit_k(f)} (N_q,L_q)$ is an index pair for $\Crit_k(f)$. 
	Hence the group isomorphism given by the additivity of K-theory by Theorem \ref{thm:kTheo-pointedAdditivity} gives us a natural isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		\bigoplus_{q\in \Crit_k(f)} \rK^{-k}(N_q/L_q) \to \rK^{-k}(\bigvee_{q\in \Crit_k(f)} (N_q/L_q))
	\end{equation*} which in turn defines a natural isomorphism of isomorphism classes of groups
	\begin{equation*}
		\bigoplus_{q\in \Crit_k(f)}K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_q) \to K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)
	\end{equation*} Hence, the class $K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)$ satisfies the propertie of a coproduct with the maps
	\begin{align*}
		\pcomp{i_q}\coloneq \pcomp{(c_q^*)}:\rK^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_{q})&\to K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k) \quad\text{ where }\\
		c: \bigvee_{r\in \Crit_k}(N_r/L_r)& \to N_q/L_q\quad \text{ is the collapse,}
	\end{align*}and the properties of the product with the mapsto
	\begin{align*}
		\pcomp{pr_q}\coloneq \pcomp{(i_q)^*}:K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k) &\to \rK^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_{q})\quad\text{ where }\\
		i_q: N_q/L_q & \to \bigvee_{r\in \Crit_k}(N_r/L_r)\text{ is the inclusion.}
	\end{align*}
\end{remark}




\begin{remark}[Generators for Canonical Isomorphism Classes]\label{rem:MorseFiltr-canonicalIdentificationOfMorseChainGrou}
	Hence, there is an isomorphism 
	\begin{equation*}
		C^k(M,\mathfrak{A},\rK^{-l}(pt))\to K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)\,.
	\end{equation*} This isomorphism can be made natural. For this we need to establish a natural generating set of $K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)$, which is done by defining a generating set for any (and thereby for all) groups in $K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)$. We pick the group $\rK^{-k}(\bigvee_{q\in \Crit_k(f)} (N_q/L_q))$. Additivity tells us that a generating set is given by including generators of the summands $\rK^{-k}(N_q/L_q)$. Now for each summand we have the isomorphism $\tilde{K}^{-\lambda_q}(N_q/L_q)\cong \tilde{K}^{-\lambda_q}(\overline{T^u_qM})$, which is natural and described in Remark \ref{rem:morseFiltr-criticalPointsAsIsolatedCompactInvariantSubsets}. Hence, if $T^u_qM$ is oriented, Corollary \ref{cor:orient-vectorspaceOrientationInducesKtheoreticOrientation} gives us just what we need, making the map canonical. The generator is then given by pulling back the generator of $K^{-\ind_p}(\overline{T^u_qM})$ along the map of pairs:
	\begin{align*}
	 \kappa_q\coloneq  \restr{\diff \phi^{-1}}{0}\circ R_0\circ s\circ \mathrm{pr}_u \circ \phi	\,:\,(\phi^{-1}( D^{s }_\epsilon\times  D^{u }_\epsilon),\phi^{-1}( D^{ s}_\epsilon\times \partial D^{u}_\epsilon))  \to (\overline{T^u_qM},\{\infty\})
	\end{align*} where $\mathrm{pr}_u$ is the projection onto the unstable coordinates and $s$ is the stretching $x\mapsto  (\frac{x}{\epsilon-\norm{x}})$ with $\restr{\diff s}{0}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Idm$. So for every critical point $q\in \Crit_k(f)$ we get a generator of $\alpha_q\in \tilde{K}^{-\lambda_q}(N_q/L_q)$ which pulls back along the collapsoiing map $c_q^*(\alpha_q)\in \rK^{-k}(\bigvee_{r\in \Crit_k}(N_q/L_r))$and then induces an element in $K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)$. 	
	We denote said element as $(\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#}$ and call it the \textbf{canonical generator corresponding} to $q$. 
	
	Notice the abuse of notation here, where $(\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#}$ can either be an element of $K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)$, or a generator of $K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_{\{q\}})$. Those two meanings are related in the following sense: 
	\begin{align*}
		\pcomp{(c_q^*)}((\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#})=(\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#}\in K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_k)\\
		\pcomp{(i_q^*)}((\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#})=(\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#}\in K^{-k}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_{\{q\}})
	\end{align*}
\end{remark}
	We could have picked another refined Morse chart, or another $\epsilon$ and hence another index pair $(N_q',L_q')$ of $q$. This would give another generator $\alpha_q'^{(N_q',L_q')}\in \tilde{K}^{-\lambda_q}(N_q'/L_q')$. However, as canonical generators, they would agree: For this we need to check the following: let $h:N_q/L_q\to N_q',L_q'$ be the homotopy equivalence from Theorem \ref{thm:morseFiltr-homotopyEquivalenceOfIndexPairs}. In their summands, $\alpha_q'^{(N_q',L_q')}$ and $\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)}$ are generators, and as $h^*$ induces an isomorphism we have $h^*(\alpha_q'{(N_q',L_q')})=\pm \alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)}$. So we need to show, that the sign here is $+$. Whilst we could brute force a calculation here using the single preimage Lemma \ref{lem:orient-singleRegularPreimage} we will later in Remark \ref{rem:morseFiltr-canonicalGeneratorsIndependOfChartAndEpsilon} proof said statement a bit more elegant.
\begin{remark}
The independence of the chosen Morse chart was the main motivation for introducing the language of canonical isomorphism classes. 
The book \cite{banyaga2004lectures} proofes the homotopy equivalence of all index pairs and proceeds much as we do to show that singular homology and Morse homology are isomorphic, albeit in singular homology rather than complex K-theory. This is a written out version of Salamons proof of said statement in \cite{Salamon1990}. 
Similar to us, they also introduce a generator for an index pair by orientation and a Morse chart, but do not show the invariance of said chart (compare \cite[Example 7.17]{banyaga2004lectures}). Whilst this is no gap in the theory (the invariance is to be expected and the authors presumably considered it evident), without the language of canonical isomorphism classes, such an invariance is cumbersome even to state precisely.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[From Morse to K-Theory]
	Let $M$ be a smooth manifold wit the Morse datum $\mathfrak{A}$ and denote the one point pointed space by $pt$. Then for any $l\in \N$, on the generators $\Crit_k(f)$ we define the morphism of canonical Isomorphism calses, where the left side consists of only one group:
	\begin{equation*}
		\Chi: C^k(M,\mathfrak{A},\rK^{-l}(pt))\,\to\, \rK_{\#}^{-(k+l)}(\mathcal{IP}_k)\,,\quad q\mapsto (\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Remark \ref{rem:MorseFiltr-canonicalIdentificationOfMorseChainGrou} ensures invaraiance of the choosen $\epsilon$ and refined Morse chart, that made up $(\alpha_q^{(N_q,L_q)})_{\#}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Adapted Lyapunov Function]\label{def:morseFilt-adaptedLyapunov}
	An \textbf{adapted Lyapunov function} for the Morse-Smale pair $(f,g)$ (where $g$ denotes the metric and $f$ the Morse function) is a smooth function 
	$\ell :M\to \R$ such that
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item $\diff \ell (-\grad (f))<0$ on $M\setminus \Crit(f)$,
		\item $\ell(r)=\ind_r(f)$ for all $r\in \Crit(f)$,
		\item every $r\in \Crit(f)$ has a neighbourhood on wich $\ell-f$ is constant.
	\end{enumerate} 
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:morseFiltr-existenceAdaptedLyapunov}
	An adapted Lyapunov function exists.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
	We apply \cite[Theorem B]{Smale1961OnGradient} to the vector field $X=\grad(f)$, which satisfies the needed conditions: For such a setup, Smales Theorem gives us a function $F$ that satisfies
	\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
		\item The critical points of $F$ are the critical points of $X$ (and thereby, in our case of $f$) and $F$ coincides with $f+c$ for $c\in \R$ in small neigbhourhoods of those critical points,
		\item $X$ is transversal to $F$ at levelsets outside of critical points.
		\item $F(r)=\ind_f(r)$ for all $r\in \Crit(F)=\Crit(f)$.
	\end{enumerate} By (a), $\diff F(\grad(f))=\diff f (\grad(f))=\norm{\grad(f)}^2$ near critical points. Outside of critical points we have that $\diff F (-\grad(f))\neq 0$ and as for $\dim(M)\geq2$ this set is connected, we conclude that $\diff F (-\grad(f))>0$ there also. If $\dim(M)=1$ we can argue similarly in each connected component that contains a point close to a critical point. Hence, all properties of an adapted Lyapunov function are satisfied by $\ell\coloneq F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Sublevel Index Triple]\label{def:morseFilt-sublevelTriple}
	Let 
\begin{equation*}
S_k\coloneq \Crit_k(f)\cup\Crit_{k-1}(f)\cup\bigcup_{q\in\Crit_k,\,p\in\Crit_{k-1}}W(q\to p
)\,.
\end{equation*}
	An  \textbf{index triple} for $S_k$ is a triple $Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X$ of compact sets such that $(X,Z)$ is an index pair of $S_{k}$, $(X,Y)$ is an index pair of $\Crit_{k}(f)$ and $(
	Y,Z)$ is an index pair of $\Crit_{k-1}(f)$. It is called a \textbf{sublevel triple} if moreover
	\begin{equation*}
		Y=Z\cup \big(X\cap  \ell^{-1}(-\infty,c]\big)\qquad \text{for some }c\in(k-1,k)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}\label{morseFiltr-sublevelTripoleGivePairs}
	Let $(X,Z)$ be an index pair of $S_{k}$, $c\in (k-1,k)$ and $Y=Z\cup \big(X\cap  \ell^{-1}(-\infty,c]\big)$. Then $(X,Y,Z)$ is a sublevel triple.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	We need to check the three axioms for the two pairs $(X,Y)$ and $(Y,Z)$. We start checking the axioms for $(X,Y)$:
	\begin{enumerate}[1.]
		\item Assume that $x\in I(\overline{X\setminus Y})$. Then $x\in I(\overline{X\setminus Z})$ and thereby $x=q$, or $x\in W(q\to p)$ for some $q\in \Crit_{k}(f)$, and $p\in \Crit_{k-1}(f)$. But the latter cannot be true, as for all $x\in W(q\to p)$, there is a $t>0$ such that $f(\phi_t(x))<c$. Hence $x=q$.
		\item Assume that $Y$ would not be positively invariant in $X$. Then there would be an $x\in \ell^{-1}(-\infty,c]$ (since $Z$ is positively invarient), and such that $f(\phi_{t}(x))>c\geq f(x)$ which cannot happen, as $f$ decreases along flow lines.
		\item Finally, as $Y$ contains $Z$, the exit set property is trivially satisfied.
	\end{enumerate}
	Now for $(Y,Z)$:
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item This is shown by the same argument as for $(X,Y)$.
		\item $Z$ is positively invariant in $X$ and $Y\subseteq X$. Hence $Z$ is positively invariant in $Y$.
		\item $Z$ is an exit set of $X$ and thereby also of $Y$, as the latter is again contained in $X$.
	\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:morseFiltr-kompactImpliesTwichImpliesU}
	Let $\phi$ be a flow on $M$ (which is a locally compact metric space or in our case a smooth manifold), $K\subseteq M$ be compact and $U$ be an open set in $K$ containing the maximal invariant subset $I(K)$. Then there exists a $T>0$ with:

	\begin{equation*}
		\phi_{[-T,T]}(x)\subseteq K \qquad \Leftrightarrow\qquad x\in U
	\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	The contradiction would imply that for all $T$ there would be an $x$ such that $\phi_{[-T,T]}(x)\subseteq K$ but $x\notin U$. With this we define the sequence $x_n$ such that $\phi_{[-n,n]}(x_n)\in K$ but $x_n\notin U$. Now decending to a subsequence gives us a sequence $x_n$ that converges in $K\setminus U$ to a point $x$. Continuity of the flow implies that $\phi_\R(x)\subseteq K$ and hence $x\in I(K)$ which implies that $U$ was not a neighbourhood of $I(K)$. Hence, by contraposition, the original claim follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Flow Maps and Triples]\label{lem:morseFiltr-flowMapOfTriples}
	Let $(X,Y,Z)$ be an index triple and $(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})$ be a sublevel triple for $S_k$, with level $\tilde{c}$. Dentote by $h_t,\,h^{k-1}_t,\, h^k_t$ the maps of Theorem \ref{thm:morseFiltr-homotopyEquivalenceOfIndexPairs} for the index pairs $(X,Z)\to (\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})$ of $S_{k}$,  $(Y,Z)\to (\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})$ of $\{p\}$ and  $(X,Y)\to (\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})$ of $\{q\}$. Then there exists a $T_0$ such that for all $t\geq T_0$:
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item $h_t(Y/Z)\subseteq \tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z}$,
		\item $\restr{h_t}{Y/Z}=h^{k-1}_t$,
		\item the map $X/Y \to \tilde{X}/\tilde{Y}$ induced by $h_t$ equals $h^k_t$.
	\end{enumerate}
	This means that we can view $h_t:(X/Z,Y/Z,[Z])\to (\tilde{X}/\tilde{Z},\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z},[\tilde{Z})$ as a map of triples, such that the two diagramms (where $q$ denotes thge quotient) commute: 
	\begin{center}
		\begin{tikzcd}
			X/Y \arrow[d,"h^k_t"]&(X/Z,Y/Z)\arrow[d,"h_t"]\arrow[l,"q"]\\
			\tilde{X}\tilde{Y}   & (\tilde{X}/\tilde{Z},\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})\arrow[l,"q"]
		\end{tikzcd}
		\qquad
				\begin{tikzcd}
			Y/Z \arrow[d,"h^{k-1}_t"]&(Y/Z,[Z])\arrow[d,"h_t"]\arrow[l,"q"]\\
			\tilde{Y}\tilde{Z}   & (\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z},\tilde{Z})\arrow[l,"q"]
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{center}
	
	in the first pair, it induces $h_t^k$ and in the second pair in induces $h_t^{k-1}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	As a reminder: For two index pairs $(N,L)$ and $(\tilde{N},\tilde{L})$, the map $h:N/L\to \tilde{N}/\tilde{L}$ is given by 
	\begin{equation*}
		([x],t) \mapsto \begin{cases}
[\phi_{3t}(x)] & \text{ if }\underbrace{ \phi_{[0,2t]}(x)\subseteq N\setminus L \text{ and } \phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\subseteq \tilde{N}\setminus \tilde{L}}_{P^{(N,l)}_{(\tilde{N},\tilde{L})}(t,x)}\\
[\tilde{L}] &\text{ else}
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation*} where $t$ is big enough. The property that gives the case destinction is denoted by $P^{(N,l)}_{(\tilde{N},\tilde{L})}(t,x)$.
	Let $x\in Y\setminus Z$ with $\phi_{[0,2t]}(x)\subseteq X\setminus Z$. The positive invariance of $Y$ in $X$ implies that $\phi_{[0,2t]}(x)\in Y\setminus Z$. By Lemma \ref{lem:morseFiltr-kompactImpliesTwichImpliesU} applied to $K\coloneq \overline{Y\setminus Z}$ with $U\coloneq \{l<\tilde{c}\}\cap K$ and $t$ big enough implies that $\phi_t(x)\in U$. Hence, $\ell(\phi_t(x))<\tilde{c}$ and as $\ell$ decreases along $\phi$, we conclude that $\ell(\phi_{[t,3t]})<\tilde{c}$. Thereby for our $x$ the conditions 
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\in \tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}\qquad\text{and}\qquad \phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\in \tilde{Y}\setminus \tilde{Z}
	\end{equation*} are equivalent, for $t$ big enough, giving us that for $x\in Y\setminus Z$, the conditions are equal: $P^{(X,Z)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})}(t,x)=P^{(X,Z)}_{(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})}(t,x)$. This implies $(i)$ and also $(ii)$, as the maps are given by the same case distinctions.
	Now for the last statement: The induced map $\overline{h_t}:X/Y\to \tilde{X}/\tilde{Y}$ is well defined by $(i)$. Again, to check the equality of the induced map $\overline{h_t}$ and $h_t^q$ we go through the case destinctions in their definitions. This time, the conditions $P$ are not equivalent. And we go through the 4 possible cases: $P^{(X,Z)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})}(t,x)$ and $P^{(X,Y)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})}(t,x)$ are both true, are both false, or only one of them is true for $x\in X$. The first two conditions obviously give rise to the same functions. Now assume that $P^{(X,Z)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})}(t,x)$ is true, whilst $P^{(X,Y)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})}(t,x)$ is false. Then: 
	\begin{equation*}
		\text{either }\underbrace{\phi_{[0,2t]}(x)\begin{cases}
			\subseteq X\setminus Z\\
			\nsubseteq X\setminus Y
		\end{cases}}_{Case 1}\qquad \text{,or }
		\underbrace{\phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\begin{cases}
			\subseteq \tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}\\
			\nsubseteq \tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Y}
		\end{cases}}_{Case 2}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Case 1: There exists a $s\leq 2t$ such that $\phi_s(x)\in Y$. Positive invariance of $Y$ in $X$ gives us that $\phi_{2t}(x)\in Y$. Now $\phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\in \tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}$ gives us by Lemma \ref{lem:morseFiltr-kompactImpliesTwichImpliesU} that $\phi_t(x)$ is close to $S_{k}$, and since $q\notin Y$, we have that the number $c_0\coloneq \max\{\restr{\ell}{Y\cap S_{k}}\}<k$ Hence, by the closenes to $S_{k}$ there exists a $\delta>0$ such that $\ell(\phi_{2t}(x))<c_0+\delta<k$ for $t$ big enough. 
	On $\overline{\tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}}\cap \ell^{-1}(\tilde{c},c_0)$ there critical points and hence $\ell$ is strictly decreasing. Using compactnes and the mean value theorem, we can conclude that there is a $\mu$ such that for every $x\in \overline{\tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}}\cap \ell^{-1}(\tilde{c},c_0+\delta)$ and $s>0$, $\ell(x)-\ell(\phi_{s}(x))\geq \mu\cdot s$. Now we apply this idea to the intervall $[2t,3t]$ and assume that there is no $T$ big enough such that for all $t>T$, $\ell(\phi_{3t}(x))>\tilde{c}$. Then the whole curfe $\phi_{[t,3t]}(x)$ would live in $\overline{\tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}}\cap \ell^{-1}(\tilde{c},c_0+\delta)$ and hence, 
	\begin{equation*}
		c_0+\delta -\tilde{c}\geq \ell(\phi_{2t}(x))-\ell(\phi_{3t}(x))\geq \mu\cdot t\,.
	\end{equation*} This cannot be true for all $t$ as the right side is unbounded in $t$ whilst the left side is constant, giving us a contradiction. Hence, for $t$ big enough $\ell(\phi_{3t}(x))<\tilde{c}$ and hence $\ell(\phi_{3t}(x))\in \tilde{Y}$. Thereby, the induced map maps such a $(x,t)$ to $[\tilde{Y}]$, similar to $h^q_t$.

	Case 2: In this case, there exists an $s<3t$ such that $\phi_s(x)\in \tilde{Y}$ and as $\tilde{Y}$ is defined as a sublevel set and $\ell$ decreases along the folow, $\phi_{3t}(x)\in \tilde{Y}$ and hence, the induced map from $h_t$ and $h^q_t$ map $(x,t)$ to $[\tilde{Y}]$.

	Now the last Option we need to check is, if $P^{(X,Z)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Z})}(t,x)$ is false, whilst $P^{(X,Y)}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})}(t,x)$ is true. This would mean that: 
		\begin{equation*}
		\text{either }\underbrace{\phi_{[0,2t]}(x)\begin{cases}
			\nsubseteq X\setminus Z\\
			\subseteq X\setminus Y
		\end{cases}}_{\text{ not possible}}\qquad \text{,or }
		\underbrace{\phi_{[t,3t]}(x)\begin{cases}
			\nsubseteq \tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Z}\\
			\subseteq \tilde{X}\setminus \tilde{Y}
		\end{cases}}_{\text{ not possible}}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	As this cannot happen, we are done. 
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}[A Map of K-goups]\label{def:morseFiltr-aMapOfKGroups}
	For an index triple $(X,Y,Z)$ for $S_k$ we have $(Y,Z)\in \mathcal{IP_{k-1}}$ and $(X,Y)\in \mathcal{IP}_k$, and we define 
	\begin{equation*}
		\Delta^{k-1}\coloneq \big(b\circ \delta_{triple}\big)_{\#}:
		\pcomp{K^{-(k-1)}}(\mathcal{IP}_{k-1})\to  \pcomp{K^{-k}}(\mathcal{IP}_k)\,,
	\end{equation*} where $\delta_{triple}:K^{-k+1}(Y,Z)\to K^{-k+2}(X,Y)$ is the triple connecting homomorphism and $b=\bottmap[]$. This map will turn out to be invariant unter the choosen index triple (compare Remark \todo{ref}). Index triples exist by Lemma \ref{morseFiltr-sublevelTripoleGivePairs}, together with the existence of index pairs of $S_k$ by Remark \ref{rem:morseFiltr-existenceOfIndexPairs}.

	For $p\in \Crit_{k-1}(f)$ and $q\in \Crit_k(f)$ we call
	\begin{equation*}
		\pcomp{(\Delta^{k-1})^{q}_p}\coloneq \pcomp{(pr_q)}\circ \Delta^{k-1}\circ \pcomp{(i_p)}:\pcomp{K^{-(k+1)}}(\mathcal{IP}_{p})\to \pcomp{K^{-k}}(\mathcal{IP}_q)
	\end{equation*} the \textbf{components} of $\Delta^{k-1}$ (summand and factor functions with respect to Corollary \ref{cor:morseFilt-summandsAndFactorsOfMorphisms}).
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}[Invariance of the Chosen Triple]\label{rem:morseFiltr-invarianceOfTriple}
	The map $\Delta^{k-1}$ does not depend on the choosen index triple.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}
		Let $(X,Y,Z)$ be an index triple and $(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})$ be a sublevel triple for $S_k$. We now want to show, that the diagram
\begin{center}\begin{adjustbox}{width=\textwidth}
% https://tikzcd.yichuanshen.de/#N4Igdg9gJgpgziAXAbVABwnAlgFyxMJZABgBpiBdUkANwEMAbAVxiRAGkA9YAWgZ4CMAXwAUAHTF4GsYAE0hpCVJkAtIQEoQC9Jlz5CKMgGYqtRizZde-YSNmkVm7SAzY8BIgNIDT9Zq0QObj5BUSUsaRg5IQB6cMjgNVIAKidSHTd9T1IAJl9zAKDrULsYlRS0jL0PFBzvfP9LYIYwyQiZAA1Y+NUFHqj5OLaEtUqXXXcDZDq86j8LQKsQ2w6y0lky9THXaqmjermCputW5SiuobPoiq108cya5H3Zs0bF5pLV+1TtiayUAAs5AaCyKfFO7XOfWGMnkvweUyBJkObzBNlE9kcWlMMCgAHN4ERQAAzABOEAAtkgyCAcBAkHUQAwsGBClAIEwAEYMVjUAAWMDoUCQYCYDAY1BwdAibEgrNuJPJVMQjLpSH2r1BIj5AH0cNwANahdScZIgagMOicmAMAAKfxqIFJWDxfJwCpAZMp1Ml9MQwjuXuVqr9QM1hQksAYUp1wBwzrQPKEHqD6t9SAArCjQZGbTG4wmk+amVabfaEWxna73c5U4gw2rEAA2C0stkc7m8kACoUisUS2nShiygisWtKzPpxAAdlb8sC7K5POLPeFgTlXalMvXo5TE+bU9n4bY2r1ho0pr33sQAA4p2HmfOQIvOyvBWvReLJUOR-Lx9eaUbO9j0CXNozoWN4ywRMYCEbhxBhKFFEQ65+kSDRk3-ZUvFpP0AE5swjMQo3zKCYLg4ARA6dYHHUZMLVLO0HQMJ0XTdK9lQI3DJyZNs2BfZd+XfPsv0HbdwF3LCkBwxsNUfdsly7VcRIHLdhx3P9A33YDGzwoQKCEIA
\begin{tikzcd}
{K^{-l-1}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})} \arrow[rd, Rightarrow] \arrow[ddd] \arrow[rrrr, "{\delta_{triple}^{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})}}"] &                                                                                            &                                                                         &                                                          & {K^{-l}(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})} \arrow[lld, Rightarrow] \arrow[ddd] \\
                                                                                                                                       & {K^{-l-1}(\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z},*)} \arrow[d, "(h_t^{k-1})^*"'] \arrow[r, "\delta_{triple}"] & {K^{-l}(\tilde{X}/\tilde{Z},\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z})} \arrow[r, Rightarrow] & {K^{-l}(\tilde{X}/\tilde{Y},*)} \arrow[d, "(h_t^{k})^*"] &                                                                   \\
                                                                                                                                       & {K^{-l-1}(Y/Z,*)} \arrow[r, "\delta_{triple}"']                                            & {K^{-l}(X/Z,Y/Z)} \arrow[r, no head, Rightarrow]                     & {K^{-l}(X/Y,*)}                                       &                                                                   \\
{K^{-l-1}(Y,Z)} \arrow[rrrr, "{\delta_{triple}^{(X,Y,Z)}}"'] \arrow[ru, Rightarrow]                                                    &                                                                                            &                                                                         &                                                          & {K^{-l}(Y,Z)} \arrow[llu, Rightarrow]                          
\end{tikzcd}			\end{adjustbox}
			\end{center} where
		\begin{equation*}
			h_t^{k}:(X/Y,*)\to (\tilde{X}/\tilde{Y},*)\qquad \text{and}\qquad h_t^{k-1}:(Y/Z,*)\to (\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z},*)
		\end{equation*}
		are the maps from Theorem \ref{thm:morseFiltr-homotopyEquivalenceOfIndexPairs}, viewed as maps of pairs. The outside vertical arrows are defind by the inside veritcal arrows. Thereby, the squares on the left and right commute by definition. The top and bottom sqare commute, as they are introduced by the quotient map 
			$q_Z:(X,Y,Z)\to (X/Z,Y/Z,*)$, which is a map of triples. The double arrows are isomorphisms. The whole square commutes, if the inner rectangle commutes. However, we know by lemma \ref{lem:morseFiltr-flowMapOfTriples}, that both $h$ maps are induced by the same $h_t$ and hence we get the diagramm:
			\begin{center}
				% https://tikzcd.yichuanshen.de/#N4Igdg9gJgpgziAXAbVABwnAlgFyxMJZABgBpiBdUkANwEMAbAVxiRAGkA9YAWgZ4CMAXwAUAHTF4GsYAE0hAeglSZALSGkAVAEoQG9Jlz5CKMgKq1GLNl179hI2QtVbd+kBmx4CRAeQv0zKyIHNx8ospY0jDAABqKkdHA6qSJMvJKklFqQm6kBl7GvqTm1IHWIbZ8gqKxzqROqtp5BUY+KABM-mVWwaF2EVlJ8ZkqMfKuevkeht4myF2llkE2Yfa1CrKubhYwUADm8ESgAGYAThAAtkhkIDgQSH7LFSAiABYA+jjcANY12pxNCBqAw6AAjGAMAAKsyKITOWH2bxwwJAELAUCQPAAbMR3OcrjdqPckF1nn0JLAGDg6B9gDgEWgGDAhFNThdrogniTEABmHorEKUyE0ukMrBMlmo0EQ6Gw9ogBFIlH4jmk4kPRAAFhBWDAfSgECYYOZqLeMDomMQYCYDAYxLoUTYkH1bJABM5-LumoArLrXSFDcbTdRzZbnQRWA6nSEXaxVYTtRqkH7yWx3l9frlAW6PUTvY9qOirbzbuU+p9vkCE5yyTyveW2JWc0IKEIgA
\begin{tikzcd}
{K^{-l-1}(\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z},*)} \arrow[d, "(h_t^{k-1})^*"', bend right=60] \arrow[r, "\delta_{triple}"] \arrow[d, "h_t^*", bend left] & {K^{-l}(\tilde{X}/\tilde{Z},\tilde{Y}/\tilde{Z})} \arrow[r, Rightarrow] \arrow[d, "h_t^*"] & {K^{-l}(\tilde{X}/\tilde{Y},*)} \arrow[d, "(h_t^{k})^*"] \\
{K^{-l-1}(Y/Z,*)} \arrow[r, "\delta_{triple}"']                                                                                         & {K^{-l}(X/Z,Y/Z)} \arrow[r, no head, Rightarrow]                                        & {K^{-l}(X/Y,*)}                                      
\end{tikzcd}
			\end{center}where the outside commutes by Lemma \ref{lem:morseFiltr-flowMapOfTriples} and the inside square commutes, as $h_t$ is a map of triples. Let $x_\#\in K^{-l-1}_\#(\mathcal{IP}_{k-1})$ with representatives
	$\tilde x\in K^{-l-1}(\tilde Y,\tilde Z)$ and $x\in K^{-l-1}(Y,Z)$, i.e.
	$x=(h^{k-1}_t)^*\tilde x$. If we denote the map $\Delta^{k-1}$ coming from any index tiple $(A,B,C)$ by $\Delta_{(A,B,C)}$ we can calculate using the established commutativity, the naturality of the bott isomorphism $b$ and identifying $h_t^*$ and $(h_t^k)^*$:
	\begin{align*}
		\Delta^{k-1}_{(X,Y,Z)}(\pcomp{x})
			&=\pcomp{\Big(b\circ \delta_{triple}(x)\Big)}\\
			&=\pcomp{\Big(b\circ \delta_{triple}\circ (h^{k-1}_t)^*(\tilde x)\Big)} \\
			&=\pcomp{\Big(b\circ (h_t)^*\circ \delta_{triple}(\tilde x)\Big)} \\
			&=\pcomp{\Big((h_t^k)^* \circ  b\circ \delta_{triple}(\tilde x)\Big)} \\
			&=\Delta^{k-1}_{(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y},\tilde{Z})}(\pcomp{x})\,.
	\end{align*}
For two arbitrary index triples $(X_i,Y_i,Z_i)$, $i=1,2$ we can now choose a sublevel triple (existence is assured by Lemma \ref{morseFiltr-sublevelTripoleGivePairs}) and apply the argument to both triples to deduce: $\Delta^{k-1}_{(X_1,Y_1,Z_1)}=\Delta^{k-1}_{(X_2,Y_2,Z_2)}$.
\end{proof}
	


\subsection{Commuting Boundary Maps}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:morseFilt-commutingMorsecoboundary}
	Let $M$ be a smooth manifold with the Morse datum $\mathfrak{A}$, denote the one pointed topological space by $pt$. Then, for any $l\in \Z$ the following diagram comutes:
	\begin{equation}
		\begin{tikzcd}\label{eq:morseFilt-commutingDiagramTheorem}
			C^{k-1}(M,\mathfrak{A},\rK^{-l}(pt)) \arrow[r,"\partial^{k-1}"] \arrow[d,"\Chi"'] 
			& 
			C^{k}(M,\mathfrak{A},\rK^{-l}(pt)) \arrow[d,"\Chi"]
			\\
			K^{-(k-1)-l}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_{k-1}) \arrow[r,"\Delta^{k-1+l}"]& K^{-(k)-l}_{\#}(\mathcal{IP}_{k})
		\end{tikzcd}
	\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
The proof of this statment will be take the most part of the rest of this section. We start by defining spaces that we well need throughout the rest of the section:
\begin{figure}
	\begin{center}
	\includegraphics[width=0.49\textwidth]{text/images/sideViewDefinitionGlobalTriple.jpg}
	\includegraphics[width=0.49\textwidth]{text/images/TopViewDefinitionGlobalTriple.png}
	\caption{A side view on the left and top view on the right of the constructions and definitions of $N^k$ for $k=2$ from Definition \ref{def:morseFilt-globalSetup}.}\label{fig:morseFiltr-SideAndTopViewDefinitionGlobalSetup}
	\end{center}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[The Global Setup]\label{def:morseFilt-globalSetup}
	Let $(M,g)$ be a smooth Riemannian manifold with the Morse Smale function $f$ and orientations $\mathfrak{o}_r$ of all unstable tangend spaces $T^u_r$ for $r\in \Crit$. Let furthermore $\ell$ be an adapted Lyapunov function. Now we fix $k$, $c\in (k-1,k)$, $\epsilon \in (0,1)$ and $T>0$. With this define the sub- and superlevel sets 
	\begin{equation*}
		M_c\coloneq \ell^{-1}[c,\infty)=\{\ell \geq c\}\qquad \text{ and }\qquad M^c\coloneq \ell^{-1}(-\infty,c]=\{\ell \leq c\}\,
	\end{equation*} 
	\begin{align*}
		N^k
		&\coloneq \{x\in M_c \mid \ell(\phi_{-T}x)\le k+\epsilon\}, &
		L^k
		&\coloneq N^k\cap\ell^{-1}(c),\\
		N^{k-1} &\coloneq \{x\in M^c \mid \ell(\phi_{T}x)\ge k-1-\epsilon\}, &
		L^{k-1} &\coloneq \{x\in N^{k-1}\mid \ell(\phi_T x)=k-1-\epsilon\},
	\end{align*}and the \textbf{global triple}
	\begin{equation*}
		C_k\coloneq N^k\cup N^{k-1},\qquad
		B_k\coloneq N^{k-1}\cup L^k,\qquad
		A_k\coloneq L^{k-1}\cup\big(L^k\setminus N^{k-1}\big).
	\end{equation*}
	For the following proof we also define 
	\begin{equation*}
		S(q\to) \coloneq W(q\to)\cap \ell^{-1}(c),\qquad
		D_q \coloneq W(q\to)\cap M_c\,.
	\end{equation*}
	\todo{Hier die benötigten konventionen/oprienbtierungen einfügen}
\end{definition}


\begin{lemma}[Cantor's Intersection Theorem]\label{lem:morseFiltr-cantorsIntersectionTheorem}
	Let $C_k$ be a family of compact spaces non-empty such that $C_k\supseteq C_{k+1}$ for all $k\in I$, where $I$ is an ordered set containing smallest element $1\in I$. Then the intersection 
	$\bigcap_{k\in I} C_k \neq \emptyset$ is non-empty.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Assume the opposite, that $\bigcup_{k\in I} C_k=\emptyset$.
	 As we defined compact spaces to be hausdorff, we can conclude that all $C_k$ are closed and thereby $U_k\coloneq C_1\setminus C_k$ is open in $C_1$. Now by our assumption, the $U_k$ cover $C_1$ (for every $x\in C_1$ there exists a $k\in I$ such that $x\notin C_k$ and hence $x\in U_k$). As $C_1$ is compact, there is a finite ascending subfamiliy that already covers $C_1=U_{k_1}\cup \cdots \cup U_{k_m}$, where ascending means that $U_{k_i}\subseteq U_{k_{i+1}}$. Hence, $C_1=U_{k_m}=C_1\setminus C_{k_m}$ (notice, that we used existence of maximum elements in finite ordered sets), and as $C_{k_m} \subseteq C_1$ this implies that $C_{k_m}=\emptyset$ which contradicts our assumtion. Hence, by contraposition, we arrived at the statement. 
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}[Splitting into Pieces]\label{lem:morseFilt-piecesOfGlobalSetup}
	Choose pairwise disjoint open subsets $U_q\supseteq D_q$ ($q\in \Crit_k(f)$)and $U_p\subseteq W(\to p)\cap M^c$ ($p\in \Crit_{k-1}(f)$). For $T$ large enough 
	$N^k\subseteq \bigsqcup_q U_q$ and $N^{k-1}\subseteq \bigsqcup_p U_p$. In this setting
	\begin{equation*}
		N_q\coloneq N^k 	\cap U_q, \quad L_q \coloneq L^k    \cap U_q,\quad 
		N_p\coloneq N^{k-1} \cap U_p, \quad L_p \coloneq L^{k-1}\cap U_p
	\end{equation*} are compact, $N^k=\bigsqcup_qN_q$, $L^k=\bigsqcup_q L_q$, $N^{k-1}=\bigsqcup_p N_p$ and $L^{k-1}=\bigsqcup_p L_p$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	Notice, that the limit $\bigcap_{T\in \R_{>0}} N^k=\bigsqcup_q D_q$: If $x\in D_q$ then $\phi_{-T}\in D_q$ for all $T$ and hence $x\in \bigcap_{T\in \R_{>0}} N^k$. Is on the other hand $x\notin D_q$ for any $q\in \Crit_k$ hence $x\notin W(q\to)\cap M^c$, then $x\in W(r\to)$ for some $r$ with $\ind(r)\neq k$, and as $\ell(r)\geq \ell(x)>{k-1}$, we know that $\ind(r)>k$. Thereby, $\phi_{-T}(x)$ approaches $r$ implying that $\ell(\phi_{-T}(x))$ approaches $\ind(r)\geq{k+1}$ and hence, for $T$ big enough $\ell(\phi_{-T}(x))\geq k+\epsilon$, concluding that indeed $\bigcap_{T\in \R_{>0}} N^k=\bigsqcup_q D_q$.


	Now the $D_q$ themself are compact, as by the $\lambda$-Lemma, the boundary of $D_q$ is given by unstably manifolds of critical points of lower degree. Hence, their $\ell$-value is below $c$ and we could rewrite our intersection $W(q\to)\cap M^c$ to become $\overline{W(q\to)\cap M^c}$ which in turn is the intersection of two compact spaces and thereby compact again.


	Finally, For every $T$, the $N^k_T$ are compact (as they are given by a closed condition on a compact manifold). Hence, if we assume $T$ to be increasing to infinity, we have a decending family of compact spaces that approaces $\bigsqcup_q D_q$. Assume that $N^k_T$ would never be contained in our neighbourhoof $\bigcup_q U_q$. Then for all $T$, $N^k_T\setminus U$ would be non-empt, compact, and again a decending family, meaning that the intersection $\bigcap_T (N^k_T\setminus U)\neq \emptyset$ by Cantor's intersection Theorem \ref{lem:morseFiltr-cantorsIntersectionTheorem}. This however contradicts that the $N^k_T$ approach $\bigsqcup_q D_q$.

	The same argument (where instead of the $-T$-limit we take toe $T$-limit) shows that $N^k$ is contained in an open neighbourhood $\bigsqcup_p U_p$ for $T$ big enough. The rest of the lemma follows imidiatly from the fact that the intersections seperate disjoint pieces of a compact space, which are compact themself.
\end{proof}
From now on, the notations from Definition \ref{def:morseFilt-globalSetup} and Lemma \ref{lem:morseFilt-piecesOfGlobalSetup} is used.

\begin{lemma}[The Global Triple]\label{lem:morseFilt-globalTriple}
	For $\epsilon$ small and $T$ large: 
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item $A_k$ is closed, and $(C_k,A_k)$ is an index pair of $S_k$.
		\item $B_k=A_k\cup \big(C_k \cap \ell^{-1}(-\infty,c]\big)$, hence $(C_k,B_k,A_k)$ is a sublevel triple for $S_k$.
		\item the inclusions induce homeomorphisms:
			\begin{equation*}
				\bigvee_q N_q/L_q = N^k/L^k \xrightarrow{\ \cong \ } C_k/B_k\,,\qquad
				\bigvee_p N_p/L_p = N^{k-1}/L^{k-1} \xrightarrow{\ \cong \ }B_k/A/k\,. 
			\end{equation*}
		\item $(N_q,L_q)$ is an index pair of $\{ q\}$ and $(N_p,L_p)$ of $\{p\}$.
		\item For every neighbourhoof $U$ of $W(\to p)\cap \ell^{-1}(c)$ that is open in $\ell^{-1}(c)$ there is a $T$ such that $N_p\cap \ell^{-1}(c)\subseteq U$.
	\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
	$A_k$ is colsed, as $L^{k-1}$ is closed and the closure 
	\begin{equation*}
		\overline{L^k\setminus N^{k-1}}=L^k\setminus \inti( \underbrace{N^{k-1} \cap L^k}_{N^{k-1}\cap \ell^{-1}(c)})=L^k\setminus N^{k-1}\cap \partial N^{k-1}\cap L^k
	\end{equation*} is only gaining the points $\partial N^{k-1}\cap L^k=\{\ell \circ \phi_T=k-1-\epsilon\}\subseteq L$ which are already in $L^{k-1}$. 
	
	(i) We need to check the three properties:
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item  It is clear that for all $T$ and $\epsilon$ $S_k\subseteq \inti(A_k \setminus C_k)$, as $N^k \cup N^{k-1}$ give an open neighbourhood of the closed $S_k$, and $A_k\subseteq \partial N^k\cup N^{k-1}$. The latter is true, as any element  $x\in A_k$ is either in $L^{k-1}\subseteq \partial N^{k-1}\setminus \overline{N^k}$, or in $L^{k-1}\setminus N^{k-1}$. But $L^{k-1}\setminus N^{k-1}\subseteq \partial N^k \setminus  \overline{N^{k-1}}$. Since $S_k$ is invariant, we have that for all $T,\epsilon$ $S_k\subseteq I(\overline{C_k\setminus A_k})$. Now for any $T>0$, and any $\epsilon \in (0,1)$ $x\in C_k\in \ell^{-1}(k-1-\epsilon,k+\epsilon)$. The flow invariant subset $I(\overline{C_k\setminus A_k})$ is contained in the flow invariant subset $I(\overline{\ell^{-1}(k-1-\epsilon,k+\epsilon)})\subseteq I(\ell^{-1}(k-2,k-1))$.
	For any $x\in \ell^{-1}(k-2,k-1)$, the limit $\lim_{s\to \pm \infty} \phi_s(x)$ either stays in the cylinder $\ell^{-1}(k-2,k-1)$ and thereby $x\in W(q\to p)\subseteq S_k$ for some $q\in \Crit_k$, $p\in \Crit_{k-1}$, or it leaves the cylinder. Hence, $I(\overline{C_k\setminus A_k}) \subseteq S_k$ and with the containmend above we can conclude, that $S_k=I(\overline{C_k\setminus A_k})$.
		\item  The positive invariance of $A_k$ in $C_k$ is a direct consequence of $\ell$ decending along the flow $\phi_s$.
		\item The exit set property is also a direct consequence of $\ell$ decending along the flow $\phi_s$.
	\end{enumerate}

	(ii) This is just a reformulation:
	\begin{equation*}
		A_k\cup \underbrace{(C_k\cap \ell^{-1}[-\infty,c))}_{=C_k\cap M^c=N^{k-1}\cup L^k}=\underbrace{L^{k-1}}_{\subseteq N^{k-1}}\cup (L^k\setminus N^{k-1})\cup N^{k-1} \cup L^k =L^k\cup N^{k-1}
	\end{equation*}
	(iii) Obviously $N^k/L^k \to C_k / B_k$ is a homeomorphism, as the inclusion induces a continuous bijection between two compact and Hausdorff spaces. The same argument works for $N^{k-1}/L^{k-1}\to B^k/ A_k$ once we realice, that if $x\in B^k$ but $x\notin N^{k-1}$ then $x\in L^k\setminus N^{k-1}\subseteq A_k$. 

	(iv) By (ii) and Lemma \ref{morseFiltr-sublevelTripoleGivePairs} (together with (iii) )we know that $(C_k,B_k)$ is an index pair for $\Crit_k$ and $C_k=\sqcup_q N_q$, where the $N_q$ are closed and open in $C_k$. Hence, $(N_q,B_k\cap N_q)$ is an index pair of $\{q\}$, as all properties translate to the smapper space that only contains $\{q\}$ as an invariant subset and finally 
		\begin{equation*}
			B_k\cap N_q=(N^{k-1}\cup L^k)\cap N_q=\underbrace{(N^{k-1}\cap N_q)}_{\subseteq (N^k\cap N^{k-1})\cap N_q \subseteq L^k \cap N_q}\cup (L^k\cap N_q)=L_q
		\end{equation*}
		Hence, $(N_q,B_k\cap N_q)=(N_q,L_q)$ is an index pair of $\{q\}$ and similar we can show that $(N_p,L_q)$ is an index pair of $\{p\}$.

	(v) Similar to the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:morseFilt-piecesOfGlobalSetup}: Assume that $T$ is increasing to infinity. Notice how the intersection $\bigcap_T (N_p \cal \ell^{-1}(c))=(W(\to p)\cup \{p\})\cap \ell^{-1}(c)$. Let $U$ be a neighbourhood of $W(\to p)\cap \ell^{-1}(c)$ and assume that for all $T$, the intersection $\big(N_p\cap \ell^{-1}(c)\big)\cap U^c\neq \emptyset$ where $U^c$ denotes the complement of $U$. Then by Canotrs intersection Theorem \ref{lem:morseFiltr-cantorsIntersectionTheorem}, the intersection $\cap_T \big(N_p\cap \ell^{-1}(c)\big)\cap U^c \neq \emptyset$ which contradicts $\cap_T (N_p \cal \ell^{-1}(c))=(W(\to p)\cup \{p\})\cap \ell^{-1}(c)$ and thereby the final statement is shown.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}[Lokal Triple]\label{def:morseFiltr-localTriplaABC}
	We define the local triple 
	\begin{equation*}
		C^{qp}\coloneq N_q\cup N_p\,\qquad B^{qp}\coloneq N_p\cup L_q\,,\qquad A^{qp}\Coloneq L_p \cup (L_q\setminus N_p)\,.
	\end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}[Restricting to Summands]\label{lem:morseFiltr-restrictingToSummand}
	Let $i_q:N_q/L_q \to \bigvee_{q'\in \Crit_k}N_{q'}/L_{q'}$ be the inclusion of a summand and $c_p:\bigvee_{p'\in \Crit_{k-1}}N_{p'}/L_{p'}\to N_p/L_p$ the collaps of everything but one summand. Under the identifications of Lemma \ref{lem:morseFilt-globalTriple}(iii) and $C^{qp}/B^{qp}=N_q/L_q$, $B^{qp}/A^{qp}=N_p/L_p$,
	\begin{equation*}
		i_q^* \circ \delta_{triple}^{(C_k,B_k,A_k)}\circ c_p^*\;=\; \delta_{triple}^{(C^{qp},B^{qp},A^{qp})}\,.
	\end{equation*}
	In particular,
		$\pcomp{(\Delta^{k-1})^{q}_p} = \pcomp{\big(b\circ \delta_{triple}^{(C^{qp},B^{qp},A^{qp})} \big)}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{figure}
	\begin{center}
		\includegraphics[width=0.7\textwidth]{text/images/restrictingToASummand.png}
	\caption{The triples of Proof of Lemma \ref{lem:morseFiltr-restrictingToSummand}. The image continuous the exampe esteblished in Figure \ref{fig:morseFiltr-SideAndTopViewDefinitionGlobalSetup}}\label{fig:MorseFiltr-theTriplesInRestrictionOfSummondsProof}
	\end{center}
\end{figure}
\begin{proof}
	This does not follow immidiatly, as the inclusion $(\qp{C},\qp{B},\qp{A})\hookrightarrow (C_k,B_k,A_k)$ is not a map of triples. This can be seen as there are elements in $L_q\setminus N_q$ that fo not live in $A_k$ but in $B_k$ (Compare Figure \ref{fig:MorseFiltr-theTriplesInRestrictionOfSummondsProof}, where the proplematic area is marked). So we go through intermediate triples
		\begin{align*}
			T_q &\coloneq \big(N_q \cup N^{k-1},\; L_q \cup N^{k-1},\; L^{k-1}\cup (L_q \setminus N^{k-1})\big)\,,\\
			W   &\coloneq \big(N_q\cup N^{k-1},\; L_q\cup N^{k-1},\;
						       L_p\cup \bigcup_{p'\in \Crit_{k-1}\setminus\{p\}}N_{p'}\cup (L_q\setminus N^{k-1})\big)\,,
		\end{align*}
	and get the maps of triples (all induced by the inclusions)
	\begin{equation*}
		(C_k,B_k,A_k)\xleftarrow{\ \iota_1\ }T_q \xrightarrow{\ \iota_2 \ } W \xleftarrow{ \ \iota_3 \ }(\qp{C},\qp{B},\qp{A})
	\end{equation*}
	In the quotient of the first two summands of the triples, the maps induce 
	$i_q$, $\id_{N_q/ L_q}$ and $ \id_{N_q/ L_q}$. In the quotients of the second and third summands of the triples the three maps induce the maps
	$\id_{N^{k-1}/L^{k-1}}$, $c_p$ and $\id_{N_p/L_p}$. 
	Here we always identified the quotiends of the second by the third summands of the triples with $N^{k-1}/L^{k-1}$ 
	respectifely with $N_p/L_p$ via the inclusion as in Lemma \ref{lem:morseFilt-globalTriple} (iii). Now naturality of the triple connecting homomorphism gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta_{triple}^{T_q}=i_q^* \circ \delta^{(C_k,B_k,A_k)}_{triple},\qquad
		\delta_{triple}^{T_q} \circ c_p^*=\delta_{triple}^{W}\,,\qquad
		\delta_{triple}^{(C^{qp},B^{qp},A^{qp})}=\delta_{triple}^W\,. \qedhere
	\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Definitions and Setup]\label{def:morseFilt-setupCommutingDiff}
	We work in a compact, Riemanian, smooth manifold $(M,g)$ of dimension $m$ together with a Morse-Smale function 
	\begin{align}
		f: M\to \R
	\end{align} and orientations $\mathfrak{o}$ of all unstable tangent spaces. We denote the flow corresponding to the negative gradient by $\phi_t(x)$.
	We assume that $q$ is a critical point of index $k$ and $p$ is a critical point of index $k-1$. We define $f(q)=b$ and $f(p)=a$ and assume that there is no critical point in $f^{-1}(a,b)\subseteq M$. We define the sublevle and superlevel sets 
	\begin{align}
		M^t \coloneq \{ x\in M | f(x)\leq t \} \quad \text{and}\quad M_t:=\{ x\in M | f(x)\geq t \} \, ,
	\end{align} and the constants 
	\begin{align}
		c \in (a,b) \quad , \quad \epsilon >0 \text{ small } \quad,\quad T>0 \text{ big .}
	\end{align} With this we define the sets: 
	\begin{align}
		N_q  & \coloneq    \{ x\in M_c |f(\phi_{-T}(x))  \leq b+ \epsilon  \}   \, ,  \\
		L_q  & \coloneq    \{ x\in N_q |f(x)             =    c            \}    \, , \\
		N_p  & \coloneq    \{ x\in M^c |f(\phi_T(x))     \geq a-\epsilon   \}    \, , \\
		L_p  & \coloneq    \{ x\in N_p |f( \phi_T(x))    =    a-\epsilon   \} \, ,
	\end{align} and finally: 
	\begin{align}
		C  & \coloneq  N_p \cup N_q  \, ,                     	\\
		B  & \coloneq  N_p \cup L_q   \, ,                   	\\
		A 	 & \coloneq  L_p \cup \inti{(L_q\setminus N_p)} 			\\
		S(q\to)& \coloneq W(q\to)\cap f^{-1}(c)\				\\
		D_q & \coloneq W(q\to)\cap M_c\,.							
	\end{align}
	
	
	Furthermore, we assume $W(q\to)$ to be oriented with respect to the orientation of $T^u_qM$ as in Proposition \ref{prop:morse-OrientedUnstableSpaces}. Let $Q^p\coloneq \restr{TM}{W(\to p)}\Big/ TW(\to p)$ be oriented with respect to $T^u_pM$ as in Proposition \ref{prop:morse-orientTheQuotient}.
	For an oriented real vector space $V$ of dimension $n$, let $\beta_V\in K^{-n}(\overline{V})$ denote its K-theoretic orientation from Corollary \ref{cor:orient-vectorspaceOrientationInducesKtheoreticOrientation}.  Notice that $(\alpha_p)_{\#}$ is induced by any of its elements.
